and let
p\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{Path}(X\times Y,B)}_{Y^{\prime\prime}}
.
Let
\varphi^{\prime}=\langle{\varphi,\psi}\rangle\in\mathbf{Ar}(Y^{\prime\prime},X\times Y)
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{B}_{Y^{\prime\prime}}
be such that
p=\varphi^{\prime}\mathrel{\triangleright}m
, we have that

\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime})\in{Y^{\prime\prime}\times Y^{\prime}}\cdot p(s^{\prime\prime},\mathsf{fl}(\eta(s^{\prime})))

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime})\in{Y^{\prime\prime}\times Y^{\prime}}\cdot m(s^{\prime\prime},\mathsf{fl}(\eta(s^{\prime}))(\varphi(s^{\prime\prime}),\psi(s^{\prime\prime})))

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime})\in{Y^{\prime\prime}\times Y^{\prime}}\cdot m(s^{\prime\prime},\eta(s^{\prime})(\varphi(s^{\prime\prime}))(\psi(s^{\prime\prime})))

is measurable because

\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime\prime}=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime})\in{Y^{\prime\prime}\times Y^{\prime}}\cdot(\varphi\mathrel{\triangleright}(\psi\mathrel{\triangleright}m))(s^{\prime\prime},\eta(s^{\prime}))

and by our assumption about
\eta
.
Last notice that
\left\|{\mathsf{fl}(\eta)}\right\|=\left\|{\eta}\right\|
which shows that
\mathsf{fl}\in\mathbf{MCones}(\mathsf{Path}(X,\mathsf{Path}(Y,B)),\mathsf{Path}(X\times Y,B))
.

As to the converse direction, given
\eta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X\times Y,B)}
let
\mathsf{fl}^{\prime}(\eta)=\boldsymbol{\lambda}r\in X\cdot\boldsymbol{\lambda}s\in Y\cdot\eta(r,s)
, we must first prove that
\mathsf{fl}^{\prime}(\eta)\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,\mathsf{Path}(Y,B))}
, we just check
measurability, boundedness being obvious. Let
p\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{Path}(Y,B)}_{Y^{\prime}}
for some
Y^{\prime}\in\mathbf{Ar}
.
Let
\varphi\in\mathbf{Ar}(Y^{\prime},Y)
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{B}_{Y^{\prime}}
be such that

p=\varphi\mathrel{\triangleright}m
, we must prove that
\psi=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s^{\prime},r)\in{Y^{\prime}\times X}\cdot p(s^{\prime},\mathsf{fl}^{\prime}(\eta)(r))=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s^{\prime},r)\in{Y^{\prime}\times X}\cdot m(s^{\prime},\eta(r,\varphi(s^{\prime})))
is
measurable.
This follows from the fact that
\varphi
and
\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s^{\prime},r,s)\in{Y^{\prime}\times X\times Y}\cdot m(s^{\prime},\eta(r,s))
are
measurable, the latter by our assumption about
\eta
.

Checking that
\mathsf{fl}^{\prime}
is a morphism in
\mathbf{MCones}
follows
exactly the same pattern as for
\mathsf{fl}
, using the obvious
bijection between
\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{Path}(X,\mathsf{Path}(Y,B))}_{Y^{\prime}}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{Path}(X\times Y,B)}_{Y^{\prime}}
induced by the fact that
\mathbf{Ar}
is cartesian.
Finally the observation that
\mathsf{fl}^{\prime}=\mathsf{fl}^{-1}
shows that
\mathsf{fl}
is an iso in

\mathbf{MCones}
.

Remark 3.24
.

So a test on the space of
C
-valued and
X
-parameterized paths
is provided by a test
m\in\mathcal{M}^{C}_{Y}
— itself parameterized by
a space
Y\in\mathbf{Ar}
— and a “variable argument” which is a
measurable function
\varphi
from
Y
to the space
X
.
When
\varphi
is not a constant function, the value of

(\varphi\mathrel{\triangleright}m)(s,\gamma)=m(s,\gamma(\varphi(s)))
depends in
general on
s
when
\gamma
is not a constant path,
even
if the function
m:Y\times\underline{C}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
does not depend on
its first argument
.
This definition of tests in the cones of paths plays a crucial role
in the proof of Lemma
3.22
.

Imagine that we want to use a simpler notion of tests, with

\mathcal{M}^{C}\subseteq{\underline{C}}^{\prime}
, that is, assume that our tests
do not have the further parameter taken in a
Y\in\mathbf{Ar}
.
The first difficulty we face consists in finding a suitable
definition for
\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{Path}(X,C)}
.
The simplest option consists in taking all the
r\mathrel{\triangleright}m
where
r\in X
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{C}
, defined by
(r\mathrel{\triangleright}m)(\beta)=m(\beta(r))
.
This choice fulfills all the expected separation properties.
With this definition, an element of
\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,\mathsf{Path}(X,C))}

is the same thing as a bounded function

\gamma:Y\times X\to\underline{B}
such that, for each
m\in\mathcal{M}^{C}
,
the function
\boldsymbol{\lambda}s\in Y\cdot m(\beta(s,r)):Y\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is measurable for all

r\in X
and the function
\boldsymbol{\lambda}r\in X\cdot m(\beta(s,r)):X\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is measurable for all

s\in Y
.
Let us assume that
C=\mathsf{FMeas}(Z)
for some
Z\in\mathbf{Ar}
, let

\varphi:Y\times X\to Z
be a function, and let

\gamma:Y\times X\to\underline{C}
be given by

\gamma(s,r)=\boldsymbol{\delta}^{Z}(\varphi(s,r))
.
Saying that
\gamma\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,\mathsf{Path}(X,C))}
means that the
function
\varphi
is separately measurable in both arguments, a
condition which is strictly weaker than measurability on

Y\times X
in general.
On the other hand, with our definition of measurability tests for

\mathsf{Path}(X,C)
, Lemma
3.22
tells us that

\gamma\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,\mathsf{Path}(X,C))}
iff
\varphi
is measurable

Y\times X\to Z
for the very simple reason that

Y\times X\in\mathbf{Ar}
.
Notice that we have equipped
P
, the cone of measurable paths
from
X
to
C
, with two different measurability structures:
the original one made of all tests
\varphi\mathrel{\triangleright}m
where

\varphi\in\mathbf{Ar}(Y,X)
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{C}_{Y}
, and the simplified
one, made of tests
\varphi\mathrel{\triangleright}m
where
\varphi\in\mathbf{Ar}(Y,X)
is
a
constant
function and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{C}_{Y}
.
The two measurable cones obtained in that way are not isomorphic
12
12

12

More precisely, the identity function between these two
cones is not an isomorphism.
in
\mathbf{MCones}
since the associated measurable paths are distinct as
we have seen.
This complements Remarks
3.18

and
3.16
.

We will meet a completely similar definition of tests for the space

{B}\multimap{C}
of linear, continuous and integrable functions from

B
to
C
in Section
5
.

4.
Integrable cones

We now introduce the main novelties of this paper, which are the
definition of the integral of a measurable path wrt. a finite measure,
the notion of integrable cone, and the notion of linear, continuous,
measurable and integral preserving functions between integrable cones.

The following definition is quite similar to Definition 2.1
in
[
Pet38
]
of the integral of a function valued in a
topological vector space.
Our integrals are valued in cones instead of vector spaces.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
B
be a measurable cone,
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
,

\beta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,B)}
and
\mu\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}
.
An
integral of
\beta
over
\mu
is an element
x
of

\underline{B}
such that, for all
m\in\mathcal{M}^{B}_{0}
, one has

\displaystyle m(x)=\int m(\beta(r))\mu(dr)\,.

Notice indeed that
m\mathrel{\circ}\beta:X\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is a bounded
measurable function so that the integral above is well defined and
belongs to
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
(remember that the measure
\mu
is finite).
Notice also that by
Definition 3.2
.
if such an integral
x
exists, it is
unique, so we can introduce a notation for it, we write

\displaystyle x=\int\beta(r)\mu(dr)\,.

When we want to stress the cone
B
where this integral is computed
we denote it as
\int^{B}\beta(r)\mu(dr)
and when we want to insist
on the measurable space on which the integral is computed we write
\int_{X}\beta(r)\mu(dr)
or
\int_{r\in X}\beta(r)\mu(dr)
.

Lemma 4.2
.

If
\beta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,B)}
is integrable over

\mu\in\mathsf{FMeas}(X)
then

\displaystyle\left\|{\int_{X}\beta(r)\mu(dr)}\right\|_{B}\leq\left\|{\beta}\right\|_{\mathsf{Path}(X,B)}\left\|{\mu}\right\|_{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}\,.

Proof 4.3
.

Let
x=\int\beta(r)\mu(dr)
. If
x=0
there is nothing to prove
so assume that
x\not=0
. Let
\varepsilon>0
and let
m\in\mathcal{M}^{B}_{0}\setminus\{0\}
be such that
\left\|{x}\right\|\leq\varepsilon+\frac{m(x)}{\left\|{m}\right\|}
, that is

\displaystyle\left\|{x}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon+\frac{1}{\left\|{m}\right\|}\int m(\beta(r))\mu(dr)\,.

For each
r\in X
we have
m(\beta(r))\leq\left\|{m}\right\|\left\|{\beta(r)}\right\|\leq\left\|{m}\right\|\left\|{\beta}\right\|
. Our
contention follows from
\left\|{\mu}\right\|=\mu(X)=\int\mu(dr)
.

Definition 4.4
.

A measurable cone is
integrable
if, for all
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
,
each
\beta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,B)}
has an integral in
\underline{B}

over each measure
\mu\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}
.
When this is the case we use
\mathcal{I}^{B}_{X}
for the uniquely defined
function
\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,B)}\times\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}\to\underline{B}
such that
\mathcal{I}^{B}_{X}(\beta,\mu)=\int\beta(r)\mu(dr)
.

Remark 4.5
.

A very natural question is whether there are measurable cones which
are not integrable.
We strongly conjecture that such cones do exist but we have not yet
tried to exhibit some.

The fundamental example of an integrable cone is the measurable cone
of finite measures described in Section
3.2.1
.

Theorem 4.6
.

For each measurable space
X
, the measurable cone
\mathsf{FMeas}(X)
is integrable.

This is just a reformulation of the standard integration of a kernel.

Proof 4.7
.

Let
Y\in\mathbf{Ar}
,
\kappa\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,\mathsf{FMeas}(X))}
, which
means that
\kappa
is a bounded kernel
Y\leadsto X
, and let
\nu\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(Y)}
, which means that
\nu
is a finite
measure.
We define
\mu:\sigma_{X}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
by

\displaystyle\forall U\in\sigma_{X}\quad\mu(U)=\int\kappa(s,U)\nu(ds)\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\,.

The fact that
\mu
defined in that way is a finite measure is
completely standard in measure theory and
\mu
is the integral of

\kappa
by the very definition of
\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}_{0}
.

In the sequel we assume that
B
is an integrable cone. We state and
prove some basic expected properties of integration.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
\varphi:Y\times X\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be measurable and bounded and
let
\kappa:Y\to\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}
be a bounded kernel.
Then the function
\boldsymbol{\lambda}s\in Y\cdot\int\varphi(s,r)\kappa(s,dr)
is measurable.

Proof 4.9
.

The property is obvious when
\varphi
is simple
13
13

13

A
\mathbb{R}
-valued measurable function is simple iff it
ranges in a finite subset of
\mathbb{R}
.
, and the result follows from the monotone convergence theorem by the
fact that each
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
-measurable function is the lub of a
increasing sequence of simple functions.

Lemma 4.10
.

For each
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
, the map
\mathcal{I}^{B}_{X}
is bilinear,
continuous and measurable.
This means that

\mathcal{I}^{B}_{X}:{\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,B)}}\mathrel{\&}{\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}}\to\underline{B}

is continuous, separately linear in each of its two arguments and
that for each
Y\in\mathbf{Ar}
,
\eta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,\mathsf{Path}(X,B))}
and
\kappa\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,\mathsf{FMeas}(X))}
, the function
\beta=\mathcal{I}^{B}_{X}\mathrel{\circ}\langle{\eta,\kappa}\rangle:Y\to\underline{B}
is a
measurable path.

Proof 4.11
.

Separate linearity in both argumets results from the linearity of
integration and from
Definition 3.2
.
satisfied by
B
, let us prove
separate continuity (which implies continuity by
Lemma
2.23
).
Let
(\beta_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be an increasing sequence in

\mathcal{B}\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,B)}
and let
\mu\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}
.
The sequence
(\mathcal{I}^{B}_{X}(\beta_{n},\mu)\in\underline{B})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is increasing by
linearity of
\mathcal{I}^{B}_{X}
and for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
we have
\left\|{\mathcal{I}^{B}_{X}(\beta_{n},\mu)}\right\|\leq\left\|{\beta_{n}}\right\|\left\|{\mu}\right\|\leq\left\|{\mu}\right\|
so that
\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\mathcal{I}^{B}_{X}(\beta_{n},\mu)\in\underline{B}
exists.
Let
\beta=\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\beta_{n}\in\mathcal{B}\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,B)}
,
that is
\forall r\in X\ \beta(r)=\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\beta_{n}(r)
.
Let
m\in\mathcal{M}^{B}_{0}
, since
(m\mathrel{\circ}\beta_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}

is an increasing sequence of measurable functions by linearity of

m
and since
m\mathrel{\circ}\beta=\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}m\mathrel{\circ}\beta_{n}
(pointwise) by
continuity of
m
, we have

\displaystyle\int m(\beta(r))\mu(dr)=\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\int m(\beta_{n}(r))\mu(dr)

by the monotone convergence theorem.
That is
m(\mathcal{I}^{B}_{X}(\beta,\mu))=\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}m(\mathcal{I}^{B}_{X}(\beta_{n},\mu))=m(\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\mathcal{I}^{B}_{X}(\beta_{n},\mu))
by
continuity of
m
. By
Definition 3.2
.
we get
\mathcal{I}^{B}_{X}(\beta,\mu)=\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\mathcal{I}^{B}_{X}(\beta_{n},\mu)
as
required.

Let
\beta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,B)}
and let
(\mu_{n}\in\mathcal{B}\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be an increasing
sequence with lub
\mu
.
It is a standard fact that for each measurable and bounded

\varphi:X\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
the sequence
(\int\varphi(r)\mu_{n}(dr))_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is increasing and has
\int\varphi(r)\mu(dr)
as lub: this is due to the fact that
\int\varphi(r)\mu(dr)=\sup_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\int\varphi_{k}(r)\mu(dr)
where
(\varphi_{k}\leq\varphi)_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is an increasing family of
simple functions whose pointwise lub is
\varphi
, and to the fact
that
\int\psi(r)\mu(dr)=\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\int\psi(r)\mu_{n}(dr)

holds trivially when
\psi
is simple.
As above the sequence
(\mathcal{I}^{B}_{X}(\beta,\mu_{n}))_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is increasing with
\forall n\in{\mathbb{N}}\ \left\|{\mathcal{I}^{B}_{X}(\beta,\mu_{n})}\right\|\leq\left\|{\beta}\right\|\left\|{\mu}\right\|
and therefore has a lub
\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\mathcal{I}^{B}_{X}(\beta,\mu_{n})\in\underline{B}
.
Let
m\in\mathcal{M}^{B}_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle m(\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\mathcal{I}^{B}_{X}(\beta,\mu_{n}))

\displaystyle=\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}m(\mathcal{I}^{B}_{X}(\beta,\mu_{n}))

\displaystyle=\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\int m(\beta(r))\mu_{n}(dr)

\displaystyle=\int m(\beta(r))\mu(dr)

\displaystyle=m(\mathcal{I}^{B}_{X}(\beta,\mu))

and the announced continuity follows by
Definition 3.2
.
in
B
.

Now we prove measurability, so let
Y\in\mathbf{Ar}
,
\eta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,\mathsf{Path}(X,B))}
and let
\kappa\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,\mathsf{FMeas}(X))}
, we prove that the
function
\beta=\mathcal{I}^{B}_{X}\mathrel{\circ}\langle{\eta,\kappa}\rangle:Y\to\underline{B}
belongs to
\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,B)}
.
The fact that
\beta(X)
is bounded results from
Lemma
4.2
.
Let
Y^{\prime}\in\mathbf{Ar}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{B}_{Y^{\prime}}
, we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s^{\prime},s)\in{Y^{\prime}\times Y}\cdot m(s^{\prime},\beta(s))

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s^{\prime},s)\in{Y^{\prime}\times Y}\cdot m(s^{\prime},\mathcal{I}^{B}_{X}(\eta(s),\kappa(s)))

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s^{\prime},s)\in{Y^{\prime}\times Y}\cdot\int m(s^{\prime},\eta(s,r))\kappa(s,dr)

and this function is measurable by
Lemma
4.8
and by our assumption about

\eta
.

Lemma 4.12

(Change of variable)
.

Let
X,Y\in\mathbf{Ar}
,
\beta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,B)}
,

\nu\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(Y)}
and
\varphi\in\mathbf{Ar}(Y,X)
.
We have

\displaystyle\int_{s\in Y}\beta(\varphi(s))\nu(ds)=\int_{r\in X}\beta(r){\varphi}_{\ast}(\nu)(dr)\,.

In other words
\mathcal{I}^{B}_{X}
is extranatural in
X
.

Proof 4.13
.

By the usual change of variable formula, through the use of
measurability tests
m\in\mathcal{M}^{B}_{0}
and
Definition 3.2
.
for

B
.

Lemma 4.14
.

If
B
is an integrable cone and
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
is such that

\alpha>0
then the measurable cone
\alpha B
is integrable,
and has the same integrals as
B
.

We can define now the category which is at the core of the present study.

Definition 4.15
.

The category
\mathbf{ICones}
has integrable cones as objects and an
element of
\mathbf{ICones}(B,C)
is an

f\in\mathbf{MCones}({B},{C})
such that, for all
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
and all
\beta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,{B})}
and
\mu\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}
one has

\displaystyle f\Big(\int\beta(r)\mu(dr)\Big)=\int f(\beta(r))\mu(dr)\,.

This property of
f
will be called
integral preservation

and when it holds we often simply say that
f
is

integrable
.

Notice that the right hand term of the above equation is well defined
because
f\mathrel{\circ}\beta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,{C})}
by our assumption on

f
. It is obvious that we define a category in that way.

Lemma 4.16
.

The functor
\mathsf{FMeas}:\mathbf{Ar}\to\mathbf{MCones}
introduced in
Lemma
3.19
is a functor
\mathbf{Ar}\to\mathbf{ICones}
.

Proof 4.17
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathbf{Ar}(X,Y)
and
\kappa\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y^{\prime},\mathsf{FMeas}(X))}
be a bounded kernel.
Given
\mu^{\prime}\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(Y^{\prime})}
and
V\in\sigma_{Y}
we have

\displaystyle{\varphi}_{\ast}\Big(\int\kappa(s^{\prime})

\displaystyle\mu^{\prime}(ds^{\prime})\Big)(V)=\Big(\int\kappa(s^{\prime})\mu^{\prime}(ds^{\prime})\Big)(\varphi^{-1}(V))

\displaystyle=\int\kappa(s^{\prime},\varphi^{-1}(V))\mu^{\prime}(ds^{\prime})\text{\hskip 10.22217ptby def.~of integration in }\mathsf{FMeas}(X)

\displaystyle=\int{\varphi}_{\ast}(\kappa(s^{\prime}))(V)\mu^{\prime}(ds^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\Big(\int{\varphi}_{\ast}(\kappa(s^{\prime}))\mu^{\prime}(ds^{\prime})\Big)(V)\text{\hskip 10.22217ptby def.~of integration in }\mathsf{FMeas}(Y)

so that
{\varphi}_{\ast}
preserves integrals.

4.1.
Integrable cones as quasi-Borel spaces with additional structure

In this section, we assume, as in Remark
3.1
, that

\mathbf{Ar}
has only two objects
\mathbb{R}
and
0
.

Then every integrable cone
C
can be seen as a QBS

[
VKS19
]
by letting
M_{C}
(which is by definition
the set of all QBS-morphisms from
\mathbb{R}
to
C
) be the set
of all maps
\alpha:\mathbb{R}\to\underline{C}
such that for all

m\in\mathcal{M}^{C}_{\mathbb{R}}
, the map
\boldsymbol{\lambda}(r,s)\cdot m(\alpha(r),s)

from
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
to
\mathbb{R}
is measurable.

There is a well-defined notion of S-finite measure (respectively:
probability measure, sub-probability measure) on QBSs.
The operation that maps a QBS
Q
to the set of all S-finite
(respectively: probability, sub-probability) measures on
Q
defines
a commutative strong monad on the category of QBSs
[
HKS+18
, end of §2]
(this is similar to the Giry monad on the category of
measurable spaces and measurable maps).
For each S-finite measure
\mu
on an integrable cone
C
, there
exists at most one element
y\in\underline{C}
such that for all

m\in\mathcal{M}^{C}_{0}
,

m(y)=\int_{x\in\underline{C}}m(x)\,\mu(dx)
.
If this element exists (which is always the case if
\mu
is
finite and has a bounded support), we will denote it by

\int_{x\in\underline{C}}x\,\mu(dx)
.
One can check that for each integrable cone
C
, this construction makes the unit ball

\mathcal{B}C
into an algebra over the monad of sub-probability measures on QBSs.

In this situation a map
f:\underline{C}\to\underline{B}
between two
integrable cones is a morphism in
\mathbf{ICones}
if and only if:

•

it is a morphism of QBSs,

•

it preserves S-finite integrals: for each S-finite measure

\mu
on
C
, if
\int_{x\in\underline{C}}x\,\mu(dx)
exists (as
an element of
\underline{C}
), then

\int_{x\in\underline{C}}f(x)\,\mu(dx)=f\Big(\int_{x\in\underline{C}}x\,\mu(dx)\Big),

•

and it is non-expansive: for all
x\in\underline{C}
,

\left\|{f(x)}\right\|\leq\left\|{x}\right\|
.

In particular, for each morphism

f\in\mathbf{ICones}(B,C)
, the restriction of
f
to

\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
is a morphism of algebras:
this was one of our main guidelines in the design of integrable cones.

However, it is not clear whether or not each morphism of algebras from

\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
to
\mathcal{B}\underline{C}
is the restriction of
some morphism in
\mathbf{ICones}(B,C)
(which would make
\mathbf{ICones}
a full
subcategory of the category of algebras over the monad of
sub-probability measures on QBSs).
It is also not clear whether the construction of Section

9.2
(which will define a fixpoint operator on integrable
cones) can be replicated in the category of algebras over the monad of
sub-probability measures on QBSs.
Indeed, we conjecture that in both cases the answer is no. On the
other hand, integrable cones are not quite algebras over the monad of
S-finite measures, because the would-be monad multiplication (namely,
S-finite integration) is only partially defined. We do not know
whether there exists a monad on (a full subcategory of) QBSs such that

\mathbf{ICones}
is equivalent to the category of algebras over this monad.

4.2.
The integrable cone of paths and a Fubini theorem for cones

Theorem 4.18
.

For each
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
and each integrable cone
B
, the measurable
cone
\mathsf{Path}(X,{B})
is integrable.

Proof 4.19
.

Let
Y\in\mathbf{Ar}
,
\eta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,\mathsf{Path}(X,B))}
and
\nu\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(Y)}
, we define
\beta:X\to\underline{B}
by
\beta(r)=\int\eta(s)(r)\nu(ds)
, in other words the integral of a path of
paths is defined pointwise.
For each
r\in X
we have

\displaystyle\left\|{\beta(r)}\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|{\int\eta(s)(r)\nu(ds)}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{\lambda}s\in Y\cdot\eta(s)(r)}\right\|\left\|{\nu}\right\|\text{\hskip 10.22217ptby Lemma~\ref{lemma:integral-bounded}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\eta}\right\|\left\|{\nu}\right\|

so
\beta
is a bounded function.
This function is a measurable path by
Lemma
4.10
so
\beta
belongs to
\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,{B})}
. Let
p\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{Path}(X,{B})}_{0}
so that
p=r\mathrel{\triangleright}m
for some
r\in X
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{B}_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle p(\beta)

\displaystyle=m(\beta(r))

\displaystyle=m\Big(\int\eta(s)(r)\nu(ds)\Big)

\displaystyle=\int m(\eta(s)(r))\nu(ds)

\displaystyle=\int p(\eta(s))\nu(ds)\text{\hskip 10.22217ptby definition of }p

and hence
\beta=\int\eta(s)\nu(ds)
.

Theorem 4.20
.

The operation
\mathsf{Path}
, extended to morphisms by pre- and post-composition, is a functor

{\mathbf{Ar}}^{\mathsf{op}}\times\mathbf{ICones}\to\mathbf{ICones}
. In other words, given

f\in\mathbf{ICones}(B,C)
and
\varphi\in\mathbf{Ar}(Y,X)
, we have

\mathsf{Path}(\varphi,f)=\boldsymbol{\lambda}\beta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,B)}\cdot(f\mathrel{\circ}\beta\mathrel{\circ}\varphi)\in\mathbf{ICones}({\mathsf{Path}(X,B)},{\mathsf{Path}(Y,C)})\,.

Proof 4.21
.

Functoriality is obvious.
We check first measurability of
\mathsf{Path}(\varphi,f)
so let
Y^{\prime}\in\mathbf{Ar}
and let
\eta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y^{\prime},\mathsf{Path}(X,B))}
, we must check that
\mathsf{Path}(\varphi,f)\mathrel{\circ}\eta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y^{\prime},\mathsf{Path}(Y,C))}
.
Let
Y^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbf{Ar}
and
p\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{Path}(Y,C)}_{Y^{\prime\prime}}
, we check
that
\psi=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime})\in{Y^{\prime\prime}\times Y^{\prime}}\cdot p(s^{\prime\prime},\mathsf{Path}(\varphi,f)(\eta(s^{\prime})))
is measurable.
So let
\rho\in\mathbf{Ar}(Y^{\prime\prime},Y)
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{C}_{Y^{\prime\prime}}
be such that
p=\rho\mathrel{\triangleright}m
.
Give
s^{\prime\prime}\in Y^{\prime\prime}
and
s^{\prime}\in Y^{\prime}
, we set

\displaystyle\psi(s^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime})

\displaystyle={m(s^{\prime\prime},\mathsf{Path}(\varphi,f)(\eta(s^{\prime}))(\rho(s^{\prime\prime})))}

\displaystyle={m(s^{\prime\prime},f(\eta(s^{\prime})(\varphi(\rho(s^{\prime\prime})))))}

\displaystyle={m(s^{\prime\prime},f(\mathsf{fl}(\eta)(s^{\prime},\varphi(\rho(s^{\prime\prime})))))}

and the map
\psi
is measurable by
Lemma
3.22
because
f\mathrel{\circ}\mathsf{fl}(\eta)\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y^{\prime}\times X,C)}
and
\varphi\mathrel{\circ}\rho
is measurable.
We prove that
\mathsf{Path}(\varphi,f)
preserves integrals.
Let
Y^{\prime}\in\mathbf{Ar}
,
\eta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y^{\prime},\mathsf{Path}(X,B))}
,
\nu^{\prime}\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(Y^{\prime})}
and let
s\in Y
.
We have

\displaystyle\mathsf{Path}(\varphi,f)\Big(

\displaystyle\int^{\mathsf{Path}(X,B)}_{s^{\prime}\in Y^{\prime}}\eta(s^{\prime})\nu^{\prime}(ds^{\prime})\Big)(s)=f\Big(\Big(\int^{\mathsf{Path}(X,B)}_{s^{\prime}\in Y^{\prime}}\eta(s^{\prime})\nu^{\prime}(ds^{\prime})\Big)(\varphi(s))\Big)

\displaystyle=f\Big(\int^{B}_{s^{\prime}\in Y^{\prime}}\eta(s^{\prime})(\varphi(s))\nu^{\prime}(ds^{\prime})\Big)\text{\hskip 10.22217ptby def.~of integration in }\mathsf{Path}(X,B)

\displaystyle=\int^{C}_{s^{\prime}\in Y^{\prime}}f(\eta(s^{\prime})(\varphi(s)))\nu^{\prime}(ds^{\prime})\text{\hskip 10.22217ptsince }f\text{ preserves integrals}

\displaystyle=\Big(\int^{\mathsf{Path}(Y,C)}_{s^{\prime}\in Y^{\prime}}\mathsf{Path}(\varphi,f)(\eta(s^{\prime}))\nu^{\prime}(ds^{\prime})\Big)(s)

which proves our contention.

Lemma 4.22
.

The bijection
\mathsf{fl}_{X,Y}
defined in
Lemma
3.22
, as well as its inverse, preserve
integrals and hence

\displaystyle\mathsf{fl}_{X,Y}\in\mathbf{ICones}(\mathsf{Path}(X,\mathsf{Path}(Y,B)),\mathsf{Path}(X\times Y,B))

is an iso in
\mathbf{ICones}
.

Proof 4.23
.

Results straightforwardly from the “pointwise” definition of
integration in the cones of paths.

Theorem 4.24

(Fubini)
.

Let
X,Y\in\mathbf{Ar}
,
\eta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,\mathsf{Path}(Y,B))}
,
\mu\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}
and
\nu\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(Y)}
.
We have

\displaystyle\int_{Y}\Big(\int_{X}\eta(r)\mu(dr)\Big)(s)\nu(ds)=\int_{X\times Y}\mathsf{fl}(\eta)(t)({\mu}\times{\nu})(dt)

Proof 4.25
.

Denoting by
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
these two elements of
\underline{B}
it
suffices to prove that for each
m\in\mathcal{M}^{B}_{0}
one has

m(x_{1})=m(x_{2})
.
Setting
\eta^{\prime}=\mathsf{fl}(\eta)
we have

\displaystyle x_{1}=\int_{Y}\Big(\int_{X}\eta^{\prime}(r,s)\mu(dr)\Big)\nu(ds)\hskip 40.00006ptx_{2}=\int_{X\times Y}\eta^{\prime}(t)({\mu}\times{\nu})(dt)

and the equation follows by application of the usual Fubini theorem
to the bounded non-negative measurable function
m\mathrel{\circ}\eta^{\prime}
and
to the finite measures
\mu
and
\nu
.
Notice that in the expression of
x_{2}
the variable
t
ranges
over pairs.

4.3.
The category of integrable cones

We start with proving that the category
\mathbf{ICones}
of
Definition
4.15
has all (projective) limits.
This is not only a very pleasant property of the probabilistic model
of
LL
that we are defining
14
14

14

Which is not so common among models of
LL
; there is
however a price to pay, it is very likely that the category

\mathbf{ICones}
has no
\ast
-autonomous structure.
, it will play a crucial role in our definition of the tensor product
and of the exponentials.

Theorem 4.26
.

The category
\mathbf{ICones}
is complete.

There is a faithful forgetful functor
\mathbf{ICones}\to\mathbf{Set}
which maps each integrable cone
C
to

\underline{C}
, considered as a set, and each morphism to itself; we will
see that this functor actually creates all the small limits in

\mathbf{ICones}
.

Proof 4.27
.

We prove first that
\mathbf{ICones}
has all small products. We use
implicitly Theorem
2.22
at several places.
Let
(C_{i})_{i\in I}
be a collection of integrable cones and let
P=\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}\underline{C_{i}}
which is the product of the
\underline{C_{i}}
’s in
\mathbf{Cones}
.
Given
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
,
i\in I

and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{C_{i}}_{X}
we define
\mathsf{in}_{i}(m):X\times P\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
by
\mathsf{in}_{i}(m)(r,\overrightarrow{x})=m(r,x_{i})
.
We set
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{M}_{X})_{X\in\mathbf{Ar}}
where
\mathcal{M}_{X}=\{\mathsf{in}_{i}(m)\mid i\in I\text{ and }m\in\mathcal{M}^{C_{i}}_{X}\}
. With the notations above, given
\overrightarrow{x}\in P
, the function
\boldsymbol{\lambda}r\in X\cdot\mathsf{in}_{i}(m)(r,\overrightarrow{x})=\boldsymbol{\lambda}r\in X\cdot m(r,x_{i})
is measurable
since
\mathcal{M}^{C_{i}}
satisfies
Definition 3.2
.
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathbf{Ar}(Y,X)
, we have
\mathsf{in}_{i}(m)\mathrel{\circ}(\varphi\times P)=\mathsf{in}_{i}(m\mathrel{\circ}(\varphi\times\underline{C_{i}}))\in\mathcal{M}_{Y}
since
m\mathrel{\circ}(\varphi\times\underline{C_{i}})\in\mathcal{M}^{C_{i}}_{Y}
by
Definition 3.2
.
in

C_{i}
.

Let
\overrightarrow{x(1)},\overrightarrow{x(2)}\in P
be such that
\forall p\in\mathcal{M}_{0}\ p(\overrightarrow{x(1)})=p(\overrightarrow{x(2)})
.
Then for each
i\in I
we have
x(1)_{i}=x(2)_{i}
by
Definition 3.2
.
holding in
C_{i}
and hence
\overrightarrow{x(1)}=\overrightarrow{x(2)}
.

Let
\overrightarrow{x}\in P\setminus\{0\}
and
\varepsilon>0
. Since
\left\|{\overrightarrow{x}}\right\|=\sup_{i\in I}\left\|{x_{i}}\right\|
there is
i\in I
such that
\left\|{\overrightarrow{x}}\right\|\leq\left\|{x_{i}}\right\|+\varepsilon/2
and
x_{i}\not=0
.
We can find
m\in\mathcal{M}^{C_{i}}_{0}\setminus\{0\}
such that
\left\|{x_{i}}\right\|\leq m(x_{i})/\left\|{m}\right\|+\varepsilon/2
.
Let
p=\mathsf{in}_{i}(m)\in\mathcal{M}_{0}
, notice that
\left\|{p}\right\|=\left\|{m_{i}}\right\|
since for each
x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{C_{i}}
the family
\overrightarrow{y}
defined by
y_{i}=x
and
y_{j}=0
if
j\not=i
satisfies
\overrightarrow{y}\in\mathcal{B}P
.
So we have
\left\|{\overrightarrow{x}}\right\|\leq p(\overrightarrow{x})/\left\|{p}\right\|+\varepsilon
which shows that

\mathcal{M}
satisfies
Definition 3.2
.
.

So the pair
(P,\mathcal{M})
is a measurable cone

C=\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}C_{i}
, we prove that it is integrable.
An element of
\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,C)}
is a family
(\gamma_{i}\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,C_{i})})_{i\in I}
such that
(\left\|{\gamma_{i}}\right\|)_{i\in I}
is bounded and, given

\mu\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}
, the family

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{x}=\Big(\int\gamma_{i}(r)\mu(dr)\Big)_{i\in I}

is in
P
by Lemma
4.2
and is the
integral of
\gamma
over
\mu
by definition of
\mathcal{M}^{C}
.

With the same notations as above, for each
i\in I
, the map
\mathsf{pr}_{i}\mathrel{\circ}\gamma
is a measurable path since, given
Y\in\mathbf{Ar}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{C_{i}}_{Y}
one has

\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in{Y\times X}\cdot\mathsf{in}_{i}(m)(s,\gamma(r))=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in{Y\times X}\cdot m(s,\mathsf{pr}_{i}(\gamma(r))
.
The fact that
\mathsf{pr}_{i}\in\mathbf{ICones}(C,C_{i})
results from the
definition of integration in
C
.

Let
(f_{i}\in\mathbf{ICones}(D,C_{i}))_{i\in I}
, then we know that
f=\langle{f_{i}}\rangle_{i\in I}\in\mathbf{Cones}(\underline{D},\underline{C})
. Let
\delta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,D)}
, we prove that

f\mathrel{\circ}\delta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,C)}
so let
i\in I
and

m\in\mathcal{M}^{C_{i}}_{Y}
.
We have
\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in{Y\times X}\cdot\mathsf{in}_{i}(m)(s,f(\delta(r)))=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in{Y\times X}\cdot m(s,f_{i}(\delta(r)))
and this latter
map is measurable for each
i\in I
thus proving that

f\mathrel{\circ}\delta
is measurable.
Using the same notations, let furthermore

\mu\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}
, we have

\displaystyle f\Big(\int\delta(r)\mu(dr)\Big)

\displaystyle=\Big(f_{i}\Big(\int\delta(r)\mu(dr)\Big)\Big)_{i\in I}

\displaystyle=\Big(\int f_{i}(\delta(r))\mu(dr))\Big)_{i\in I}\text{\hskip 10.22217ptsince each }f_{i}\text{ preserves integrals}

\displaystyle=\int f(\delta(r))\mu(dr)

which shows that
f\in\mathbf{ICones}(D,C)
as required.
This proves that
\mathbf{ICones}
has all small products.

We prove now that
\mathbf{ICones}
has equalizers, so let
f,g\in\mathbf{ICones}(C,D)
.
Let
(P,e\in\mathbf{Cones}(P,\underline{C}))
be the equalizer of
f
and
g

in
\mathbf{Cones}
, see Theorem
2.25
.
Remember that if
x,y\in P
satisfy
x\leq_{\underline{C}}y
then

y-x\in P
.

We define
\mathcal{M}_{X}
as the set of
all
p:X\times P\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that there is
m\in\mathcal{M}^{C}_{X}
satisfying
\forall x\in P\,\forall r\in X\ p(r,x)=m(r,x)
.
Then it is clear that
p\in({P}^{\prime})^{X}
and we actually
identify
\mathcal{M}_{X}
with
\mathcal{M}^{C}_{X}
although several elements of
the latter can induce the same element of the former.
We prove that
(\mathcal{M}_{X})_{X\in\mathbf{Ar}}
defines a measurability
structure on
P
, the only non trivial property being
Definition 3.2
.
.
Let
x\in P\setminus\{0\}
and
\varepsilon>0
.
Let
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
be such that
\varepsilon^{\prime}\leq\varepsilon
and
\varepsilon^{\prime}<\left\|{x}\right\|
(remember that
we have assumed that
x\not=0
and hence
\left\|{x}\right\|>0
).
Applying
Definition 3.2
.
in
C
we can find

m\in\mathcal{M}^{C}_{0}\setminus\{0\}
such that
\left\|{x}\right\|=\left\|{x}\right\|_{C}
satisfies

\left\|{x}\right\|\leq m(x)/\left\|{m}\right\|^{\underline{C}}+\varepsilon^{\prime}
where we have added
the superscript to
\left\|{m}\right\|
to insist on the fact that it is
computed in
\underline{C}
, that is

\left\|{m}\right\|^{\underline{C}}=\sup_{y\in\mathcal{B}\underline{C}}m(y)
.
By our assumption that
\varepsilon^{\prime}<\left\|{x}\right\|
we must have

m(x)\not=0
.
By definition of
\left\|{\_}\right\|_{P}
we have

\mathcal{B}P=P\cap\mathcal{B}\underline{C}\subseteq\mathcal{B}\underline{C}
and
hence
\left\|{m}\right\|^{P}=\sup_{y\in\mathcal{B}P}m(y)\leq\sup_{y\in\mathcal{B}\underline{C}}m(y)=\left\|{m}\right\|^{\underline{C}}
(and
\left\|{m}\right\|^{P}\not=0
since
m(x)\not=0
and
x\in P
) and
hence

\displaystyle\left\|{x}\right\|_{P}=\left\|{x}\right\|_{C}\leq\frac{m(x)}{\left\|{m}\right\|^{\underline{C}}}+\varepsilon^{\prime}\leq\frac{m(x)}{\left\|{m}\right\|^{P}}+\varepsilon

since
\left\|{m}\right\|^{P}\leq\left\|{m}\right\|^{\underline{C}}
and

\varepsilon^{\prime}\leq\varepsilon
, and since this holds for all

\varepsilon>0
, it follows that
P
satisfies
Definition 3.2
.
.

So we have defined a measurable cone
E=(P,\mathcal{M})
, we check that it
is integrable.
Let
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
,
\beta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,E)}
and
\mu\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}
, we
have

\displaystyle f\Big(\int\beta(r)\mu(dr)\Big)=\int f(\beta(r))\mu(dr)=\int g(\beta(r))\mu(dr)=g\Big(\int\beta(r)\mu(dr)\Big)

since
\beta
ranges in
\underline{E}=P
and
f
and
g
preserve
integrals.
Hence
\int\beta(r)\mu(dr)\in\underline{E}
and this element of
\underline{E}
is
the integral of
\beta
over
\mu
by definition of
\mathcal{M}^{E}
.

We check now that
(E,e)
is the equalizer of
f,g
in

\mathbf{ICones}
.
The inclusion
e\in\mathbf{Cones}(\underline{E},\underline{C})
is measurable

E\to C
by definition of the measurability structure of
E

which is essentially the same as that of
C
and preserves
integrals because the integral in
E
is defined as in
C
.

We already know that
f\,e=g\,e
.
Let
H
be
an integrable cone and
h\in\mathbf{ICones}(H,C)
be such that

f\,h=g\,h
.
Let
h_{0}
be the unique element of
\mathbf{Cones}(\underline{H},\underline{E})

such that
h=e\,h_{0}
.
Let
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
and
\gamma\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,H)}
be a measurable path of
H
.
Let
Y\in\mathbf{Ar}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{E}_{Y}
so that actually
m\in\mathcal{M}^{C}_{Y}
.
We have
\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in{Y\times X}\cdot m(s,h_{0}(\gamma(r)))=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in{Y\times X}\cdot m(s,h(\gamma(r)))
which is
measurable since
h
is.
With the same notation, taking also
\mu
in
\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}
, we have

\displaystyle h_{0}\Big(\int^{H}\gamma(r)\mu(dr)\Big)

\displaystyle=h\Big(\int^{H}\gamma(r)\mu(dr)\Big)\text{\hskip 10.22217ptby definition of }h_{0}

\displaystyle=\int^{C}h(\gamma(r))\mu(dr)\text{\hskip 10.22217ptsince }h\text{ preserves integrals}

\displaystyle=\int^{E}h_{0}(\gamma(r))\mu(dr)

and hence
h_{0}\in\mathbf{ICones}(H,E)
.
Since
h=e\,h_{0}
and is unique with this property in

\mathbf{Cones}
, it has the same properties in
\mathbf{ICones}
.

This shows that
\mathbf{ICones}
has all small limits.

Lemma 4.28
.

Let
C
be an integrable cone,
S
be a set, and let

f:\underline{C}\to S
be a bijection.
There is an integrable cone structure on
S
such that
f

is an iso in
\mathbf{ICones}
.

This structure is not unique
a priori
(other choices for the
measurability structure are possible in general), but this is not an
issue for the use that we will make of this lemma.

Proof 4.29
.

By Lemma
2.31
we can equip
S
with a
cone structure such that
f\in\mathbf{Cones}(\underline{C},S)
(we use
S
for
the cone obtained by equipping
S
with this structure), and hence

f
is an iso in
\mathbf{Cones}
since
f
is a bijection.
Let
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
and let
m\in\mathcal{M}^{C}_{X}
.
We define
{f}_{\ast}(m):X\times S\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
by
{f}_{\ast}(m)(r,z)=m(r,f^{-1}(z))
.
We set
\mathcal{M}_{X}=\{{f}_{\ast}(m)\mid m\in\mathcal{M}^{C}_{X}\}
.
In view of the definition of the algebraic structure and of the norm
of
S
, it is clear that
(\mathcal{M}_{X})_{X\in\mathbf{Ar}}
is a
measurability structure on
S
, we still use
S
for denoting
this measurable cone and we observe that
f
is an iso from
C

to
S
in
\mathbf{MCones}
.
It is also easy to check that

\mathsf{Path}(X,S)=\{f\mathrel{\circ}\gamma\mid\gamma\in\mathsf{Path}(X,C)\}
.
This cone
S
is integrable: given
\mu\in\mathsf{FMeas}(X)
and
\gamma\in\mathsf{Path}(X,S)
, we have
f^{-1}\mathrel{\circ}\gamma\in\mathsf{Path}(X,C)
and hence the integral
x=\int_{C}f^{-1}(\gamma(r))\mu(dr)\in\underline{C}
exists.
Then for each
p={f}_{\ast}(m)\in\mathcal{M}^{S}_{0}
where

m\in\mathcal{M}^{C}_{0}
, we have
p(f(x))=m(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}m(f^{-1}(\gamma(r)))\mu(dr)=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}p(\gamma(r))\mu(dr)
which shows that
\int_{S}\gamma(r)\mu(dr)
exists and is
f(x)
.
It follows also trivially that
f
preserves integrals.

Theorem 4.30
.

In the category
\mathbf{ICones}
the object
1
is a coseparator
and a separator
15
15

15

In the literature one also finds the words

generator
and
cogenerator
for such objects.
and
\mathbf{ICones}
is well-powered.

Proof 4.31
.

Let
f\not=g\in\mathbf{ICones}(C,D)
and let
x\in\underline{C}
be such
that
f(x)\not=g(x)
. By
Definition 3.2
.
there is
m\in\mathcal{M}^{C}_{0}

such that
m(f(x))\not=m(g(x))
and since
m\in\mathbf{ICones}(C,1)
(using
the definition of integrals) this shows that
1
is
a coseparator.

Given
x\in\underline{C}
we check that the function

\widehat{x}:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\underline{C}
defined by
\widehat{x}(\lambda)=\lambda x
belongs to
\mathbf{ICones}(1,C)
.
It is clearly linear and continuous.
Let
\beta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,1)}
for some
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
.
This simply means that
\beta
is a measurable and bounded
function
X\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, we must check that
\widehat{x}\mathrel{\circ}\beta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,C)}
so let
Y\in\mathbf{Ar}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{C}_{Y}
, we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in Y\times X\cdot m(s,\widehat{x}(\beta(r)))

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in Y\times X\cdot m(s,\beta(r)x)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in Y\times X\cdot\beta(r)m(s,x)

which is measurable by measurability of multiplication.
With the same notations and using moreover some

\mu\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}
we must prove that

\displaystyle\widehat{x}\Big(\int^{1}\beta(r)\mu(dr)\Big)=\int^{C}\widehat{x}(\beta(r))\mu(dr)\,.

Remember that the second member of this equation is well defined
since we have shown that
\widehat{x}\mathrel{\circ}\beta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,C)}
. To
check the equation, let
m\in\mathcal{M}^{C}_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle m\Big(\widehat{x}\Big(\int^{1}\beta(r)\mu(dr)\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle=m\Big(\Big(\int^{1}\beta(r)\mu(dr)\Big)x\Big)

\displaystyle=\Big(\int^{1}\beta(r)\mu(dr)\Big)m(x)

\displaystyle=\int^{1}\beta(r)m(x)\mu(dr)

\displaystyle=\int^{1}m(\widehat{x}(\beta(r)))\mu(dr)

\displaystyle=m\Big(\int^{C}\widehat{x}(\beta(r))\mu(dr)\Big)\,.

So
\widehat{x}\in\mathbf{ICones}(1,C)
as contended. Since
\widehat{x}(1)=x

this shows that
1
is a separator.

Let
D
be a subobject of
C
; more precisely let
h\in\mathbf{ICones}(D,C)
be a mono.
This implies that
h
is injective because
1
is
a separator.
Let
S=h(\underline{D})\subseteq\underline{C}
.
By Lemma
4.28
, there is an integrable
cone
D^{\prime}
such that
\underline{D^{\prime}}=S
(as sets) and
f
, the
corestriction of
h
to
S
, is an iso in
\mathbf{ICones}
from
D

to
D^{\prime}
.
Moreover the inclusion
e
of
S
into the set
\underline{C}

satisfies
e=h\mathrel{\circ}f^{-1}
and hence
e\in\mathbf{ICones}(D^{\prime},C)
.

We have proven that, in the slice category
\mathbf{ICones}/C
, each subobject

(D,h)
of
C
is isomorphic to a subobject
(D^{\prime},h^{\prime})
of
C

such that
h^{\prime}
is an inclusion (that is

\forall y\in\underline{D^{\prime}}\ h^{\prime}(y)=y
).
Notice finally that that the class of subobjects
(D^{\prime},h^{\prime})
of
C
such
that
h^{\prime}
is an inclusion is a set because
\mathbf{Ar}
is a set
16
16

16

It is only here that we use this assumption but it is
essential.
.
Consider indeed a subset
S
of
\underline{C}
.
The class of all structures of measurable cones
D^{\prime}
whose underlying set is
S
is contained in

\displaystyle S^{S\times S}\text{\hskip 10.22217pt(contains all possible additions)}

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle S^{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times S}\text{\hskip 10.22217pt(contains all possible scalar multiplications)}

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{S}\text{\hskip 10.22217pt(contains all possible norms)}

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\prod_{X\in\mathbf{Ar}}{\mathcal{P}}\left({\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{X\times S}}\right)\text{\hskip 10.22217pt(contains all possible measurability structures)}

which is a set
\mathcal{F}(S)

because

\mathbf{Ar}
is small
.
Now the class of all subobjects
(D^{\prime},h^{\prime})
of
C
such that

h^{\prime}
is an inclusion is
contained in
\{(S,F)\mid S\subseteq\underline{C}\text{ and }F\in\mathcal{F}(S)\}
, which is a
set.
This shows that the class of subobjects of
C
is essentially
small, that is
\mathbf{ICones}
is well-powered.

Theorem 4.32
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is a locally small category and
R:\mathbf{ICones}\to\mathcal{C}
is a functor which preserves all limits, then

R
has a left adjoint.

Proof 4.33
.

Apply the special adjoint functor theorem.

Remark 4.34
.

This implies in particular that the forgetful functor

\mathbf{ICones}\to\mathbf{MCones}
(which obviously preserves all limits) has a
left adjoint, meaning that each measurable cone can be “completed
with integrals”.

4.4.
Colimits and coproducts

Theorem 4.35
.

The category
\mathbf{ICones}
has all small colimits.

Proof 4.36
.

Let
I
be a small category, we use
\mathbf{ICones}^{I}
for the category
whose objects are the functors
I\to\mathbf{ICones}
and the morphisms are
the natural transformations, which is locally small since
I
is
small.
Then we have a “diagonal” functor
\Delta:\mathbf{ICones}\to\mathbf{ICones}^{I}

which maps each object of
\mathbf{ICones}
to the corresponding constant
functor and each morphism to the identity natural transformation.
It is easily checked that
\Delta
preserves all limits and hence
it has a left adjoint by Theorem
4.32
.
By definition of an adjunction, this functor maps each functor

I\to\mathbf{ICones}
to its colimit which shows that
\mathbf{ICones}
is
cocomplete.

This theorem does not give any insight on the structure of these
colimits
17
17

17

In particular it would be interesting to have a more
explicit description of coequalizers.
, so it is reasonable to have at least a closer look at coproducts.

Coproducts of cones

Let
I
be a set, without any restrictions on its cardinality for
the time being.
Let
(P_{i})_{i\in I}
be a family of cones. Let
P
be the set of
all families
\overrightarrow{x}=(x_{i})_{i\in I}\in\prod_{i\in I}P_{i}
such that
\sum_{i\in I}\left\|{x_{i}}\right\|<\infty
.
Notice that for such a family
\overrightarrow{x}
, the set

\{i\in I\mid x_{i}\not=0\}
is countable.
We turn
P
into a cone by defining the operations componentwise and
by setting
\left\|{\overrightarrow{x}}\right\|=\sum_{i\in I}\left\|{x_{i}}\right\|
.
The induced cone order relation on
P
is the pointwise order
and
\omega
-completeness is easily proven (by commutations of lubs with
sums in
\overline{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}
).
In
\mathbf{Cones}
, this cone
P
is the coproduct of
the
P_{i}
’s with obvious injections
\mathsf{in}_{i}\in\mathbf{Cones}(P_{i},P)

mapping
x
to the family
\overrightarrow{x}
such that
x_{i}=x
and

x_{j}=0
for
j\not=i
.
Given a family
(f_{i}\in\mathbf{Cones}(P_{i},Q))_{i\in I}
the unique map
\left[{f_{i}}\right]_{i\in I}\in\mathbf{Cones}(P,Q)
such that
\forall j\in I\ \left[{f_{i}}\right]_{i\in I}\,\mathsf{in}_{j}=f_{j}
is given by

\displaystyle\left[{f_{i}}\right]_{i\in I}(\overrightarrow{x})=\sum_{i\in I}f_{i}(x_{i})\,.

This sum converges because for each finite
J\subseteq I
one has

\displaystyle\left\|{\sum_{i\in J}f_{i}(x_{i})}\right\|\leq\sum_{i\in J}\left\|{f_{i}(x_{i})}\right\|\leq\sum_{i\in J}\left\|{x_{i}}\right\|=\left\|{x}\right\|

and this map
\left[{f_{i}}\right]_{i\in I}
is easily seen to be linear
and continuous.
We use
\mathop{\oplus}_{i\in I}P_{i}
for the cone
P
defined in that way.

Lemma 4.37
.

For each cone
Q
the cones
{(\mathop{\oplus}_{i\in I}P_{i})}\multimap{Q}
and
\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}\left({P_{i}}\multimap{Q}\right)
are isomorphic in
\mathbf{Cones}
.

Proof 4.38
.

The fact that
\mathop{\oplus}_{i\in I}P_{i}
is the coproduct of the

P_{i}
’s means that the function

\mathcal{B}({(\mathop{\oplus}_{i\in I}P_{i})}\multimap{Q})\to\mathcal{B}(\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}\left({P_{i}}\multimap{Q}\right))

which maps
f
to
(f\,\mathsf{in}_{i})_{i\in I}
is a bijection. It is linear
and continuous by linearity and continuity of composition of
morphisms. So this bijection is an isomorphism.

In particular
(\mathop{\oplus}_{i\in I}P_{i})^{\prime}\simeq\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}{P_{i}^{\prime}}
. Given
\overrightarrow{x^{\prime}}\in\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}P_{i}^{\prime}
the associated linear and
continuous form
\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{fun}}}(\overrightarrow{x^{\prime}})
on
\mathop{\oplus}_{i\in I}P_{i}
is
given by

\displaystyle\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{fun}}}(\overrightarrow{x^{\prime}})(\overrightarrow{x})=\langle\overrightarrow{x},\overrightarrow{x^{\prime}}\rangle=\sum_{i\in I}\langle x_{i},x^{\prime}_{i}\rangle\leq\left\|{\overrightarrow{x}}\right\|\left\|{\overrightarrow{x^{\prime}}}\right\|\,.

We use these observations in the sequel.

Coproduct of measurable cones

Let
(C_{i})_{i\in I}
be a family of measurable cones. Let

P=\mathop{\oplus}_{i\in I}\underline{C_{i}}
.
Let
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{M}_{X})_{X\in\mathbf{Ar}}
where
\mathcal{M}_{X}
is the set of all

p\in(P^{\prime})^{X}
such that there is a family of coefficients

(\lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})_{i\in I}
with
\boldsymbol{\lambda}r\in X\cdot\lambda_{i}p(r)_{i}\in\mathcal{M}^{C_{i}}_{X}
, identifying

P^{\prime}
with
\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}\underline{C_{i}}^{\prime}
as explained above.
In other words
p\in\mathcal{M}_{X}
means that there are families
\overrightarrow{m}=(m_{i}\in\mathcal{M}^{C_{i}}_{X})_{i\in I}
and
\overrightarrow{\lambda}=(\lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})_{i\in I}
such that, for all

r\in X
, the family
(\lambda_{i}\left\|{m_{i}(r)}\right\|)_{i\in I}
is bounded by
1
, and we have

p(r)=\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{fun}}}(\overrightarrow{\lambda}\overrightarrow{m}(r))
(where

\overrightarrow{\lambda}\overrightarrow{x}=(\lambda_{i}x_{i})_{i\in I}
).
Remember indeed from
Definition 3.2
.
that, for each measurable cone
C
, it
is assumed that each
m\in\mathcal{M}^{C}_{X}
satisfies that

m(r,x)\in[0,1]
for all
r\in X
and

x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{C}
.

Remark 4.39
.

These coefficients
\lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
are necessary because in the
definition of measurable cones, we make very weak assumptions about
the sets of measurability tests, in particular we do not assume that
they are closed under multiplication by nonnegative coefficients

\leq 1
.
Such assumptions — and stronger ones, for instance, as suggested
by one of the reviewers, we could require these sets of tests to be
cones with operations defined pointwise — would be quite
meaningful, but would require to check additional conditions in the
proofs, for artificial reasons.
The sets of measurability tests of a cone
C
should be understood
as a kind of “predual” of the cone of paths
\mathsf{FMeas}(X,C)
, in
the sense that the criterion for a bounded map
X\to\underline{C}
to
belong to this cone is the measurability of the (suitably defined)
composition of this map with all measurability tests.

We prove that
\mathcal{M}
is a measurability structure on
P
.
Given
p\in\mathcal{M}_{X}
and
\overrightarrow{x}\in P
the map
\boldsymbol{\lambda}r\in X\cdot p(r)(\overrightarrow{x})
is measurable by the monotone
convergence theorem because the set
\{i\in I\mid x_{i}\not=0\}
is
countable so the condition
Definition 3.2
.
holds.
The conditions
Definition 3.2
.
and
Definition 3.2
.
obviously hold, let us check

Definition 3.2
.
. Let
\overrightarrow{x}\in P\setminus\{0\}
and let

\varepsilon>0
.
Let
J=\{i\in I\mid x_{i}\not=0\}
which is countable and let

(i(n))_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be an enumeration of this set (assuming that it
is infinite; the case where it is finite is simpler).
For each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
let
m_{n}\in\mathcal{M}^{C_{i(n)}}_{X}
be such that

m_{n}\not=0
and

\displaystyle\left\|{x_{i(n)}}\right\|_{C_{i(n)}}\leq\frac{m_{n}(x_{i(n)})}{\left\|{m_{n}}\right\|}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2^{n+1}}\,.

Let
p\in\mathcal{M}_{0}
be given by

p(\overrightarrow{y})=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\frac{m_{n}(y_{i(n)})}{\left\|{m_{n}}\right\|}
.
We have
p(y)\leq\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\left\|{y_{i(n)}}\right\|\leq 1
and hence

0<\left\|{p}\right\|\leq 1
.
So we have

\displaystyle\left\|{\overrightarrow{x}}\right\|=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\left\|{x_{i(n)}}\right\|\leq\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\frac{m_{n}(x_{i(n)})}{\left\|{m_{n}}\right\|}+\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\frac{\varepsilon}{2^{n+1}}=p(\overrightarrow{x})+\varepsilon\leq\frac{p(\overrightarrow{x})}{\left\|{p}\right\|}+\varepsilon

proving our contention. We have shown that
C=(P,\mathcal{M})
is a
measurable cone that we denote as
\mathop{\oplus}_{i\in I}C_{i}
.

Theorem 4.40
.

For each
j\in I
one has

(\mathsf{in}_{j}\in\mathbf{MCones}(C_{j},\mathop{\oplus}_{i\in I}C_{i}))_{i\in I}
.

If
I
is countable then

(\mathop{\oplus}_{i\in I}C_{i},(\mathsf{in}_{i})_{i\in I})
is the coproduct of the

C_{i}
’s in
\mathbf{MCones}
.
If moreover the
C_{i}
’s are integrable then so is

\mathop{\oplus}_{i\in I}C_{i}
, the
\mathsf{in}_{i}
’s preserve integrals and

(\mathop{\oplus}_{i\in I}C_{i},(\mathsf{in}_{i})_{i\in I}
is the coproduct of the

C_{i}
’s in
\mathbf{ICones}
.

Proof 4.41
.

The measurability of the
\mathsf{in}_{i}
’s is easy to prove.

We assume that
I
is countable.
Let
(f_{i}\in\mathbf{MCones}(C_{i},D))_{i\in I}
, we have already defined
f=\left[{f_{i}}\right]_{i\in I}\in\mathbf{Cones}(\mathop{\oplus}_{i\in I}{\underline{C_{i}}},\underline{D})
and we must prove that this function is
measurable.
Let
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
and
\gamma\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,\mathop{\oplus}_{i\in I}C_{i})}
, we prove that
f\mathrel{\circ}\gamma\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,D)}
so let
Y\in\mathbf{Ar}
and
q\in\mathcal{M}^{D}_{Y}
, we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in{Y\times X}\cdot q(s,f(\gamma(r)))

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in{Y\times X}\cdot q\big(s,\sum_{i\in I}f_{i}(\gamma(r)_{i})\big)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in{Y\times X}\cdot\sum_{i\in I}q(s,f_{i}(\gamma(r)_{i}))

which is measurable by the monotone convergence theorem since
I

is countable.

Assume moreover that the
C_{i}
’s are integrable and let

\mu\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}
.
For each
i\in I
we have
\boldsymbol{\lambda}r\in X\cdot\gamma(r)_{i}\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,C_{i})}

because, for each
Y\in\mathbf{Ar}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{C_{i}}_{Y}
we know
that
\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in{Y\times X}\cdot p(s,\gamma(r))
is measurable, where
p=\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{fun}}}(\overrightarrow{m})
with
m_{j}=m
if
i=j

and
m_{j}=0
otherwise
18
18

18

It is harmless to assume that
0\in\mathcal{M}^{B}_{X}
for each
measurable cone
B
and
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
: if
B
is a measurable
cone and
C=(\underline{B},\mathcal{M})
where
\mathcal{M}_{X}=\mathcal{M}^{B}_{X}\cup\{0\}

then
C
is a measurable cone and
B
and
C
are isomorphic
in
\mathbf{MCones}
, see Remark
3.16
.
And similarly in the category of integrable cones.
. Therefore we can define
\overrightarrow{x}\in\prod_{i\in I}\underline{C_{i}}
by
x_{i}=\int\gamma(r)_{i}\mu(dr)
.

Given
p=\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{fun}}}(\overrightarrow{\lambda}\overrightarrow{m})\in\mathcal{M}^{C}_{0}
, the map

p\mathrel{\circ}\gamma:X\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is bounded and measurable, and
we have

\displaystyle\int p(\gamma(r))\mu(dr)

\displaystyle=\int\big(\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{i}m_{i}(\gamma(r)_{i})\big)\mu(dr)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in I}\int\lambda_{i}m_{i}(\gamma(r)_{i})\mu(dr)\text{\hskip 10.22217ptby the monotone convergence theorem,}

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt\text{since }I\text{ is countable}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{i}m_{i}\Big(\int\gamma(r)_{i}\mu(dr)\Big)\text{\hskip 10.22217ptby definition of integrals}

\displaystyle=p(\overrightarrow{x})\,.

By
Definition 3.2
.
holding in
C
as shown above, this proves that

\left\|{\overrightarrow{x}}\right\|<\infty
and hence
\overrightarrow{x}\in\underline{C}
and the
computation above shows also that
\overrightarrow{x}=\int\gamma(r)\mu(dr)

and hence the cone
C
is integrable. The proof that it is the
coproduct of the
C_{i}
’s in
\mathbf{ICones}
is routine.

Even if
I
is not countable we know that
(C_{i})_{i\in I}
has a
coproduct in
\mathbf{ICones}
by Theorem
4.35
, but
we don’t know yet how to describe it concretely.

5.
Internal linear hom and the tensor product

The main goal of this section is to define a tensor product of two
integrable cones, to prove that this operation is functorial and that

\mathbf{ICones}
can be equipped with a structure of symmetric monoidal
category (SMC) which is closed (SMCC).

Remark 5.1
.

Of course we first tried to define the tensor product concretely as
one usually does in algebra, using some quotient.
However the complicated interaction between the algebraic and the
order theoretic properties of cones made the resulting description
ineffective for proving basic properties expected from a tensor
product.
Now that we know that the tensor product exists for abstract
reasons, and has the required structures and properties, the quest
for a reasonably simple concrete description can be undertaken with
a more relaxed mind.

We define first the integrable cone
{C}\multimap{D}
of
linear, continuous, measurable and integrable morphisms
C\to D
.
There are two good reasons for doing so.

•

The definition of this object is easy and natural.

•

Building this object will be necessary for proving that the SMC
we define is closed.

Moreover, it is easy to prove that the associated internal hom functor

{C}\multimap{\_}:\mathbf{ICones}\to\mathbf{ICones}
preserves all limits.
Then, thanks to Theorem
4.32
this functor
has a left adjoint
{\_}\mathop{\otimes}{C}:\mathbf{ICones}\to\mathbf{ICones}
, and this operation
is also functorial wrt.
C
because
{\_}\multimap{\_}
is a functor

{\mathbf{ICones}}^{\mathsf{op}}\times\mathbf{ICones}\to\mathbf{ICones}
(thanks to a standard result in
category theory), and we have a natural bijection of sets
\mathbf{ICones}({B}\mathop{\otimes}{C},D)\to\mathbf{ICones}(B,{C}\multimap{D})
.

Last we prove that this natural bijection is actually an isomorphism

({{B}\mathop{\otimes}{C}}\multimap{D})\to({B}\multimap{({C}\multimap{D})})
in
\mathbf{ICones}
and
we show how to derive the SMC structure of
\mathbf{ICones}
from this
property.

We are convinced that this method is exactly the one described
axiomatically in
[
EK66
]
.
However we do not prove explicitly that
\mathbf{ICones}
is closed in the
sense of that paper and do not apply explicitly its results, first for
the sake of self-containedness and also because, due to the concrete
features of our category (basically: our morphisms are functions) the
direct approach remains tractable.

Another approach, equivalent but conceptually more
elegant, would have been to describe first

\mathbf{ICones}
as a multicategory, introducing from the beginning a
notion of multilinear morphism on
\mathbf{ICones}
in a completely standard
way.
Then the tensor product would have been defined by a familiar
universal property wrt. bilinear morphisms.

5.1.
The cone of linear morphisms

Let
C
and
D
be objects of
\mathbf{ICones}
and let
P
be the set
of all
f:\underline{C}\to\underline{D}
such that, for some
\varepsilon>0
, one
has
\varepsilon f\in\mathbf{ICones}(C,D)
, equipped with the same algebraic
structure as
{\underline{C}}\multimap{\underline{D}}
(see Lemma
2.17
).
This makes sense since the algebraic laws of the cone

{\underline{C}}\multimap{\underline{D}}
preserve measurability and since
integration is linear.
Moreover given an increasing sequence
(f_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of measurable
and integral preserving elements of
{\underline{C}}\multimap{\underline{D}}
such
that
\left\|{f_{n}}\right\|\leq 1
(remember that

\left\|{f}\right\|=\sup_{x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{C}}\left\|{f(x)}\right\|
), the linear and
continuous map
f=\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}f_{n}
is measurable and preserves
integrals by the monotone convergence theorem, as we show now.

Let
\gamma\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,C)}
be a measurable path and let

m\in\mathcal{M}^{D}_{Y}
for some
Y\in\mathbf{Ar}
.
The function
\varphi=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in{Y\times X}\cdot m(s,f(\gamma(r)):{Y\times X}\to[0,1]
satisfies
\varphi(s,r)=\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\varphi_{n}
where
(\varphi_{n}=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in{Y\times X}\cdot m(s,f_{n}(\gamma(r)))_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is an increasing sequence of measurable functions by measurability of
the
f_{n}
’s and linearity and continuity of
m
in its second parameter, so
\varphi
is measurable which shows
that
f
is measurable.
Next, with the same notations and taking moreover some

\mu\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}
we have, for each
m\in\mathcal{M}^{D}_{0}
,

\displaystyle m\Big(f\Big(\int^{C}\gamma(r)\mu(dr))\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle=m\Big(\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}f_{n}\Big(\int^{C}\gamma(r)\mu(dr)\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle=m\Big(\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\int^{D}f_{n}(\gamma(r))\mu(dr)\Big)

\displaystyle=\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}m\Big(\int^{D}f_{n}(\gamma(r))\mu(dr)\Big)\text{\quad by cont.~of }m

\displaystyle=\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\int^{1}m(f_{n}(\gamma(r)))\mu(dr)\text{\quad by def.~of integration in }D

\displaystyle=\int^{1}\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}m(f_{n}(\gamma(r)))\mu(dr)\text{\quad by the monotone conv.~th.}

\displaystyle=\int^{1}m(f(\gamma(r)))\mu(dr)\text{\quad by continuity of }m

\displaystyle=m\Big(\int^{D}f(\gamma(r))\mu(dr)\Big)\text{\quad by def.~of integration in }D

and hence
f
preserves integrals by
Definition 3.2
.
.

Given
\gamma\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,C)}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{D}_{X}
we define
\gamma\mathrel{\triangleright}m=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(r,f)\in X\times P\cdot m(r,f(\gamma(r))):X\times P\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.
For each
r\in X
the function
l=(\gamma\mathrel{\triangleright}m)(r,\_):\underline{{C}\multimap{D}}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is linear and
continuous by linearity and continuity of
m
in its second
argument.
We define
\mathcal{M}_{X}=\{\gamma\mathrel{\triangleright}m\mid\gamma\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,C)}\text{ and
}m\in\mathcal{M}^{D}_{X}\}
.

Remark 5.2
.

We use the same notations for measurability tests on the cone of
linear, continuous and integrable morphisms as for the cone of
measurable paths in Section
3.2.2
for two reasons.
The first one is that these tests are defined in a very similar way,
the second one is Theorem
6.1
which allows to
see measurable paths as linear, continuous and integrable maps.

We check that the family
(\mathcal{M}_{X})
is a measurability structure
on the cone
P=\left({\underline{C}}\multimap{\underline{D}}\right)
.

\blacktriangleright

Definition 3.2
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{B}P
,
\gamma\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,C)}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{D}_{X}
, then the map
\varphi=\boldsymbol{\lambda}r\in X\cdot m(r,f(\gamma(r)))
is measurable because

f\mathrel{\circ}\gamma\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,D)}
by measurability
of
f
and hence

\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in{X\times X}\cdot m(s,f(\gamma(r))):{X\times X}\to[0,1]
is measurable from which follows the measurability
of
\varphi
.
The fact that
\varphi
ranges in
[0,1]

results from the assumption that
\left\|{f}\right\|\leq 1
.

\blacktriangleright

Definition 3.2
.

Let
\gamma\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,C)}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{D}_{X}
, and let
\varphi\in\mathbf{Ar}(Y,X)
for some
Y\in\mathbf{Ar}
.
Then we have
(\gamma\mathrel{\triangleright}m)\mathrel{\circ}(\varphi\times P)=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,f)\in Y\times P\cdot m(\varphi(s),f(\gamma(\varphi(s)))))=(\gamma\mathrel{\circ}\varphi)\mathrel{\triangleright}(m\mathrel{\circ}(\varphi\times\underline{D}))
and since

\gamma\mathrel{\circ}\varphi\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,C)}
by
Lemma
3.10
and
m\mathrel{\circ}(\varphi\times\underline{D})\in\mathcal{M}^{D}_{Y}
by property
Definition 3.2
.

satisfied in
D
, we have
(\gamma\mathrel{\triangleright}m)\mathrel{\circ}(\varphi\times P)\in\mathcal{M}_{Y}
.

\blacktriangleright

Definition 3.2
.

Let
f_{1},f_{2}\in P
and assume that for all
x\in\underline{C}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{D}_{0}
one has
(x\mathrel{\triangleright}m)(f_{1})=(x\mathrel{\triangleright}m)(f_{2})
, that is
m(f_{1}(x))=m(f_{2}(x))
.
By
Definition 3.2
.
in
D
we have
f_{1}(x)=f_{2}(x)
, and since this holds for all
x\in\underline{C}
we have
f_{1}=f_{2}
.

\blacktriangleright

Definition 3.2
.

Let
f\in P\setminus\{0\}
.
Let
\varepsilon>0
, we can assume without loss of generality that

\varepsilon<2\left\|{f}\right\|
.
By definition of
\left\|{f}\right\|
there is
x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{C}
such
that
\left\|{f}\right\|\leq\left\|{f(x)}\right\|+\varepsilon/3
and hence

\left\|{f}\right\|<\left\|{f(x)}\right\|+\varepsilon/2
.
This implies in particular that
f(x)\not=0
by our assumption that

\varepsilon<2\left\|{f}\right\|
.
By
Definition 3.2
.
in
D
there is

m\in\mathcal{M}^{D}_{0}\setminus\{0\}
such that

\left\|{f(x)}\right\|\leq m(f(x))/\left\|{m}\right\|+\min(\varepsilon/2,\left\|{f}\right\|-\varepsilon/2)\,.

If
m(f(x))=0
we have
\left\|{f(x)}\right\|\leq\left\|{f}\right\|-\varepsilon/2
which
is not possible since
\left\|{f}\right\|<\left\|{f(x)}\right\|+\varepsilon/2
, so

(x\mathrel{\triangleright}m)(f)=m(f(x))\not=0
.
This implies in particular that
\left\|{x\mathrel{\triangleright}m}\right\|\not=0
.
We have
\left\|{x\mathrel{\triangleright}m}\right\|=\sup_{g\in\mathcal{B}P}m(g(x))\leq\left\|{m}\right\|\sup_{g\in\mathcal{B}P}\left\|{g(x)}\right\|\leq\left\|{m}\right\|
since

x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{C}
, and hence

\displaystyle\left\|{f}\right\|\leq\left\|{f(x)}\right\|+\varepsilon/2

\displaystyle\leq\frac{m(f(x))}{\left\|{m}\right\|}+\varepsilon=\frac{(x\mathrel{\triangleright}m)(f)}{\left\|{m}\right\|}+\varepsilon

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(x\mathrel{\triangleright}m)(f)}{\left\|{x\mathrel{\triangleright}m}\right\|}+\varepsilon\text{\quad since }0<\left\|{x\mathrel{\triangleright}m}\right\|\leq\left\|{m}\right\|\,.

So we have defined a measurable cone that we denote as
{C}\multimap{D}
.

We will need a technical lemma whose intuitive meaning is interesting

per se
: a linear morphisms valued in a cone of paths is the
same thing as a path valued in the measurable cone of linear morphisms
just defined.
This lemma will be essential for proving that the measurable cone

{C}\multimap{D}
is integrable.

Lemma 5.3
.

There is an argument swapping isomorphism

\mathsf{sw}\in\mathbf{MCones}(C\multimap\mathsf{Path}(X,D),\mathsf{Path}(X,C\multimap D)

which maps
f
to
\boldsymbol{\lambda}r\in X\cdot\boldsymbol{\lambda}x\in\underline{C}\cdot f(x)(r)
.

This iso preserves integrals, but this property is not required for
what follows.

Proof 5.4
.

Let
f\in\underline{C\multimap\mathsf{Path}(X,D)}
.
If
r\in X
, the map
g=\boldsymbol{\lambda}x\in\underline{C}\cdot f(x)(r):\underline{C}\to\underline{D}
is linear and
continuous because
f
is, and the algebraic operations and the
lubs are computed pointwise in
\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,D)}
, we prove that

g
is measurable.
Let
Y,Y^{\prime}\in\mathbf{Ar}
,
\gamma\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,C)}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{D}_{Y^{\prime}}
, we set
\varphi=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s^{\prime},s)\in{Y^{\prime}\times Y}\cdot m(s^{\prime},g(\gamma(s)))=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s^{\prime},s)\in{Y^{\prime}\times Y}\cdot m(s^{\prime},f(\gamma(s))(r))
.
Notice that, identifying
r
with the constant
r
-valued
measurable function
Y^{\prime}\to X
, we have

r\mathrel{\triangleright}m\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{Path}(X,D)}_{Y^{\prime}}
and
\varphi=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s^{\prime},s)\in Y^{\prime}\times Y\cdot(r\mathrel{\triangleright}m)(s^{\prime},f(\gamma(s)))

which is measurable because
f\mathrel{\circ}\gamma\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,\mathsf{Path}(X,D))}
since
f\in\underline{C\multimap\mathsf{Path}(X,D)}
.
This shows that
g
is measurable, we prove that
g
preserves
integrals so let moreover
\nu\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(Y)}
, we have

\displaystyle g\Big(\int_{s\in Y}^{C}\gamma(s)\nu(ds)\Big)

\displaystyle=f\Big(\int_{s\in Y}^{C}\gamma(s)\nu(ds)\Big)(r)

\displaystyle=\Big(\int_{s\in Y}^{\mathsf{Path}(X,D)}f(\gamma(s))\nu(ds)\Big)(r)\text{\hskip 10.22217ptsince }f\text{ preserves integrals.}

\displaystyle=\int_{s\in Y}^{D}f(\gamma(s))(r)\nu(ds)

\displaystyle=\int_{s\in Y}^{D}g(\gamma(s))\nu(ds)\,.

since integrals in
\mathsf{Path}(X,D)
are computed pointwise.
This shows that
g=\mathsf{sw}(f)(r)\in\underline{{C}\multimap{D}}
for all

r\in X
.

We prove next that
\eta=\mathsf{sw}(f)
belongs to
\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,C\multimap D)}
so let
Y\in\mathbf{Ar}
and
p\in\mathcal{M}^{C\multimap D}_{Y}
.
Let
\gamma\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,C)}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{D}_{Y}
be such that
p=\gamma\mathrel{\triangleright}m
.
The function
\varphi=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in Y\times X\cdot p(s,\eta(r))
satisfies

\displaystyle\varphi

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in Y\times X\cdot m(s,\eta(r)(\gamma(s)))

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in Y\times X\cdot m(s,f(\gamma(s))(r))\,.

We know that
\delta=f\mathrel{\circ}\gamma\mathrel{\circ}\mathsf{pr}_{1}\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y\times X,\mathsf{Path}(X,D))}

because
\gamma\mathrel{\circ}\mathsf{pr}_{1}\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y\times X,C)}

and
f\in\mathbf{MCones}(C,\mathsf{Path}(X,D))
.
Let
m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}^{D}_{Y\times X}
be defined by
m^{\prime}(s,r,y)=m(s,y)
, we have
\mathsf{pr}_{2}\mathrel{\triangleright}m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{Path}(X,D)}_{Y\times X}
and hence
\varphi^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in Y\times X\cdot(\mathsf{pr}_{2}\mathrel{\triangleright}m^{\prime})(s,r,\delta(s,r))
is measurable.
But

\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime}(s,r)={m^{\prime}(s,r,\delta(s,r)(\mathsf{pr}_{2}(s,r)))}={m(s,f(\gamma(s))(r))}=\varphi(s,r)

so that
\varphi
is measurable, this shows that
\mathsf{sw}(f)\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,C\multimap D)}
.
The linearity and continuity of
\mathsf{sw}
are obvious (the
algebraic operations and lubs are defined pointwise) as well as the
fact that
\left\|{\mathsf{sw}}\right\|\leq 1
.
Its measurability relies on the obvious bijection between
\mathcal{M}^{C\multimap\mathsf{Path}(X,D)}_{Y^{\prime}}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{Path}(X,C\multimap D)}_{Y^{\prime}}
which maps
\gamma\mathrel{\triangleright}(\varphi\mathrel{\triangleright}m)
to
\varphi\mathrel{\triangleright}(\gamma\mathrel{\triangleright}m)
for all
Y^{\prime}\in\mathbf{Ar}
(with

\gamma\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y^{\prime},C)}
,
\varphi\in\mathbf{Ar}(Y^{\prime},X)
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{D}_{Y^{\prime}}
).
We have proven that
\mathsf{sw}
is a morphism in
\mathbf{MCones}
.

Conversely given
\eta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,C\multimap D)}
we define
f=\mathsf{sw}^{\prime}(\eta)=\boldsymbol{\lambda}x\in\underline{C}\cdot\boldsymbol{\lambda}r\in X\cdot\eta(r)(x)
and prove first that
f\in\underline{C\multimap\mathsf{Path}(X,D)}
.
Let
x\in\underline{C}
and
\delta=f(x):X\to\underline{D}
.
If
r\in X
we have
\eta(r)\leq\left\|{\eta}\right\|
and hence
\left\|{\delta(r)}\right\|=\left\|{\eta(r)(x)}\right\|\leq\left\|{\eta}\right\|\left\|{x}\right\|
which
shows that the function
\delta
is bounded.
Let
Y\in\mathbf{Ar}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{D}_{Y}
, we set
\varphi=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in Y\times X\cdot m(s,\delta(r))
.
For
s\in Y
and
r\in X
, we have

\displaystyle\varphi(s,r)={m(s,\delta(r))}={m(s,\eta(r)(x))}={(x\mathrel{\triangleright}m)}(s,\eta(r))

where we identify
x
with the path
\gamma\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,C)}

such that
\gamma(s)=x
, so that
x\mathrel{\triangleright}m\in\mathcal{M}^{{C}\multimap{D}}_{Y}
.
It follows that
\varphi
is measurable and hence
\delta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,D)}
.

Linearity of
f
is obvious and continuity results from the fact that lubs
in
\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,D)}
are computed pointwise.
Let
\gamma\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,C)}
for some
Y\in\mathbf{Ar}
, we must
prove next that
f\mathrel{\circ}\gamma\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,\mathsf{Path}(X,D))}
.
Let
Y^{\prime}\in\mathbf{Ar}
and
p\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{Path}(X,D)}_{Y^{\prime}}
, we must
prove that
\psi=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s^{\prime},s)\in Y^{\prime}\times Y\cdot p(s^{\prime},f(\gamma(s)))
is measurable.
Let
\varphi\in\mathbf{Ar}(Y^{\prime},X)
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{D}_{Y^{\prime}}
be such that
p=\varphi\mathrel{\triangleright}m
.
For
s^{\prime}\in Y^{\prime}
and
s\in Y
we have

\displaystyle\psi(s^{\prime},s)

\displaystyle={m(s^{\prime},f(\gamma(s))(\varphi(s^{\prime}))}

\displaystyle={m(s^{\prime},\eta(\varphi(s^{\prime}))(\gamma(s)))}

\displaystyle={((\gamma\mathrel{\circ}\mathsf{pr}_{2})\mathrel{\triangleright}(m\mathrel{\circ}(\mathsf{pr}_{1}\times\underline{D})))(s^{\prime},s,\eta\mathrel{\circ}\varphi)}

and hence
\psi
is measurable since
\eta\mathrel{\circ}\varphi
is a
measurable path.
This shows that
f
is measurable, we prove last that
f

preserves integrals.
So let
\gamma\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,C)}
and
\mu\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(Y)}
.
Given
r\in X
we have

\displaystyle f\Big(\int^{C}_{s\in Y}\gamma(s)\mu(ds)\Big)(r)

\displaystyle=\eta(r)\Big(\int^{C}_{s\in Y}\gamma(s)\mu(ds)\Big)

\displaystyle=\int^{D}_{s\in Y}\eta(r)(\gamma(s))\mu(ds)\text{\hskip 10.22217ptsince }\eta(r)\in\underline{{C}\multimap{D}}

\displaystyle=\int^{D}_{s\in Y}f(\gamma(s))(r)\mu(ds)\text{\hskip 10.22217ptby definition of }f

\displaystyle=\Big(\int^{\mathsf{Path}(X,D)}_{s\in Y}f(\gamma(s))\mu(ds)\Big)(r)

since integrals in
\mathsf{Path}(X,D)
are computed pointwise.

The proof that
\mathsf{sw}^{\prime}
is a morphism in
\mathbf{MCones}
follows the same pattern as for
\mathsf{sw}
.

Lemma 5.5
.

The measurable cone
{C}\multimap{D}
is integrable.

Proof 5.6
.

Let
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
,
\eta\in\mathsf{Path}(X,C\multimap D)
and
\mu\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}
.
Let

\displaystyle f=\boldsymbol{\lambda}x\in\underline{C}\cdot\int^{D}\eta(r)(x)\mu(dr)=\boldsymbol{\lambda}x\in\underline{C}\cdot\int^{D}\mathsf{sw}(\eta)(x)(r)\mu(dr)\,.

This function is well defined since for each
x\in\underline{C}
one has
\mathsf{sw}(\eta)(x)\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,D)}
by
Lemma
5.3
so that the integral
\int\mathsf{sw}(\eta)(x)(r)\mu(dr)\in\underline{D}
is well defined.
The fact that
f:\underline{C}\to\underline{D}
is linear and continuous results
from the linearity of integration and from the monotone convergence
theorem.
Let us check that
f
is measurable so let
Y\in\mathbf{Ar}

and let
\gamma\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,C)}
, we must prove that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\lambda}s\in Y\cdot\int^{D}\mathsf{sw}(\eta)(\gamma(s))(r)\mu(dr)\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,D)}

so let
Y^{\prime}\in\mathbf{Ar}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{D}_{Y^{\prime}}
, we must check that
the function

\displaystyle\psi

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s^{\prime},s)\in Y^{\prime}\times Y\cdot m\Big(s^{\prime},\int^{D}\mathsf{sw}(\eta)(\gamma(s))(r)\mu(dr)\Big)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s^{\prime},s)\in Y^{\prime}\times Y\cdot\int^{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}m(s^{\prime},\mathsf{sw}(\eta)(\gamma(s))(r))\mu(dr)

is measurable.
We know that the function
\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s^{\prime},s,r)\in Y^{\prime}\times Y\times X\cdot m(s^{\prime},\mathsf{sw}(\eta)(\gamma(s))(r))
is measurable and bounded
because
\mathsf{sw}(\eta)\mathrel{\circ}\gamma\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,\mathsf{Path}(X,D))}
by Lemma
5.3
and we get the announced
measurability by Lemma
4.10
(in the special case
where
\kappa
is the kernel constantly equal to
\mu
). Next we prove that
f
preserves integrals, so let moreover

\nu\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(Y)}
, we have

\displaystyle f\Big(\int^{C}_{s\in Y}\gamma(s)\nu(ds)\Big)

\displaystyle=\int^{D}_{r\in X}\eta(r)\Big(\int^{C}_{s\in Y}\gamma(s)\nu(ds)\Big)\mu(dr)

\displaystyle=\int^{D}_{r\in X}\Big(\int^{D}_{s\in Y}\eta(r)(\gamma(s))\nu(ds)\Big)\mu(dr)\text{\hskip 10.22217ptsince }\eta(r)\in\underline{C\multimap D}

\displaystyle=\int^{D}_{r\in X}\Big(\int^{D}_{s\in Y}\mathsf{sw}(\eta)(\gamma(s))(r)\nu(ds)\Big)\mu(dr)

\displaystyle=\int^{D}_{s\in Y}\Big(\int^{D}_{r\in X}\mathsf{sw}(\eta)(\gamma(s))(r)\mu(dr)\Big)\nu(ds)

\displaystyle\hskip 30.00005pt\text{by Th.~\ref{th:paths-Fubini} (Fubini), since }\mathsf{sw}(\eta)\mathrel{\circ}\gamma\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,\mathsf{Path}(X,D))}

\displaystyle=\int^{D}_{s\in Y}f(\gamma(s))\nu(ds)\,.

This completes the proof that
f\in\underline{C\multimap D}
as contended.

Let
p\in\mathcal{M}^{C\multimap D}_{0}
.
Let
x\in\underline{C}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{D}_{0}
be such that
p=x\mathrel{\triangleright}m
, we have

\displaystyle p(f)

\displaystyle=m\Big(\int^{D}\eta(r)(x)\mu(dr)\Big)

\displaystyle=\int m(\eta(r)(x))\mu(dr)

\displaystyle=\int p(\eta(r))\mu(dr)\,,

so
\eta
is integrable over
\mu
, and

\int^{{C}\multimap{D}}\eta(r)\mu(dr)=f
.

This is the right place to insert a lemma very similar to
Lemma
5.3
which will be useful for exhibiting
the symmetry of our tensor product.

Lemma 5.7
.

There is an argument swapping natural isomorphism

\mathsf{sw}\in\mathbf{ICones}(B_{1}\multimap(B_{2}\multimap C),B_{2}\multimap(B_{1}\multimap C))

which maps
f
to
\boldsymbol{\lambda}x_{1}\in\underline{B_{1}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{2}\in\underline{B_{2}}\cdot f(x_{1})(x_{2})
.

Proof 5.8
.

One checks that

\mathsf{sw}\in\mathbf{MCones}(B_{1}\multimap(B_{2}\multimap C),B_{2}\multimap(B_{1}\multimap C))

as in the proof of
5.3
, and this morphism
preserves integrals because integrals are computed pointwise in

{D}\multimap{E}
for all integrable cones
D
and
E
and by the
Fubini theorem because all the considered measures are finite.

5.2.
Bilinear maps

After the linear function space
{C}\multimap{D}
that we have just
defined, the next concept deeply related to the tensor product is of
course the concept of bilinear map that we introduce now.

Definition 5.9
.

Let
C_{1},C_{2},D
be integrable cones, we define formally

\displaystyle{C_{1},C_{2}}\multimap{D}={C_{1}}\multimap{\left({C_{2}}\multimap{D}\right)}

and call this integrable cone
the cone of integrable bilinear and
continuous maps
C_{1},C_{2}\to D
.

Indeed, thanks to Lemma
2.23
, an
element of
\underline{{C_{1},C_{2}}\multimap{D}}
can be seen as a function
f:\underline{C_{1}}\mathrel{\&}\underline{C_{2}}\to\underline{D}
which is separately linear
and
\omega
-continuous.
Measurability of
f
is expressed equivalently by saying that given

(X_{i}\in\mathbf{Ar})_{i=1,2}
and
(\gamma_{i}\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X_{i},C_{i})})_{i=1,2}
the map
\boldsymbol{\lambda}(r_{1},r_{2})\in X_{1}\times X_{2}\cdot f(\gamma_{1}(r_{1}),\gamma_{2}(r_{2})):X_{1}\times X_{2}\to\underline{D}
is a measurable path
or that, given
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
and
(\gamma_{i}\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,C_{i})})_{i=1,2}
, the map
\boldsymbol{\lambda}r\in X\cdot f(\gamma_{1}(r),\gamma_{2}(r))
is a measurable path.
Preservation of integrals means that, given moreover
(\mu_{i}\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(X_{i})})_{i=1,2}
, we have

\displaystyle f\Big(\int^{C_{1}}\gamma_{1}(r_{1})\mu_{1}(dr_{1}),\int^{C_{2}}\gamma_{2}(r_{2})\mu_{2}(dr_{2})\Big)=\iint^{D}f(\gamma_{1}(r_{1}),\gamma_{2}(r_{2}))\mu_{1}(dr_{1})\mu_{2}(dr_{2})

where we can use the double integral symbol by
Theorem
4.24
.

Continuing to spell out the definition above of the integrable cone
{C_{1},C_{2}}\multimap{D}
, we see that, given
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
, an element of
\mathcal{M}^{{C_{1},C_{2}}\multimap{D}}_{X}
is a

\displaystyle\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\mathrel{\triangleright}m=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(r,f)\in X\times\underline{({C_{1},C_{2}}\multimap{D})}\cdot m(r,f(\gamma_{1}(r),\gamma_{2}(r))

where
(\gamma_{i}\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,C_{i})})_{i=1,2}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{D}_{X}
.
Last the integral of a measurable path
\eta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,({C_{1},C_{2}}\multimap{D}))}
over
\mu\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}
is characterized by

\displaystyle\Big(\int\eta(r)\mu(dr)\Big)(x_{1},x_{2})=\int\eta(r)(x_{1},x_{2})\mu(dr)\,.

5.3.
The linear hom functor

In order to define the tensor product as a left adjoint, we need to
consider the operation
{}\multimap{}
as an operation on morphisms of

\mathbf{ICones}
, not only on objects.
We define this operation and prove that it preserves all limits in its
second argument.

Definition 5.10
.

Let
g\in\mathbf{ICones}(D_{1},D_{2})
and
h\in\mathbf{ICones}(C_{2},C_{1})
.
The function

{h}\multimap{g}:\underline{{C_{1}}\multimap{D_{1}}}\to\underline{{C_{2}}\multimap{D_{2}}}
is
defined by
({h}\multimap{g})(f)=g\,f\,h
.

Proposition 5.11
.

If
g\in\mathbf{ICones}(D_{1},D_{2})
and
h\in\mathbf{ICones}(C_{2},C_{1})
then
{h}\multimap{g}\in\mathbf{ICones}({C_{1}}\multimap{D_{1}},{C_{2}}\multimap{D_{2}})
.

Proof 5.12
.

The linearity and continuity of
{h}\multimap{g}
result from the same
properties satisfied by
g
and
h
.
The fact that
\left\|{{h}\multimap{g}}\right\|\leq 1
results from the fact that
\left\|{g}\right\|,\left\|{h}\right\|\leq 1
, so let us check that
{h}\multimap{g}
is
measurable. Let
\eta_{1}\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,{C_{1}}\multimap{D_{1}})}
for some

X\in\mathbf{Ar}
.
We must prove that
({h}\multimap{g})\mathrel{\circ}\eta_{1}\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,{C_{2}}\multimap{D_{2}})}
so let
p\in\mathcal{M}^{{C_{2}}\multimap{D_{2}}}_{Y}
for some
Y\in\mathbf{Ar}
, we must
prove that

\displaystyle\varphi=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in Y\times X\cdot p(s,({h}\multimap{g})(\eta_{1}(r)))

is measurable.
Let
\gamma\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,C_{2})}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{D_{2}}_{Y}
be such that
p=\gamma\mathrel{\triangleright}m
.
For
s\in Y
and
r\in X
we have

\displaystyle\varphi(s,r)={m(s,g(\eta_{1}(r)(h(\gamma(s))))}={m(s,g(\delta_{1}(s)(r)))}={m(s,g(\mathsf{fl}(\delta_{1})(s,r)))}

where
\delta_{1}=\mathsf{sw}^{-1}(\eta_{1})\mathrel{\circ}h\mathrel{\circ}\gamma\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,\mathsf{Path}(X,D_{1}))}
by Lemma
5.3
and hence
g\mathrel{\circ}\mathsf{fl}(\delta_{1})\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y\times X,D_{2})}
by
Lemma
3.22
so that
\varphi
is measurable.
We need last to prove that
{h}\multimap{g}
preserves integrals so let moreover
\mu\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}
, we have

\displaystyle({h}\multimap{g})\Big(\int^{{C_{1}}\multimap{D_{1}}}\eta_{1}(r)\mu(dr)\Big)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}x\in\underline{C_{2}}\cdot g\Big(\Big(\int^{{C_{1}}\multimap{D_{1}}}\eta_{1}(r)\mu(dr)\Big)(h(x))\Big)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}x\in\underline{C_{2}}\cdot g\Big(\int^{D_{1}}\eta_{1}(r)(h(x))\mu(dr)\Big)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}x\in\underline{C_{2}}\cdot\int^{D_{2}}g\Big(\eta_{1}(r)(h(x))\Big)\mu(dr)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}x\in\underline{C_{2}}\cdot\int^{D_{2}}({h}\multimap{g})(\eta_{1}(r))(x)\mu(dr)

\displaystyle=\int^{{C_{2}}\multimap{D_{2}}}({h}\multimap{g})(\eta_{1}(r))\mu(dr)\,.

So we have defined a functor
{\_}\multimap{\_}:{\mathbf{ICones}}^{\mathsf{op}}\times\mathbf{ICones}\to\mathbf{ICones}
. We identify

{1}\multimap{\_}
with the identity functor: we make no distinction between
x\in\underline{C}
and the function
