\widehat{x}\in\underline{{1}\multimap{C}}
(this notation is introduced in the
proof of Theorem
4.30
).

Theorem 5.13
.

For each integrable cone
C
, the functor
{C}\multimap{\_}
has a
left adjoint.

Proof 5.14
.

By Theorem
4.32
it suffices to prove that
{C}\multimap{\_}
preserves all limits.

\blacktriangleright
Products.
Let
(D_{i})_{i\in I}
be a family of measurable cones and let
D=\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}D_{i}
as described in the proof of
Theorem
4.26
.
We have a morphism

\displaystyle k=\langle{{C}\multimap{\mathsf{pr}_{i}}}\rangle_{i\in I}\in\mathbf{ICones}({C}\multimap{D},\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}\left({C}\multimap{D_{i}}\right))

and we must prove that
k
is an iso.
It is clearly injective, to prove surjectivity, let
\overrightarrow{f}=(f_{i}\in\underline{{C}\multimap{D_{i}}})_{i\in I}\in\underline{\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}{\left({C}\multimap{D_{i}}\right)}}
so that
(\left\|{f_{i}}\right\|)_{i\in I}
is bounded in
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and hence for
each
x\in\underline{C}
the family
(\left\|{f_{i}(x)}\right\|)_{i\in I}
is
bounded.
So we can define a function
f:\underline{C}\to\underline{D}
by
f(x)=(f_{i}(x))_{i\in I}
.
This function is clearly linear and continuous.
To prove measurability, take
\gamma\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,C)}
for some
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
and
p\in\mathcal{M}^{D}_{Y}
for some
Y\in\mathbf{Ar}
.
This means that
p=\mathsf{in}_{i}(m)
for some
i\in I
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{D_{i}}_{Y}
.
Then
\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in Y\times X\cdot p(s,f(\gamma(r)))=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in Y\times X\cdot m(s,f_{i}(\gamma(r)))
is measurable because
f_{i}
is.
Last let moreover
\mu\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}
, we have

f(\int^{C}\gamma(r)\mu(dr))=\int^{D}f(\gamma(r))\mu(dr)
by
definition of
f
and of integration in
D
.
This shows that
f\in\underline{{C}\multimap{D}}
and hence that
k
is a bijection since

f_{i}=\mathsf{pr}_{i}\,f
for each
i\in I
and hence

\overrightarrow{f}=k(f)
.

We prove that
k^{-1}\in\mathbf{ICones}(\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}\left({C}\multimap{D_{i}}\right),{C}\multimap{D})
.
Linearity and continuity follow from the fact that all
operations are defined componentwise in
\underline{\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}}\left({C}\multimap{D_{i}}\right)
.
Next, given

\overrightarrow{f}\in\mathcal{B}(\underline{\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}}\left({C}\multimap{D_{i}}\right))
, we
have

\displaystyle\left\|{k^{-1}(\overrightarrow{f})}\right\|

\displaystyle=\sup_{x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{C}}\left\|{k^{-1}(\overrightarrow{f})(x)}\right\|

\displaystyle=\sup_{x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{C}}\sup_{i\in I}\left\|{f_{i}(x)}\right\|

\displaystyle=\sup_{i\in I}\sup_{x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{C}}\left\|{f_{i}(x)}\right\|

\displaystyle=\sup_{i\in I}\left\|{f_{i}}\right\|\leq 1\,.

Next we prove that
k^{-1}
is measurable so let
\eta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}\left({C}\multimap{D_{i}}\right))}
for some
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
, we must prove that
\eta^{\prime}=k^{-1}\mathrel{\circ}\eta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,{C}\multimap{D})}
. Notice that for all
r\in X
we can write
\eta(r)=(\eta_{i}(r))_{i\in I}
where
\eta_{i}=\mathsf{pr}_{i}\mathrel{\circ}\eta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,{C}\multimap{D_{i}})}
for
each
i\in I
.
Let
Y\in\mathbf{Ar}
,
\gamma\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,C)}
and
p\in\mathcal{M}^{D}_{Y}
, so that
p=\mathsf{in}_{i}(m)
for some
i\in I
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{D_{i}}_{Y}
.
We have
\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in Y\times X\cdot(\gamma\mathrel{\triangleright}p)(s,\eta^{\prime}(r))=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in Y\times X\cdot m_{i}(s,\eta_{i}(r))
which is measurable since each
\eta_{i}
is a measurable path.
Last let moreover
\mu\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}
, we must prove that
g_{1}=k^{-1}(\int^{\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}({C}\multimap{D_{i}})}\eta(r)\mu(dr))
and
g_{2}=\int^{{C}\multimap{D}}k^{-1}(\eta(r))\mu(dr)
are the same function. Let
x\in\underline{C}
, we have
g_{1}(x)=(\int^{D_{i}}\eta_{i}(r)(x)\mu(dr))_{i\in I}=g_{2}(x)
.
This ends the proof that
k
is an iso in
\mathbf{ICones}
and hence
that
{C}\multimap{\_}
preserves all products.

\blacktriangleright
Equalizers. Let
f,g\in\mathbf{ICones}(D_{1},D_{2})
and let
(E,e)
be the corresponding equalizer in
\mathbf{ICones}
, as
described in the proof of Theorem
4.26
.
Then we have
\left({C}\multimap{f}\right)\,\left({C}\multimap{e}\right)=\left({C}\multimap{g}\right)\,\left({C}\multimap{e}\right)
by
functoriality of
{C}\multimap{\_}
and it will be sufficient to prove
that
({C}\multimap{E},{C}\multimap{e})
has the universal property of an
equalizer. Let
H
be an integrable cone and
h\in\mathbf{ICones}(H,{C}\multimap{D_{1}})
be such that
\left({C}\multimap{f}\right)\,h=\left({C}\multimap{g}\right)\,h
.
Identifying
h
with its “uncurried” version
h^{\prime}\in\underline{{H,C}\multimap{D_{1}}}
, the integrable bilinear and
continuous map (see Section
5.2
) given by

h^{\prime}(z,x)=h(z)(x)
, we have
f\mathrel{\circ}h^{\prime}=g\mathrel{\circ}h^{\prime}
.
In other words
h^{\prime}
ranges in
\underline{E}\subseteq\underline{D_{1}}
, allowing to define
h^{\prime}_{0}\in\underline{{H,C}\multimap{E}}
which is the same function as

h^{\prime}
and is bilinear continuous and integrable by definition of

E
(which inherits the norm, the measurability and integrability
structure of
C
).
We use
h_{0}
for the corresponding element of
\mathbf{ICones}(H,{C}\multimap{E})
, so that
h=({C}\multimap{e})h_{0}
.
The fact that
h_{0}
is unique with this property results from the
fact that
{C}\multimap{e}
is a mono (it is actually the inclusion of
\underline{{C}\multimap{E}}
into
\underline{{C}\multimap{D_{1}}}
resulting from
the inclusion
e
of
\underline{E}
into
\underline{D_{1}}
). This shows that
({C}\multimap{E},{C}\multimap{e})
is the equalizer of
{C}\multimap{f}
and
{C}\multimap{g}
and ends the proof that
{C}\multimap{\_}
preserves all limits.

Lemma 5.15
.

Let
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
and let
B,C,D
be measurable cones. Let
f
be an element of
\mathbf{ICones}(B,\mathsf{Path}(X,{C}\multimap{D}))
.
Then
f^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(y,r,x)\in\underline{C}\times X\times\underline{B}\cdot f(x,r,y)
belongs to
\mathbf{ICones}(C,\mathsf{Path}(X,{B}\multimap{D}))
.

Proof 5.16
.

This results from the following sequence of isos in
\mathbf{ICones}
:

\displaystyle{B}\multimap{\mathsf{Path}(X,{C}\multimap{D})}

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt\simeq{B}\multimap{\left({C}\multimap{\mathsf{Path}(X,D)}\right)}\quad\text{by Lemma~\ref{lemma:swap-lin-path}
and functoriality~of }{C}\multimap{\_}

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt={B,C}\multimap{\mathsf{Path}(X,D)}

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt\simeq{C,B}\multimap{\mathsf{Path}(X,D)}\text{\hskip 10.22217ptby Section~\ref{sec:bilin-measurable}.}

5.4.
The tensor product of integrable cones

Let
C
be an integrable cone. We denote by
{\_}\mathop{\otimes}{C}
the left adjoint of the functor
{C}\multimap{\_}
, see Theorem
5.13
.
Because
{\_}\multimap{\_}
is a functor
{\mathbf{ICones}}^{\mathsf{op}}\times\mathbf{ICones}\to\mathbf{ICones}
(see
Section
5.3
), we know by the adjunction with a
parameter theorem (
[
Mac71
]
, Chapter IV, Section 7,
Theorem 3), that the so defined operation
19
19

19

According to one of the reviewers of this paper, this tensor
product can be understood as the adaptation to the setting of
integrable cones of the standard projective tensor product of
locally convex spaces.

\mathop{\otimes}
can uniquely be extended in a bifunctor
\mathop{\otimes}:\mathbf{ICones}^{2}\to\mathbf{ICones}
in such a way that the bijection

\displaystyle\Phi_{B,C,D}:\mathbf{ICones}({B}\mathop{\otimes}{C},D)\to\mathbf{ICones}(B,{C}\multimap{D})

given by the adjunction for each
C
is natural in
B,C,D
.
We define

\displaystyle\tau_{B,C}=\Phi_{B,C,{B}\mathop{\otimes}{C}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Id}}_{{B}\mathop{\otimes}{C}})\in\mathbf{ICones}(B,{C}\multimap{{B}\mathop{\otimes}{C}})=\mathcal{B}(\underline{{B,C}\multimap{{B}\mathop{\otimes}{C}}})

and, for
x\in\underline{B}
and
y\in\underline{C}
we use the notation
{x}\mathop{\otimes}{y}=\tau_{B,C}(x,y)
.
By naturality of
\Phi
we have that, for each

f\in\underline{{{B}\mathop{\otimes}{C}}\multimap{D}}
,

\displaystyle\Phi_{B,C,D}(f)=f\mathrel{\circ}\tau_{B,C}.

(1)

The next lemma is the key observation for proving that the above
bijection is a cone isomorphism.

Lemma 5.17
.

Let
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
and
B,C
be integrable cones. Let
\eta:X\to\underline{{{B}\mathop{\otimes}{C}}\multimap{1}}
be a function.
One has
\eta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,{{B}\mathop{\otimes}{C}}\multimap{1})}
as soon as

•

\eta(X)\subseteq\underline{{{B}\mathop{\otimes}{C}}\multimap{1}}
is bounded

•

and for all
Y\in\mathbf{Ar}
,
\beta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,B)}
and
\gamma\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,C)}
,
the function
\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in Y\times X\cdot\eta(r)({\beta(s)}\mathop{\otimes}{\gamma(s)}):Y\times X\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is measurable.

Proof 5.18
.

Let
\eta^{\prime}:\underline{B}\times\underline{C}\times X\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be defined
by
\eta^{\prime}(x,y,r)=\eta(r)({x}\mathop{\otimes}{y})
.
We have
\eta^{\prime}\in\mathbf{ICones}(B,{C}\multimap{\mathsf{Path}(X,1)})
by our
assumptions.
Let us check that
\eta^{\prime}
indeed preserves integrals.
Let
Y,Z\in\mathbf{Ar}
,
\mu\in\mathsf{FMeas}(Y)
,

\nu\in\mathsf{FMeas}(Z)
,
\beta\in\mathsf{Path}(Y,B)
,

\gamma\in\mathsf{Path}(Z,C)
, and

m=\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{Path}(X,1)}_{0}
(i.e. there is

r\in X
such that
m(\xi)=\xi(r)
for all

\xi\in\mathsf{Path}(X,1)
).
Then

\displaystyle m\Big(\eta^{\prime}\Big(\int\beta(s)\mu(ds),

\displaystyle\int\gamma(t)\nu(dt)\Big)\Big)=\eta^{\prime}\Big(\int\beta(s)\mu(ds),\int\gamma(t)\nu(dt)\Big)(r)

\displaystyle=\eta(r)\Big({\Big(\int\beta(s)\mu(ds)\Big)}\mathop{\otimes}{\Big(\int\gamma(t)\nu(dt)\Big)}\Big)

\displaystyle=\iint\eta(r)({\beta(s)}\mathop{\otimes}{\gamma(t)})\mu(ds)\nu(dt)\text{\hskip 10.22217ptsince }\tau\text{ preserves integrals}

\displaystyle=\iint m(\eta^{\prime}(\beta(s),\gamma(t)))\mu(ds)\nu(dt)

\displaystyle=m\Big(\iint\eta^{\prime}(\beta(s),\gamma(t))\mu(ds)\nu(dt)\Big)\,.

Let

\eta^{\prime\prime}=\Phi_{B,C,\mathsf{Path}(X,1)}^{-1}(\eta^{\prime})\in\mathbf{ICones}({B}\mathop{\otimes}{C},\mathsf{Path}(X,1))=\mathbf{ICones}({B}\mathop{\otimes}{C},\mathsf{Path}(X,{1}\multimap{1}))

up to a trivial
\mathbf{ICones}
iso and so by Lemma
5.15
there is a
h\in\mathbf{ICones}(1,\mathsf{Path}(X,{{B}\mathop{\otimes}{C}}\multimap{1}))
such that
h(1)(r)(z)=\eta^{\prime\prime}(z)(r)
for all
z\in\underline{{B}\mathop{\otimes}{C}}
and
r\in X
.
So we have
h(1)(r)({x}\mathop{\otimes}{y})=\eta(r)({x}\mathop{\otimes}{y})
and hence
\eta(r)=h(1)(r)
since both are elements of
\underline{{{B}\mathop{\otimes}{C}}\multimap{1}}
.
Since this holds for all
r\in X
we have proven that
\eta=h(1)
and hence
\eta\in\mathsf{Path}(X,{{B}\mathop{\otimes}{C}}\multimap{1})

as contended.

Now we can prove the main property of our tensor product which will
allow us to prove that it has a structure of monoidal product on

\mathbf{ICones}
.

Theorem 5.19
.

For each integrable cones
B,C,D
, the function
\Phi_{B,C,D}
is an isomorphism of integrable cones from
{{B}\mathop{\otimes}{C}}\multimap{D}
to
{B}\multimap{\left({C}\multimap{D}\right)}=({B,C}\multimap{D})
.

Proof 5.20
.

By linearity and
\omega
-continuity of composition on the left, the
function
\Phi_{B,C,D}
— characterized
by (
1
) — is a linear and continuous map
\Phi_{B,C,D}:\underline{{{B}\mathop{\otimes}{C}}\multimap{D}}\to\underline{{B,C}\multimap{D}}
which satisfies
\left\|{\Phi_{B,C,D}(f)}\right\|\leq\left\|{f}\right\|
for all
f\in\underline{{{B}\mathop{\otimes}{C}}\multimap{D}}
.
This latter property is due to the fact that if

f\in\underline{{{B}\mathop{\otimes}{C}}\multimap{D}}
satisfies
\left\|{f}\right\|\leq 1

then
f\in\mathbf{ICones}({B}\mathop{\otimes}{C},D)
and hence
\Phi_{B,C,D}(f)\in\mathbf{ICones}(B,{C}\multimap{D})
so that
\left\|{\Phi_{B,C,D}(f)}\right\|\leq 1
and hence for an arbitrary
f\in\underline{{{B}\mathop{\otimes}{C}}\multimap{D}}
such that
f\not=0
we have
\left\|{(1/\left\|{f}\right\|)f}\right\|\leq 1
and hence
\left\|{\Phi_{B,C,D}((1/\left\|{f}\right\|)f)}\right\|\leq 1
which is exactly our
contention, which trivially also holds when
f=0
.

Let us prove that
\Phi_{B,C,D}

is measurable, so let
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
and
\eta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,{{B}\mathop{\otimes}{C}}\multimap{D})}
, we must prove that
\Phi_{B,C,D}\mathrel{\circ}\eta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,({B,C}\multimap{D}))}
.
So let
Y\in\mathbf{Ar}
and
p\in\mathcal{M}^{{B,C}\multimap{D}}_{Y}
, which means that
p=\beta,\gamma\mathrel{\triangleright}m
for some
\beta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,B)}
,

\gamma\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,C)}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{D}_{Y}
.
We have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in Y\times X\cdot p(s,\Phi_{B,C,D}(\eta(r)))

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in Y\times X\cdot p(s,\eta(r)\mathrel{\circ}\tau_{B,C})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in Y\times X\cdot m(s,\eta(r)({\beta(s)}\mathop{\otimes}{\gamma(s)}))

which is measurable because
{\beta}\mathop{\otimes}{\gamma}\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,{B}\mathop{\otimes}{C})}
(defining

{\beta}\mathop{\otimes}{\gamma}
by
({\beta}\mathop{\otimes}{\gamma})(s)={\beta(s)}\mathop{\otimes}{\gamma(s)}
) by measurability of
\tau
, and by our assumption that
\eta

is a measurable path.
Altogether we have proven that

\displaystyle\Phi_{B,C,D}\in\mathbf{MCones}({({{B}\mathop{\otimes}{C}}\multimap{D})},{({B,C}\multimap{D})})

and we prove now that this morphism preserves integrals, so let
moreover
\mu\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}
, we have

\displaystyle\Phi_{B,C,D}\Big(\int\eta(r)\mu(dr)\Big)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(x,y)\in\underline{B}\times\underline{C}\cdot\Big(\int\eta(r)\mu(dr)\Big)({x}\mathop{\otimes}{y})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(x,y)\in\underline{B}\times\underline{C}\cdot\Big(\int\eta(r)({x}\mathop{\otimes}{y})\mu(dr)\Big)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(x,y)\in\underline{B}\times\underline{C}\cdot\Big(\int\Phi_{B,C,D}(\eta(r))(x,y)\mu(dr)\Big)

\displaystyle=\int\Phi_{B,C,D}(\eta(r))\mu(dr)\,.

This shows that
\Phi_{B,C,D}\in\mathbf{ICones}({({{B}\mathop{\otimes}{C}}\multimap{D})},{({B,C}\multimap{D})})

and we show now that this morphism is an iso.

We know that this function is bijective, let us use
\Psi_{B,C,D}
for its inverse, which is linear and continuous by
Lemma
2.8
.
Since
\Psi_{B,C,D}:\mathbf{ICones}(B,{C}\multimap{D})\to\mathbf{ICones}({B}\mathop{\otimes}{C},D)
, we have
\left\|{\Psi_{B,C,D}(g)}\right\|\leq\left\|{g}\right\|
for all
g\in\underline{B,{C}\multimap{D}}
, using also the linearity of

\Psi_{B,C,D}
.
We prove that
\Psi_{B,C,D}
is measurable.
Let
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
and
\eta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,({B,C}\multimap{D}))}
, we must prove that
\Psi_{B,C,D}\mathrel{\circ}\eta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,\left({{B}\mathop{\otimes}{C}}\multimap{D}\right))}
. Without loss of generality we assume that
\left\|{\eta}\right\|\leq 1
. Let
Y\in\mathbf{Ar}
and
p\in\mathcal{M}^{{{B}\mathop{\otimes}{C}}\multimap{D}}_{Y}
, we must check that
\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in Y\times X\cdot p(s,\Psi_{B,C,D}(\eta(r)))
is measurable.
There is
\theta\in\mathsf{Path}(Y,{B}\mathop{\otimes}{C})
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{D}_{Y}
such that
p=\theta\mathrel{\triangleright}m
, and we must check that
\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in Y\times X\cdot m(s,\Psi_{B,C,D}(\eta(r))(\theta(s)))
is measurable. For this, since
\mathbf{Ar}
is cartesian, it suffices
to prove that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,s^{\prime},r)\in Y\times Y\times X\cdot m(s,\Psi_{B,C,D}(\eta(r))(\theta(s^{\prime})))

is measurable.
Since
\theta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,{B}\mathop{\otimes}{C})}
it suffices to prove that

\displaystyle\eta^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r,z)\in Y\times X\times\underline{{B}\mathop{\otimes}{C}}\cdot m(s,\Psi_{B,C,D}(\eta(r))(z))\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y\times X,{{B}\mathop{\otimes}{C}}\multimap{1})}

and to this end we apply Lemma
5.17
.
The boundedness assumption is satisfied because
\left\|{\eta}\right\|\leq 1
and hence
\left\|{\Psi_{B,C,D}(\eta(r))}\right\|\leq 1
for each
r\in X
.
So let
Y^{\prime}\in\mathbf{Ar}
,
\beta\in\mathsf{Path}(Y^{\prime},B)
and
\gamma\in\mathsf{Path}(Y^{\prime},C)
.
We have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s^{\prime},s,r)\in Y^{\prime}\times Y\times X\cdot\eta^{\prime}(s,r)({\beta(s^{\prime})}\mathop{\otimes}{\gamma(s^{\prime})})

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s^{\prime},s,r)\in Y^{\prime}\times Y\times X\cdot m(s,\Psi_{B,C,D}(\eta(r))(\beta(s^{\prime})\mathop{\otimes}\gamma(s^{\prime})))

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s^{\prime},s,r)\in Y^{\prime}\times Y\times X\cdot m(s,\eta(r)(\beta(s^{\prime}),\gamma(s^{\prime})))

which is measurable by our assumption about
\eta
.
Last we must prove that
\Psi_{B,C,D}
preserves integrals.
Using the same path
\eta
let furthermore
\mu\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}
, we must prove that

\displaystyle g_{1}

\displaystyle=\Psi_{B,C,D}\Big(\int\eta(r)\mu(dr)\Big)\in\underline{{{B}\mathop{\otimes}{C}}\multimap{D}}

\displaystyle\text{and }g_{2}

\displaystyle=\int\Psi_{B,C,D}(\eta(r))\mu(dr)\in\underline{{{B}\mathop{\otimes}{C}}\multimap{D}}

are equal.
Since
\Phi_{B,C,D}
preserves integrals we have
\Phi_{B,C,D}(g_{1})=\Phi_{B,C,D}(g_{2})
and the required property follows from the injectivity of
\Phi_{B,C,D}
.

Theorem 5.21
.

For each
x\in\underline{B}
and
y\in\underline{C}
we have
\left\|{{x}\mathop{\otimes}{y}}\right\|=\left\|{x}\right\|\left\|{y}\right\|
.

Proof 5.22
.

Since
\tau_{B,C}\in\mathbf{ICones}(B,{C}\multimap{{B}\mathop{\otimes}{C}})
we have
\left\|{{x}\mathop{\otimes}{y}}\right\|\leq\left\|{x}\right\|\left\|{y}\right\|
, we just have to prove the
converse.
If
x=0
or
y=0
our contention trivially holds so we can
assume without loss of generality that
\left\|{x}\right\|=\left\|{y}\right\|=1
and let

\varepsilon>0
.
By Proposition
3.12
there is

x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}{\underline{B}}^{\prime}
and
y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}{\underline{C}}^{\prime}
such
that
\langle x,x^{\prime}\rangle\geq 1-\varepsilon/2
and

\langle y,y^{\prime}\rangle\geq 1-\varepsilon/2
.
Let
g:\underline{B}\times\underline{C}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be defined by
g(x_{0},y_{0})=\langle x_{0},x^{\prime}\rangle\langle y_{0},y^{\prime}\rangle
.
Then

g\in\underline{{B,C}\multimap{1}}
and moreover
\left\|{g}\right\|\leq 1
.
Let
z^{\prime}=\Phi_{B,C,1}^{-1}(g)\in\mathcal{B}{(\underline{{B}\mathop{\otimes}{C}})}^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|{{x}\mathop{\otimes}{y}}\right\|

\displaystyle\geq\langle{x}\mathop{\otimes}{y},z^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle x,x^{\prime}\rangle\langle y,y^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle\geq\big(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\big)^{2}>1-\varepsilon

so that
\left\|{{x}\mathop{\otimes}{y}}\right\|\geq 1
.

5.5.
The symmetric monoidal structure of
\mathbf{ICones}

We want now to exploit Theorem
5.19
to show
that the category
\mathbf{ICones}
can be endowed with a symmetric monoidal
structure whose monoidal functor is our tensor product
\mathop{\otimes}
.

One very convenient tool for proving the associated coherence diagrams
will be Proposition
5.23
which uses binary
trees given by the following syntax:
\ast
is a tree (a leaf)
and if
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
are trees then
\langle t_{1},t_{2}\rangle
is a
tree.
We use
\mathcal{T}_{n}
for the set of trees which have
n
leaves (for

n\in{{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}
).

These trees are used to specify arbitrary “tensor expressions” as
follows.
If
n\in{{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}
,
\overrightarrow{B}=(B_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
is a sequence of objects
of
\mathbf{ICones}
and
t\in\mathcal{T}_{n}
, we define an object

t^{\otimes}(\overrightarrow{B})
of
\mathbf{ICones}
by a straightforward induction,
for instance

\langle\ast,\langle\ast,\ast\rangle\rangle^{\otimes}(B_{1},B_{2},B_{3})={B_{1}}\mathop{\otimes}{({B_{1}}\mathop{\otimes}{B_{2}})}
.
In the same way, given
\overrightarrow{x}=(x_{i}\in\underline{B_{i}})_{i=1}^{n}
one
defines
t^{\otimes}(\overrightarrow{x})\in\underline{t^{\otimes}(\overrightarrow{B})}
, for
instance

\langle\ast,\langle\ast,\ast\rangle\rangle^{\otimes}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})={x_{1}}\mathop{\otimes}{({x_{1}}\mathop{\otimes}{x_{2}})}
.

Proposition 5.23
.

Let
n\in{{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}
,
B_{1},\dots,B_{n},C
be integrable cones and

t\in\mathcal{T}_{n}
.
Let
f,g\in\mathbf{ICones}(t^{\otimes}(\overrightarrow{B}),C)
.
If, for all
(x_{i}\in\underline{B_{i}})_{i=1}^{n}
one has
f(t^{\otimes}(\overrightarrow{x}))=g(t^{\otimes}(\overrightarrow{x}))
, then
f=g
.

Proof 5.24
.

By induction on the structure of
t
.
The base case
t=\ast
being trivial, assume that

t=\langle t_{1},t_{2}\rangle
with
(t_{i}\in\mathcal{T}_{n_{i}})_{i=1,2}
and

n_{1}+n_{2}=n
(notice that
n_{1},n_{2}<n
) so that we can write
\overrightarrow{B}=(B_{1}^{1},\dots,B_{n_{1}}^{1},B_{1}^{2},\dots,B_{n_{2}}^{2})=(\overrightarrow{B^{1}},\overrightarrow{B^{2}})

and we have
t^{\otimes}{(\overrightarrow{B})}={D_{1}}\mathop{\otimes}{D_{2}}
where
(D_{i}=t_{i}^{\otimes}{(\overrightarrow{B^{i}})})_{i=1,2}
.
With these notations, we have
f,g\in\mathbf{ICones}({D_{1}}\mathop{\otimes}{D_{2}},C)
and so it suffices to prove that
\Phi_{D_{1},D_{2},C}(f)=\Phi_{D_{1},D_{2},C}(g)\in\mathbf{ICones}(D_{1},{D_{2}}\multimap{C})
.
By inductive hypothesis, it suffices to prove that for all
\overrightarrow{x^{1}}=(x^{1}_{i}\in\underline{B^{1}_{i}})_{i=1}^{n_{1}}
, one has
\Phi_{D_{1},D_{2},C}(f)(t_{1}^{\otimes}(\overrightarrow{x^{1}}))=\Phi_{D_{1},D_{2},C}(g)(t_{1}^{\otimes}(\overrightarrow{x^{1}}))\in\mathbf{ICones}(B_{2},C)

(the fact that both morphisms have norm
\leq 1
is true but not
essential), and so, by inductive hypothesis again, it suffices to
prove that for all
\overrightarrow{x^{2}}=(x^{2}_{i}\in\underline{B^{2}_{i}})_{i=1}^{n_{2}}
, one has
\Phi_{D_{1},D_{2},C}(f)(t_{1}^{\otimes}(\overrightarrow{x^{1}}))(t_{2}^{\otimes}(\overrightarrow{x^{2}}))=\Phi_{D_{1},D_{2},C}(g)(t_{1}^{\otimes}(\overrightarrow{x^{1}}))(t_{2}^{\otimes}(\overrightarrow{x^{2}}))\in\underline{C}
which results from
\Phi_{D_{1},D_{2},C}(f)(t_{1}^{\otimes}(\overrightarrow{x^{1}}))(t_{2}^{\otimes}(\overrightarrow{x^{2}}))=f({t_{1}^{\otimes}(\overrightarrow{x^{1}})}\mathop{\otimes}{t_{2}^{\otimes}(\overrightarrow{x^{2}})})=f(t^{\otimes}(\overrightarrow{x}))
and similarly for
g
, and from our
assumption about
f
and
g
.

Theorem 5.25
.

The category
\mathbf{ICones}
, equipped with the bifunctor
\mathop{\otimes}
and
unit
1
has a structure of symmetric monoidal category, and
this SMC is closed.

Proof 5.26
.

Let us deal first with the associator.
We have two natural bijections
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{ICones}({({B_{1}}\mathop{\otimes}{B_{2}})}\mathop{\otimes}{B_{3}},C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{ICones}({B_{1}}\mathop{\otimes}{B_{2}},{B_{3}}\multimap{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{ICones}(B_{1},{B_{2}}\multimap{\left({B_{3}}\multimap{C}\right)})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{{B_{1}}\mathop{\otimes}{B_{2}},B_{3},C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{B_{1},B_{2},{B_{3}}\multimap{C}}}

and — notice that here we use Theorem
5.19

in a crucial way —
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{ICones}({B_{1}}\mathop{\otimes}{({B_{2}}\mathop{\otimes}{B_{3}})},C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{ICones}(B_{1},{{B_{2}}\mathop{\otimes}{B_{3}}}\multimap{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{ICones}(B_{1},{B_{2}}\multimap{\left({B_{3}}\multimap{C}\right)})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{B_{1},{B_{2}}\mathop{\otimes}{B_{3}},C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{ICones}(B_{1}{,}\Phi_{B_{2},B_{3},C})}

that we call respectively
\Psi_{B_{1},B_{2},B_{3},C}
and

\Psi^{\prime}_{B_{1},B_{2},B_{3},C}
so that
({\Psi_{B_{1},B_{2},B_{3},C}})^{-1}\mathrel{\circ}\Psi^{\prime}_{B_{1},B_{2},B_{3},C}
is a
natural bijection
\mathbf{ICones}({B_{1}}\mathop{\otimes}{({B_{2}}\mathop{\otimes}{B_{3}})},C)\to\mathbf{ICones}({({B_{1}}\mathop{\otimes}{B_{2}})}\mathop{\otimes}{B_{3}},C)
and hence, setting

C={B_{1}}\mathop{\otimes}{({B_{2}}\mathop{\otimes}{B_{3}})}
, we know by
Lemma
1.1
that

\displaystyle\alpha_{B_{1},B_{2},B_{3}}=({\Psi_{B_{1},B_{2},B_{3},C}})^{-1}(\Psi^{\prime}_{B_{1},B_{2},B_{3},C}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Id}}_{C}))\in\mathbf{ICones}({({B_{1}}\mathop{\otimes}{B_{2}})}\mathop{\otimes}{B_{3}},{B_{1}}\mathop{\otimes}{({B_{2}}\mathop{\otimes}{B_{3}})})

is a natural iso.
Moreover the definition of the natural iso
\Phi
implies that for
all
x_{1}\in\underline{B_{1}}
,
x_{2}\in\underline{B_{2}}
and

x_{3}\in\underline{B_{3}}
, one has

\displaystyle\alpha_{B_{1},B_{2},B_{3}}({({x_{1}}\mathop{\otimes}{x_{2}})}\mathop{\otimes}{x_{3}})={x_{1}}\mathop{\otimes}{({x_{2}}\mathop{\otimes}{x_{3}})}

(2)

Indeed
\Psi^{\prime}_{B_{1},B_{2},B_{3},{B_{1}}\mathop{\otimes}{({B_{2}}\mathop{\otimes}{B_{3}})}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Id}}_{C})

is

\displaystyle f=\boldsymbol{\lambda}x_{1}\in\underline{B_{1}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\lambda}x_{2}\in\underline{B_{2}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\lambda}x_{3}\in\underline{B_{3}}\cdot{x_{1}}\mathop{\otimes}{({x_{2}}\mathop{\otimes}{x_{3}})}

and

\alpha_{B_{1},B_{2},B_{3}}

must satisfy
\Psi_{B_{1},B_{2},B_{3},C}(\alpha_{B_{1},B_{2},B_{3}})=f
which
is exactly Equation (
2
).

Similarly one defines natural isos

\lambda_{B}\in\mathbf{ICones}({1}\mathop{\otimes}{B},B)
(using the obvious natural
bijection
\mathbf{ICones}(1,{B}\multimap{C})\to\mathbf{ICones}(B,C)
),
\rho_{B}\in\mathbf{ICones}({B}\mathop{\otimes}{1},C)
(using the obvious natural
iso
({1}\multimap{C})\to C
in
\mathbf{ICones}
) and
\gamma_{B_{1},B_{2}}\in\mathbf{ICones}({B_{1}}\mathop{\otimes}{B_{2}},{B_{2}}\mathop{\otimes}{B_{1}}))

(using the natural iso of Lemma
5.7
).
These isos satisfy the following equations

\displaystyle\forall x\in\underline{B},\,\forall u\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\quad

\displaystyle\lambda_{B}({u}\mathop{\otimes}{x})=ux=\rho_{B}({x}\mathop{\otimes}{u})

(3)

\displaystyle\forall x_{1}\in\underline{B_{1}},\,\forall x_{2}\in\underline{B_{2}}\quad

\displaystyle\gamma_{B_{1},B_{2}}({x_{1}}\mathop{\otimes}{x_{2}})={x_{2}}\mathop{\otimes}{x_{1}}\,.

(4)

The required coherence diagrams are easily proven using
Equations (
2
),
(
3
) and (
4
)
combined with Proposition
5.23
.
In that way, we have endowed
\mathbf{ICones}
with an SMC structure whose
monoidal product is our tensor product
\mathop{\otimes}
.
The natural isomorphism
\Phi
tells us moreover that this SMC is
closed.

6.
Categorical properties of integration

From now on all the cones we consider are integrable cones, unless
otherwise specified.
We use letters
B
,
C
,
D
and
E
, possibly with
subscripts, to denote such cones.

In Lemma
4.16
we have defined the
functor
\mathsf{FMeas}:\mathbf{Ar}\to\mathbf{ICones}
which maps each
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
to
the integrable cone
\mathsf{FMeas}(X)
of finite non-negative measures
on
X
and acts on measurable functions by the standard
push-forward operation,
\mathsf{FMeas}(\varphi)={\varphi}_{\ast}
.

Notice that for each
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
we have a specific element
\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,\mathsf{FMeas}(X))}
such that
\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r)
is the Dirac mass at
r\in X
, the measure
defined by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r)(U)=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }r\in U\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

The boundedness of
\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}
is obvious and its measurability
results from the observation that if
m=\widetilde{U}
(for some

U\in\sigma_{X}
) we have
m\mathrel{\circ}\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}=\chi_{U}
(the
characteristic function of
U
) which is measurable.

Theorem 6.1
.

For each
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
and integrable cone
B
, one has

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{B}_{X}\in\mathbf{ICones}(\mathsf{Path}(X,B),{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}\multimap{B})

and
\mathcal{I}^{B}_{X}
(this notation is introduced in
Definition
4.4
) is an isomorphism which is
natural in
X
and in
B
(between functors
{\mathbf{Ar}}^{\mathsf{op}}\times\mathbf{ICones}\to\mathbf{ICones}
).

This means that
\mathcal{I}^{B}_{X}
is bilinear continuous and
measurable, and preserves integrals on both sides, and that,
considered as a linear morphism acting on
\mathsf{Path}(X,B)
, it is an iso
in
\mathbf{ICones}
.

Proof 6.2
.

For the first statement we just have to prove preservation of
integrals in both arguments since bilinearity, continuity and
measurability have already been proven in
Lemma
4.10
.
So let
Y\in\mathbf{Ar}
,
\nu\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(Y)}
,
\eta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,\mathsf{Path}(X,B))}
and
\mu\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{B}_{X}\Big({\int^{\mathsf{Path}(X,B)}_{s\in Y}\eta(s)\nu(ds)},{\mu}\Big)

\displaystyle=\int^{B}_{r\in X}\Big(\int^{\mathsf{Path}(X,B)}_{s\in Y}\eta(s)\nu(ds)\Big)(r)\mu(dr)

\displaystyle=\int^{B}_{r\in X}\Big(\int^{B}_{s\in Y}\eta(s)(r)\nu(ds)\Big)\mu(dr)

\displaystyle=\int^{B}_{s\in Y}\Big(\int^{B}_{r\in X}\eta(s)(r)\mu(dr)\Big)\nu(ds)\text{\hskip 10.22217ptby Theorem~\ref{th:paths-Fubini}}

\displaystyle=\int\mathcal{I}^{B}_{X}(\eta(s),\mu)\nu(ds)\,.

Next let
\beta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,B)}
and
\kappa\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,\mathsf{FMeas}(X))}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{B}_{X}\Big(\beta,\int^{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}_{s\in Y}\kappa(s)\nu(ds)\Big)

\displaystyle=\int^{B}_{r\in X}\beta(r)\Big(\int^{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}_{s\in Y}\kappa(s)\nu(ds)\Big)(dr)

where one should remember that the value of the integral
\int\kappa(s)\nu(ds)
is the finite measure on
X
which maps
U\in\sigma_{X}
to
\int\kappa(s,U)\nu(ds)\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. We claim that
x_{1}=x_{2}
where

\displaystyle x_{1}=\int^{B}_{r\in X}\beta(r)\Big(\int^{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}_{s\in Y}\kappa(s)\nu(ds)\Big)(dr)\hskip 40.00006ptx_{2}=\int^{B}_{s\in Y}\Big(\int^{B}_{r\in X}\beta(r)\kappa(s,dr)\Big)\nu(ds)\,.

Upon applying to both members an element of
\mathcal{M}^{B}_{0}

and using
Definition 3.2
.
for
B
we can assume that
B=1
.
By the monotone convergence theorem and the fact that each measurable
function is the lub of a monotone sequence of simple measurable
functions, we can assume that
\beta
is simple, and by linearity
of integrals we can assume that
\beta=\chi_{U}
for some

U\in\sigma_{X}
.
Then we have
x_{1}=\int\kappa(s,U)\nu(ds)=x_{2}
.

Now we define a function
\mathcal{K}^{B}_{X}:\underline{{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}\multimap{B}}\to\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,B)}
by
setting

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{B}_{X}(f)=f\mathrel{\circ}\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}\,,

which belongs indeed to
\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,B)}
because
\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}
is
a bounded measurable path.
Linearity and continuity of
\mathcal{K}^{B}_{X}
result from linearity
and continuity of composition.
We prove measurability so let
Y\in\mathbf{Ar}
and
\eta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}\multimap{B})}
, we contend that
\mathcal{K}^{B}_{X}\mathrel{\circ}\eta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,\mathsf{Path}(X,B))}
.
So let
Y^{\prime}\in\mathbf{Ar}
and let
p\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{Path}(X,B)}_{Y^{\prime}}
, we
must prove that

\displaystyle\psi=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s^{\prime},s)\in Y^{\prime}\times Y\cdot p(s^{\prime},\mathcal{K}^{B}_{X}(\eta(s)))

is measurable.
Let
\varphi\in\mathbf{Ar}(Y^{\prime},X)
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{B}_{Y^{\prime}}
be such that
p=\varphi\mathrel{\triangleright}m
, we have

\displaystyle\psi

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s^{\prime},s)\in Y^{\prime}\times Y\cdot m(s^{\prime},\mathcal{K}^{B}_{X}(\eta(s))(\varphi(s^{\prime})))

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s^{\prime},s)\in Y^{\prime}\times Y\cdot m(s^{\prime},\eta(s)(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(\varphi(s^{\prime}))))

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s^{\prime},s)\in Y^{\prime}\times Y\cdot(((\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}\mathrel{\circ}\varphi)\mathrel{\triangleright}m)(s^{\prime},\eta(s))

which is measurable since
(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}\mathrel{\circ}\varphi)\mathrel{\triangleright}m\in\mathcal{M}^{{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}\multimap{B}}_{Y^{\prime}}
and
\eta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}\multimap{B})}
.

We prove that
\mathcal{K}^{B}_{X}
preserves integrals so let moreover
\nu\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(Y)}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{B}_{X}\Big(\int^{{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}\multimap{B}}_{s\in Y}\eta(s)\nu(ds)\Big)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}r\in X\cdot\int^{B}_{s\in Y}\eta(s)(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r))\nu(ds)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}r\in X\cdot\int^{B}_{s\in Y}\mathcal{K}^{B}_{X}(\eta(s))(r)\nu(ds)

\displaystyle=\int^{\mathsf{Path}(X,B)}_{s\in Y}\mathcal{K}^{B}_{X}(\eta(s))\nu(ds)

so that

\mathcal{K}^{B}_{X}\in\mathbf{ICones}({\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}\multimap{B},\mathsf{Path}(X,B))
.

Let
f\in\underline{{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}\multimap{B}}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{B}_{X}(\mathcal{K}^{B}_{X}(f))

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}\mu\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}\cdot\int^{B}_{r\in X}f(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r))\mu(dr)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}\mu\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}\cdot f\Big(\int^{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}_{r\in X}\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r)\mu(dr)\Big)\text{\hskip 10.22217ptsince }f\text{ preserves integrals}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}\mu\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}\cdot f(\mu)=f

and let
\beta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,B)}
, we have

\mathcal{K}^{B}_{X}(\mathcal{I}^{B}_{r\in X}(\beta))=\boldsymbol{\lambda}r\in X\cdot\Big(\int^{B}_{r^{\prime}\in X}\beta(r^{\prime})\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r,dr^{\prime})\Big)=\beta\,.

Checking naturality is routine.

Theorem 6.3
.

Let
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
,
B
be an object of
\mathbf{ICones}
and

f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathbf{ICones}(\mathsf{FMeas}(X),B)
.
If, for all
r\in X
, one has

f_{1}(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r))=f_{2}(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r))
then
f_{1}=f_{2}
.

Proof 6.4
.

This results from Theorem
6.1
.

Remark 6.5
.

In other words the Dirac measures
\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r)
are “dense” in
the integrable cone
\mathsf{FMeas}(X)
, in the sense that two
\mathbf{ICones}

morphisms which take the same values on Dirac measures are equal.
This property is one of the main benefits of integrability of cones
and it does not hold in
\mathbf{MCones}
as shown
in Remark
2.7
.

It is easy to check that for each
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
the functor
\mathsf{Path}(X,\_):\mathbf{ICones}\to\mathbf{ICones}
preserves all limits.
It follows by Theorem
4.32
that it has a
left adjoint.
We provide an explicit description of this adjoint.
We define the functor
\mathsf{FMeas}^{\mathord{\otimes}}:\mathbf{Ar}\times\mathbf{ICones}\to\mathbf{ICones}
by
\mathsf{FMeas}^{\mathord{\otimes}}(X,B)={\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}\mathop{\otimes}{B}
and similarly for morphisms.

Theorem 6.6
.

For each
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
we have
\mathsf{FMeas}^{\mathord{\otimes}}(X,\_)\mathrel{\dashv}\mathsf{Path}(X,\_)

Proof 6.7
.

We have the following sequence of natural bijections:

\displaystyle\mathbf{ICones}(

\displaystyle{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}\mathop{\otimes}{B},C)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathbf{ICones}({B}\mathop{\otimes}{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)},C)\text{\hskip 10.22217ptby symmetry of }\mathop{\otimes}\text{, Th.~\ref{th:icones-smcc}}

\displaystyle\simeq\mathbf{ICones}(B,{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}\multimap{C})\text{\hskip 10.22217ptsince }\mathbf{ICones}\text{ is an SMCC, Th.~\ref{th:icones-smcc}}

\displaystyle\simeq\mathbf{ICones}(B,\mathsf{Path}(X,C))\text{\hskip 10.22217ptby Theorem~\ref{th:meas-path-equiv}.}

6.1.
The category of substochastic kernels as a full
subcategory of
\mathbf{ICones}

If
X,Y\in\mathbf{Ar}
, a substochastic kernel from
X
to
Y
is
an element of
\mathbf{Skern}(X,Y)=\mathcal{B}\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,\mathsf{FMeas}(Y))}
: this
is an equivalent characterization of this standard measure theory and
probability notion.
Then
\mathbf{Skern}
is the category whose objects are those or
\mathbf{Ar}

and:

•

the identity at
X
is
\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}\in\mathbf{Skern}(X,X)

•

and given
\kappa_{1}\in\mathbf{Skern}(X_{1},X_{2})
and

\kappa_{2}\in\mathbf{Skern}(X_{2},X_{3})
, their composite

\kappa=\kappa_{2}\,\kappa_{1}
is given by

\displaystyle\kappa(r_{1})(U_{3})=\int_{r_{2}\in X_{2}}^{1}\kappa_{2}(r_{2},U_{3})\kappa_{1}(r_{1},dr_{2})

for
U_{3}\in\sigma_{X_{3}}
, that is
\kappa(r_{1})=\mathcal{I}^{\mathsf{FMeas}(X_{3})}_{X_{2}}(\kappa_{2})(\kappa_{1}(r_{1}))
:
this formula is a continuous generalization of the product of
substochastic matrices.

As is well known the category of measurable spaces and substochastic
kernels can be presented as the Kleisli category of the Giry monad
(or more precisely, of the Panangaden monad since we consider
substochastic kernels instead of stochastic kernels),
but this point of view does not apply to our case because the set of
objects of our small category
\mathbf{Ar}
has no reason to be
stable under the action of the Panangaden monad.

If
\kappa\in\mathbf{Skern}(X,Y)
, we set
\mathsf{Klin}(\kappa)=\mathcal{I}^{\mathsf{FMeas}(Y)}_{X}(\kappa)\in\mathbf{ICones}(\mathsf{FMeas}(X),\mathsf{FMeas}(Y))

defining a functor
\mathsf{Klin}:\mathbf{Skern}\to\mathbf{ICones}
which maps

X\in\mathbf{Ar}
to
\mathsf{FMeas}(X)
.
Remember that we use
\mathsf{FMeas}
for the functor

\mathbf{Ar}\to\mathbf{ICones}
defined on morphisms by

\mathsf{FMeas}(\varphi)={\varphi}_{\ast}=\mathsf{Klin}(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{Y}\mathrel{\circ}\varphi)\in\mathbf{ICones}(\mathsf{FMeas}(X),\mathsf{FMeas}(Y))
for

\varphi\in\mathbf{Ar}(X,Y)
.

Theorem 6.8
.

The functor
\mathsf{Klin}:\mathbf{Skern}\to\mathbf{ICones}
is full and faithful.

Proof 6.9
.

By Theorem
6.1
.

Remark 6.10
.

So we can consider the category of measurable spaces (at least those
sorted out by
\mathbf{Ar}
) and
substochastic kernels
as a full
subcategory of
\mathbf{ICones}
and again, this is a major consequence of
the assumption that linear morphisms must preserve integrals.
This has to be compared with QBSs which form a cartesian closed
category which contain the category of measurable spaces and

measurable functions
(or a full subcategory thereof such as
our
\mathbf{Ar}
) as a full subcategory through the Yoneda embedding.

In Section
9.1.1
we will see that, under
very reasonable assumptions about its objects,
\mathbf{Ar}
arises as
a full subcategory of
{\mathbf{ICones}}^{\oc}
, the category of

\oc
-coalgebras for an exponential comonad
\oc
based on
stable and measurable functions, or on analytical morphisms.

Theorem 6.11
.

There is an iso in
\mathbf{ICones}(\mathsf{FMeas}(0),1)
and, given

X,Y\in\mathbf{Ar}
, there is an iso in

\mathbf{ICones}(\mathsf{FMeas}(X\times Y),{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}\mathop{\otimes}{\mathsf{FMeas}(Y)})

which is natural in
X
and
Y
on the category
\mathbf{Ar}
.
These isos turn
\mathsf{FMeas}
into a strong monoidal functor

(\mathbf{Ar},\mathord{\times})\to(\mathbf{ICones},\mathord{\otimes})
.

Proof 6.12
.

Remember first that
\mathsf{FMeas}
is a functor
\mathbf{Ar}\to\mathbf{ICones}

which acts on morphisms by push-forward (see
Lemma
4.16
).

The first statement is obvious since a finite measure on a one
element space is the same thing as an element of
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
whose
norm is its value, and in that case our definition of measurability
and integrals coincide with the usual ones.

Given an object
B
of
\mathbf{ICones}
we have the following sequence
of natural bijections between functors
{\mathbf{Ar}}^{\mathsf{op}}\times{\mathbf{Ar}}^{\mathsf{op}}\times\mathbf{ICones}\to\mathbf{Set}

\displaystyle\mathbf{ICones}(\mathsf{FMeas}(X\times Y),B)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathcal{B}\underline{{\mathsf{FMeas}(X\times Y)}\multimap{B}}

\displaystyle\simeq\mathcal{B}\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X\times Y,B)}\text{\hskip 10.22217ptby Theorem~\ref{th:meas-path-equiv}}

\displaystyle\simeq\mathcal{B}\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,(\mathsf{Path}(Y,B)))}\text{\hskip 10.22217ptby Lemma~\ref{lemma:meas-path-flat}}

\displaystyle\simeq\mathcal{B}\underline{{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}\multimap{\left({\mathsf{FMeas}(Y)}\multimap{B}\right)}}\text{\hskip 10.22217ptby Theorem~\ref{th:meas-path-equiv}}

\displaystyle\simeq\mathbf{ICones}(\mathsf{FMeas}(X),{\left({\mathsf{FMeas}(Y)}\multimap{B}\right)})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathbf{ICones}({\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}\mathop{\otimes}{\mathsf{FMeas}(Y)},B)

because
\mathbf{ICones}
is an SMCC and so by
Lemma
1.1
we have a natural transformation

\displaystyle\psi_{X,Y}\in\mathbf{ICones}({\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}\mathop{\otimes}{\mathsf{FMeas}(Y)},\mathsf{FMeas}(X\times Y))

between functors
\mathbf{Ar}\times\mathbf{Ar}\to\mathbf{ICones}
and this natural
transformation is completely characterized by

\psi_{X,Y}({\mu}\mathop{\otimes}{\nu})=\mu\times\nu
by the definition of the
iso
\mathsf{fl}
used in Lemma
3.22
.
Using this characterization as well as
Proposition
5.23
, it is easy to prove that
this natural isomorphism (together with its
0
-ary version) turns

\mathsf{FMeas}
into a monoidal functor from the cartesian category

\mathbf{Ar}
to the monoidal category
\mathbf{ICones}
.
The proof uses also the fact that

{(\varphi_{1}\times\varphi_{2})}_{\ast}{(\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2})}={(\varphi_{1})}_{\ast}{(\mu_{1})}\times{(\psi_{2})}_{\ast}({\mu_{2}})
for

\mu_{i}\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}{X_{i}}}
and
\varphi_{i}\in\mathbf{Ar}(X_{i},Y_{i})
for

i=1,2
.

Remark 6.13
.

This is yet another highly desirable property of the tensor product
which results from the preservation of integrals by linear
morphisms.
It means that an element
\pi
of
{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}\mathop{\otimes}{\mathsf{FMeas}(Y)}

whose norm is
1
can be considered as the joint probability
distribution of two (not necessarily independent) random variables
valued in
X
and
Y
respectively.
The case where
\pi={\mu}\mathop{\otimes}{\nu}
corresponds to the situation
where the random variables are independent, of associated
distributions
\mu
and
\nu
.

7.
Stable and measurable functions

We start studying the non-linear maps between integrable cones and we
will consider actually two kinds of non-linear morphisms:

•

the stable and measurable morphisms in the present section

•

and the analytic ones in Section
8
.

The first ones were introduced in
[
EPT18b
]
in a
weaker setting (no general notion of integration was considered in
that paper).
The second ones are naturally derived from the monoidal structure of

\mathbf{ICones}
and from the
\omega
-completeness of cones.
The two notions are deeply connected: analytic morphisms are in
particular stable, and some properties proven in
Section
7
will be useful in
Section
8
.
We will also see in Remark
9.10
that there are
stable functions which are not analytic, the fundamental reason for
that being that the definition of stability does not refer to
integrals.
In the
discrete probability
setting of probabilistic coherence
spaces (see Section
10
), it has been proved that
the two notions are equivalent, see
[
Cru18
]
.

Stable morphisms satisfy a strong form of monotonicity, which, in
ordinary real analysis, can be expressed in terms of derivatives:

\forall n\in{\mathbb{N}}\ f^{(n)}(x)\geq 0
(absolute monotonicity).
Because we are working in the setting of cones, we use iterated
differences instead of derivatives, following an idea first introduced
by Bernstein in 1914.

The definition of these iterated differences uses a notion of

local cone
introduced in
[
EPT18b
]
and that
we recall now.
This construction could be rephrased in terms of summability
structure
[
Ehr23
]
, but this is not necessary for our purpose
here.

7.1.
The local cone

One major feature of stable functions
20
20

20

This will be also the case of analytic morphisms in
Section
8
.
from an integrable cone
B
to an integrable cone
C
is that,
contrarily to linear morphisms, they will be defined, in general, only
on the closed “unit ball”
\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
of
B
, see
Remark
2.5
.
A typical example is the already mentioned function

f:\mathcal{B}\underline{1}=[0,1]\to\underline{1}
given by

f(x)=1-\sqrt{1-x}
, see Remark
2.5
.
To deal with such a function
\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\to\underline{C}
and its
strong monotonicity properties at a given
x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
, it
will be important to be able to consider the set
U
of all

u\in\underline{B}
such that
x+u\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
, or rather of all
elements of
\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
of shape
\lambda u
for such an

u
and for
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
: we will see that
U
can itself
be considered as a cone, the
local cone
of
B
at
x
.

Let
B
be an integrable cone and
x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
.
Let

\displaystyle P=\{u\in\underline{B}\mid\exists\varepsilon>0\ x+\varepsilon u\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\}\,.

Then
P
is a precone whose
0
element is the
0
of

\underline{B}
.
Indeed if
u_{1},u_{2}\in P
there is
\varepsilon>0
such that

(x+\varepsilon u_{i}\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B})_{i=1,2}
and hence

\displaystyle x+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}(u_{1}+u_{2})=\frac{1}{2}(x+\varepsilon u_{1})+\frac{1}{2}(x+\varepsilon u_{2})\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}

so that
u_{1}+u_{2}\in P
.
The fact that

u\in P\Rightarrow\forall\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\ \lambda u\in P
is
clear. The condition
2.1.
Basic definitions
and
2.1.
Basic definitions
result easily from the
fact that they hold in
\underline{B}
.

We can equip
P
with a map (sometimes called a gauge)
N:P\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
defined by

\displaystyle N(u)=(\sup\{\lambda>0\mid x+\lambda u\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\})^{-1}=\inf\{\lambda^{-1}\mid\lambda>0\text{ and }x+\lambda u\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\}

Lemma 7.1
.

The function
N
is a norm on
P
and, equipped with this norm,

P
is a cone whose
0
element and algebraic operations are
those of
\underline{B}
.

Proof 7.2
.

Assume that
N(u)=0
, this means that
\forall\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\ \left\|{x+\lambda u}\right\|\leq 1
and hence
\forall\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\ \lambda\left\|{u}\right\|\leq 1
so that
u=0
. Let
u_{1},u_{2}\in P
and let
\varepsilon>0
.
We can find
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}>0
such that

x+\lambda_{i}u_{i}\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
and
\lambda_{i}^{-1}\leq N(u_{i})+\varepsilon/2
for
i=1,2
.
We have

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda_{1}}{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}(x+\lambda_{2}u_{2})+\frac{\lambda_{2}}{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}(x+\lambda_{1}u_{1})\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}

so that

\displaystyle x+\frac{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}(u_{1}+u_{2})\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}

so that
N(u_{1}+u_{2})\leq(\frac{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}})^{-1}=\lambda_{1}^{-1}+\lambda_{2}^{-1}\leq N(u_{1})+N(u_{2})+\varepsilon
.
Since this holds for all
\varepsilon>0
we have

N(u_{1}+u_{2})\leq N(u_{1})+N(u_{2})
.
The property
2.1.
Basic definitions
is easy, let us prove
2.1.
Basic definitions
.
Observe first that, for
u,v\in P
, we have
u\leq_{P}v
iff
u\leq_{B}v
.
Let
(u_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be an increasing sequence in
P
such that
\forall n\in{\mathbb{N}}\ N(u_{n})\leq 1
.
Then we have
\forall n\in{\mathbb{N}}\ x+u_{n}\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
and hence the
sequence
(x+u_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is an increasing sequence in

\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
and so it has a lub in
\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
which coincides with
x+u
where
u
is the lub of

(u_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in
\underline{B}
.
Thus we have
u\in P
and
N(u)\leq 1
.
Last observe that
u
is
a fortiori
the lub of the

u_{n}
’s in
P
.

We use now the standard notation
\left\|{\_}\right\|
for that norm. Notice that

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}P=\{u\in\underline{B}\mid x+u\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\}

and also that
\left\|{u}\right\|_{B}\leq\left\|{u}\right\|
, for all
u\in P
.

For each
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
we define
\mathcal{M}_{X}
as the set of all
test functions
\boldsymbol{\lambda}r\in X\cdot\boldsymbol{\lambda}u\in P\cdot m(r,u)
for the elements

m
of
\mathcal{M}^{B}_{X}
(such an element of
\mathcal{M}_{X}
will still be
denoted by
m
even if two different elements of
\mathcal{M}^{B}_{X}

possibly induce the same test).
The fact that
\forall r\in X,\,u\in\mathcal{B}P\ m(r,u)\leq 1
whenever

m\in\mathcal{M}_{X}
results from
\mathcal{B}P\subseteq\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
.

Then it is straightforward to check that

(P,(\mathcal{M}_{X})_{X\in\mathbf{Ar}})
is a measurable cone, that we denote as

{B}_{x}
and call

the local cone of
B
at
x
and it is also clear that this
measurable cone is integrable, the integral of a path in
{B}_{x}

being defined exactly as in
B
.

It is important to keep in mind the meaning of the norm in this local
cone, which is most usefully described as follows.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
and

u\in\underline{{B}_{x}}\setminus\{0\}
. Then we have

x+\left\|{u}\right\|^{-1}_{{B}_{x}}u\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
and
x+\lambda u\notin\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
for all
\lambda>\left\|{u}\right\|^{-1}_{\underline{{B}_{x}}}
.

Proof 7.4
.

By definition of the norm in
{B}_{x}
and by the
\omega
-closedness
of
\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
.

Example 7.5
.

Let
B=1
so that
\underline{1}=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and

\mathcal{B}\underline{1}=[0,1]
. If
x\in[0,1]
we have
two cases: if
x=1
then
{B}_{x}=0
and if
x<1
we
have
\underline{{B}_{x}}=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. If
u\in\underline{B}_{x}\setminus\{0\}

(which implies
x<1
) then the largest
\lambda>0
such that

x+\lambda u\in[0,1]
is
\frac{1-x}{u}
and hence
\left\|{u}\right\|_{{B}_{x}}=\frac{u}{1-x}=\frac{1}{1-x}\left\|{u}\right\|_{B}
so the
local cone
{B}_{x}
can be seen as an homothetic image of
B

by a factor
\frac{1}{1-x}
which goes to
\infty
when
x

gets closer to the border
1
of the unit ball

[0,1]
. This very simple example gives an intuition of what
happens in general, with the difference that
B_{x}
has no reason
to be always homothetic to
B
, think for instance of the case
where
B={1}\mathrel{\&}{1}
and
x=(1,0)
: then
{B}_{x}
is
isomorphic to
1
.

7.2.
The integrable cone of stable and measurable functions

Given
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we use
\mathcal{P}^{-}(n)
(resp.
\mathcal{P}^{+}(n)
) for the
set of all subsets
I
of
\{1,\dots,n\}
such that

n-\#{I}
is odd (resp. even).
In particular
\{1,\dots,n\}\in\mathcal{P}^{+}(n)
.

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
j\in\{1,\dots,n+1\}
and
\varepsilon\in\{-,+\}
. Given
I\in\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}(n)
, the set

\displaystyle\mathsf{inj}_{j}(I)=\{i\in I\mid i<j\}\cup\{j\}\cup\{i+1\mid i\in I\text{ and }i\geq j\}

belongs to
\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}(n+1)
and
\mathsf{inj}_{j}
defines a bijection between
\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}(n)
and
the set of all
J\subseteq\{1,\dots,n+1\}
such that

J\in\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}(n+1)
and
j\in J
.

This is obvious.

Definition 7.7
.

Let
P
and
Q
be cones, a function
f:\mathcal{B}P\to Q
is
totally monotonic
if for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and each
x,u_{1},\dots,u_{n}\in P
such that
x+\sum_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}\in\mathcal{B}P
one has

\displaystyle\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{-}(n)}f(x+\sum_{i\in I}u_{i})\leq\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{+}(n)}f(x+\sum_{i\in I}u_{i})\,.

(5)

For
n=1
this condition means that
f
is increasing.
For
n=2
we have
\mathcal{P}^{-}(2)=\{\{1\},\{2\}\}
and

\mathcal{P}^{+}(2)=\{\{1,2\},\emptyset\}
, so
Condition (
5
) means

\displaystyle f(x+u_{1})+f(x+u_{2})\leq f(x+u_{1}+u_{2})+f(x)

that is, assuming that
f
is increasing,

\displaystyle f(x+u_{1})-f(x)\leq f(x+u_{1}+u_{2})-f(x+u_{2})

in other words, the map
x\mapsto f(x+u_{1})-f(x)
is increasing (where
it is defined).
For
n=3
we have
\mathcal{P}^{-}(3)=\{\{1,2\},\{2,3\},\{1,3\},\emptyset\}

and
\mathcal{P}^{+}(3)=\{\{1,2,3\},\{1\},\{2\},\{3\}\}
, so
Condition (
5
) means:

f(x+u_{1}+u_{2})+f(x+u_{2}+u_{3})+f(x+u_{1}+u_{3})+f(x)\\
\leq f(x+u_{1}+u_{2}+u_{3})+f(x+u_{1})+f(x+u_{2})+f(x+u_{3})\,.

Remark 7.8
.

This kind of definition appears in many places in the literature in
real analysis, differential equations, Laplace transforms etc.
The corresponding conditions, first considered by Hausdorff, are
then usually expressed in terms of derivatives: for instance a
function
f
from some open interval
I
of
\mathbb{R}
to

\mathbb{R}
is
absolutely monotonic
(resp.
completely
monotonic
) if it is
C^{\infty}
and satisfies

f^{(n)}(x)\geq 0
(resp.
(-1)^{n}f^{(n)}(x)\geq 0
) for all

x\in I
and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
Bernstein introduces in
[
Ber14
]
, in the case of real
functions of one real parameter, iterated differences allowing to
characterize absolutely monotonic functions — which in turn can be
shown to be analytic — by a condition which is equivalent
to (
5
).
We use the expression “totally monotonic” for this extension to
cones of Bernstein’s definition to avoid confusion with “completely
monotonic” and “absolutely monotonic”.

This definition arose in denotational semantics during the work of
the first author reported in
[
EPT18b
]
, when the
authors of that paper tried to build a
cartesian closed
category
whose objects are Selinger’s cones, ordered by the
cone order (
x\leq y
if there is a
z
such that

x+z=y
).
A careful analysis of these constraints (which are actually quite
strong) leads unavoidably to the conclusion that the morphisms of
the sought CCC should be totally monotonic.
We will see in Section
7.4
how total monotonicity
leads indeed to cartesian closedness.

Definition 7.9
.

Let
P
and
Q
be cones.
A function
f:\mathcal{B}P\to Q
is
stable
if
f
is totally monotonic, bounded and
\omega
-continuous (see
Definition
2.2
).

Remark 7.10
.

This terminology is motivated by the fact that stable functions (in
the sense of Berry
[
Ber78
]
) on Girard’s coherence
spaces
[
Gir86
]
can be characterized by a property completely
similar to total monotonicity.
We have the intuition that our stable morphisms on cones are a
quantitative analog of the notion of stable function introduced in
“qualitative” denotational semantics.

Example 7.11
.

Let
B=\mathsf{FMeas}{(\{0,1\})}
so that
\underline{B}=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}
with the norm given by
\left\|{x}\right\|=x_{0}+x_{1}
.
Then for each
\overrightarrow{a}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{{\mathbb{N}}\times{\mathbb{N}}}
which satisfies

\forall t\in[0,1]\quad\sum_{p,q\in{\mathbb{N}}}a_{p,q}t^{p}(1-t)^{q}\leq 1
,
the function
f:\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
defined by
f(x)=\sum_{p,q\in{\mathbb{N}}}a_{p,q}x_{0}^{p}x_{1}^{q}
is totally monotonic.
An example of such a function is
f(x)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}2^{n}x_{0}^{n}x_{1}^{n}
since for

x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
one has
x_{0}x_{1}\leq\frac{1}{4}
.
One might think that
f
is convex since all its iterated partial
derivatives are
\geq 0
everywhere, but this is not true since for
instance
f(0,1)=f(1,0)=0
whereas
f(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})=1
.
In general, total monotonicity of functions defined by power series
with one or more parameters as in this example correspond to the
fact that all the partial derivatives are everywhere
\geq 0
, which
is related simply to convexity only in the one parameter case.

Definition 7.12
.

Let
C,D
be measurable cones. A stable function
f:\mathcal{B}\underline{C}\to\underline{D}
is measurable if for each

X\in\mathbf{Ar}
and
\gamma\in\mathcal{B}\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,C)}
one has
f\mathrel{\circ}\gamma\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,D)}
.

Lemma 7.13
.

The set of stable and measurable functions
C\to D
, equipped with
algebraic operations defined pointwise, is a precone.

This is straightforward, we use
P
for this precone.
We need first to understand the order on stable functions induced by
the addition operation of
P
.
As usual, this order relation is simply denoted as
\leq
of
\leq_{P}
.

Lemma 7.14
.

Let
f,g\in P
. One has
f\leq g
iff for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
each
x,u_{1},\dots,u_{n}\in\mathcal{B}\underline{C}
such that
x+\sum_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}\in\mathcal{B}\underline{C}
one has

\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{-}(n)}g(x+\sum_{i\in I}u_{i})+\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{+}(n)}f(x+\sum_{i\in I}u_{i})\\
\leq\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{+}(n)}g(x+\sum_{i\in I}u_{i})+\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{-}(n)}f(x+\sum_{i\in I}u_{i})\,.

Proof 7.15
.

Assume first that
f\leq g
and let
h=g-f
.
Notice that since addition is defined pointwise in
P
we have

h(x)=g(x)-f(x)
for all
x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{C}
, and by
definition of the order relation of
P
, this function
h
is
stable.
Given
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
x,u_{1},\dots,u_{n}\in\mathcal{B}\underline{C}
such that

x+\sum_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}\in\mathcal{B}\underline{C}
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{-}(n)}h(x+\sum_{i\in I}u_{i})\leq\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{+}(n)}h(x+\sum_{i\in I}u_{i})

and the announced inequality follows. Conversely if the property
expressed in the lemma holds we have in particular

\forall x\in\mathcal{B}P\ f(x)\leq g(x)
(take
n=0
) and so we
can define a function
h:\mathcal{B}\underline{C}\to\underline{D}
by

h(x)=g(x)-f(x)
and this function is totally monotonic.
This function is
\omega
-continuous by
Lemma
2.12
and is measurable because
subtraction is measurable on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Remark 7.16
.

Notice that if
f\leq g
(still for the cone order relation of

P
, characterized by Lemma
7.14
) then

f(x)\leq g(x)
for all
x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
, but the converse
is not true.
As an example take
f(x)=x
and
g(x)=1
, defining two stable
functions (for
B=C=1
) which do not satisfy
f\leq_{P}g
but
are such that
f(x)\leq g(x)
holds for all
x\in[0,1]
.
It is natural to call this order relation on stable functions the

stable order
in reference to
[
Ber78
,
Gir86
]
where
the stable order behaves in a very similar way, and admits a similar
characterization, in terms of differences.

We equip this precone
P
with the norm

\left\|{f}\right\|=\sup_{x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{C}}\left\|{f(x)}\right\|
which is well
defined by our assumptions that stable functions are bounded.

Lemma 7.17
.

Let
(f_{n}\in\mathcal{B}P)_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be an increasing sequence (for the
stable order).
Then
f:\mathcal{B}\underline{C}\to\underline{D}
defined by

f(x)=\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}f_{n}(x)
is bounded, totally monotonic,

\omega
-continuous and measurable, that is,
f\in P
.
This map
f
is the lub of the
f_{n}
’s in

P
.

Proof 7.18

(Proof sketch)
.

Total monotonicity follows from
\omega
-continuity of addition,

\omega
-continuity is straightforward and measurability results from
the monotone convergence theorem.
The fact that
f
is the lub of the
f_{n}
’s results from the
fact that it is defined as a pointwise lub.

So
P
is a cone that we equip with a measurability structure

\mathcal{M}
defined as in
{C}\multimap{D}
: a
p\in\mathcal{M}_{X}
is a function

p=\gamma\mathrel{\triangleright}m
where
\gamma\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,C)}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{D}_{Y}
, given by

\displaystyle\gamma\mathrel{\triangleright}m=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(r,f)\in X\times P\cdot m(r,f(\gamma(r)))\,.

Then we check that
\mathcal{M}
satisfies the required conditions exactly
as we did for
{C}\multimap{D}
in Section
5.1
.
We have defined a measurable cone
C\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{s}}D
that we prove now to
be integrable.

Let
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
and
\eta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,C\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{s}}D)}
, and
let
\mu\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}
.
We define a function
f:\mathcal{B}\underline{C}\to\underline{D}
by

\displaystyle f(x)=\int_{r\in X}\eta(r)(x)\mu(dr)\,.

This integral is well defined because, for each

x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
, the function
\boldsymbol{\lambda}r\in X\cdot\eta(r)(x)

is measurable and bounded since
\eta
is a measurable path.
The function
f
is totally monotonic by bilinearity of integration,

\omega
-continuous by the monotone convergence theorem, we check
that it is measurable.
Let
Y\in\mathbf{Ar}
and
\gamma\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,C)}
, we have

\displaystyle f\mathrel{\circ}\gamma

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}s\in Y\cdot\int_{r\in X}\eta(r)(\gamma(s))\mu(dr)

and we must prove that
f\mathrel{\circ}\gamma\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,D)}
,
so let
Y^{\prime}\in\mathbf{Ar}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{D}_{Y^{\prime}}
, we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s^{\prime},s)\in Y^{\prime}\times Y\cdot m(s^{\prime},(f\mathrel{\circ}\gamma)(s))

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s^{\prime},s)\in Y^{\prime}\times Y\cdot m\Big(s^{\prime},\int_{r\in X}\eta(r)(\gamma(s))\mu(dr)\Big)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s^{\prime},s)\in Y^{\prime}\times Y\cdot\int_{r\in X}m(s^{\prime},\eta(r)(\gamma(s)))\mu(dr)

which is measurable because
\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s^{\prime},s,r)\in Y^{\prime}\times Y\times X\cdot m(s^{\prime},\eta(r)(\gamma(s)))
is measurable by our assumption about
\eta
and by
Lemma
4.8
.
This shows that
f\in\underline{C\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{s}}D}
.
Let
p\in\mathcal{M}^{{C}\Rightarrow{D}}_{0}
so that
p=x\mathrel{\triangleright}m
for some
x\in\underline{C}
and

m\in\mathcal{M}^{D}_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle p(f)

\displaystyle=m\Big(\int_{r\in X}\eta(r)(x)\mu(dr)\Big)

\displaystyle=\int_{r\in X}m(\eta(r)(x))\mu(dr)

\displaystyle=\int_{r\in X}p(\eta(r))\mu(dr)

so that
f
is the integral of
\eta
over
\mu
, this shows that

C\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{s}}D
is an integrable cone.

7.3.
Finite differences

We introduce a natural difference operator on totally monotonic
functions which provides an inductive characterization of total
monotonicity that we will use to prove two basic properties of
totally monotonic functions, Lemmas
7.39

and
7.41
, which will show useful to
establish a property which is not completely obvious: the composition
of two stable functions is stable.
In the setting of complete and absolute monotonicity in real analysis,
the corresponding property can be obtained by means of the
Faà di Bruno formula (higher derivative of composite of functions) as
mentioned in
[
LN83
]
, Section 7.
Our notion of total monotonicity being defined in terms of iterated
differences, we need a specific reasoning.

Given
\overrightarrow{u}\in{\underline{B}}^{n}
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
we use
{B}_{\overrightarrow{u}}

for the local cone
{B}_{\sum_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}}
(see
Section
7.1
).

Let
B,C
be cones,
f:\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\to\underline{C}
be a function,

n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
u_{1},\dots,u_{n}\in\underline{B}
such that

\sum_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
we define

\displaystyle\Delta^{\varepsilon}f(\overrightarrow{u}):\mathcal{B}\underline{{B}_{\overrightarrow{u}}}

\displaystyle\to\underline{C}

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}(n)}f(x+\sum_{i\in I}u_{i})

for
\varepsilon\in\{-,+\}

and if
f
is totally monotonic we set

\displaystyle\Delta f(\overrightarrow{u})=\Delta^{+}f(\overrightarrow{u})-\Delta^{-}f(\overrightarrow{u}):\mathcal{B}\underline{{B}_{\overrightarrow{u}}}\to\underline{C}\,,

the difference being computed pointwise.
Notice that for
n=0
(so that
\overrightarrow{u}=(\,)
) we have
\Delta f((\,))=f

since
\mathcal{P}^{+}(0)=\{\emptyset\}
and
\mathcal{P}^{-}(0)=\emptyset
.

Definition 7.19
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\to\underline{C}
be a function and let

n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. We say that
f
is
n
-increasing from
B
to
C
if

•

n=0
and
f
is increasing

•

or
n>0
,
f
is increasing and, for all

u\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
the function

\Delta f(u):\mathcal{B}\underline{{B}_{u}}\to\underline{C}
(which maps
x

to
f(x+u)-f(x)
) is
(n-1)
-increasing from
{B}_{u}
to

C
.

Lemma 7.20
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\to\underline{C}
be a function which is

n
-increasing for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
Then for all
u\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
, the function
\Delta f(u):\mathcal{B}\underline{{B}_{u}}\to\underline{C}
is
n
-increasing
for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

Proof 7.21
.

Immediate consequence of the definition of
n
-increasing functions.

Lemma 7.22
.

Let
f:\underline{B}\to\underline{C}
be totally monotonic.
For
u,u_{1},\dots,u_{n}\in\underline{B}
and

x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{{B}_{u,\overrightarrow{u}}}
, one has

\Delta^{\varepsilon}f(u,\overrightarrow{u})(x)=\Delta^{\varepsilon}f(\overrightarrow{u})(x+u)+\Delta^{-\varepsilon}f(\overrightarrow{u})(x)
for
\varepsilon\in\{\mathord{+},\mathord{-}\}
.
Moreover

\displaystyle\Delta f(\overrightarrow{u})(x)\leq\Delta f(\overrightarrow{u})(x+u)\text{\hskip 10.22217ptand\hskip 10.22217pt}\Delta f(u,\overrightarrow{u})=\Delta(\Delta f(\overrightarrow{u}))(u)\,.

Proof 7.23
.

Let
\overrightarrow{v}=(u,\overrightarrow{u})
, of length
n+1
.
We have

\displaystyle\Delta^{\varepsilon}f(\overrightarrow{v})(x)=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}(n+1)}f(x+\sum_{i\in I}v_{i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{I\in\Epset\epsilon{n+1}}{1\in I}}f(x+\sum_{i\in I}v_{i})+\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{I\in\Epset\epsilon{n+1}}{1\notin I}}f(x+\sum_{i\in I}v_{i})\,.

Now observe that

\displaystyle\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{I\in\Epset{\epsilon}{n+1}}{1\notin I}}f(x+\sum_{i\in I}v_{i})=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{-\varepsilon}(n)}f(x+\sum_{i\in I}u_{i})=\Delta^{-\varepsilon}f(\overrightarrow{u})(x)

by definition of
\overrightarrow{v}
and, using
Lemma
7.6
, observe also that

\displaystyle\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{I\in\Epset\epsilon{n+1}}{1\in I}}f(x+\sum_{i\in I}v_{i})=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}(n)}f(x+u+\sum_{i\in I}u_{i})=\Delta^{\varepsilon}f(\overrightarrow{u})(x+u)\,.

So we have

\Delta^{\varepsilon}f(u,\overrightarrow{u})(x)=\Delta^{\varepsilon}f(\overrightarrow{u})(x+u)+\Delta^{-\varepsilon}f(\overrightarrow{u})(x)
.
Since
f
is totally monotonic, we have

\displaystyle\Delta^{-}f(u,\overrightarrow{u})(x)\leq\Delta^{+}f(u,\overrightarrow{u})(x)

and hence

\displaystyle\Delta^{-}f(\overrightarrow{u})(x+u)-\Delta^{-}f(\overrightarrow{u})(x)\leq\Delta^{+}f(\overrightarrow{u})(x+u)-\Delta^{+}f(\overrightarrow{u})(x)

both subtractions being defined since
f
is increasing.
Therefore
\Delta f(\overrightarrow{u})(x)\leq\Delta f(\overrightarrow{u})(x+u)
.
Moreover

\displaystyle\Delta f(u,\overrightarrow{u})(x)

\displaystyle=\Delta^{+}f(u,\overrightarrow{u})(x)-\Delta^{-}f(u,\overrightarrow{u})(x)

\displaystyle=(\Delta^{+}f(\overrightarrow{u})(x+u)+\Delta^{-}f(\overrightarrow{u})(x))-(\Delta^{-}f(\overrightarrow{u})(x+u)+\Delta^{+}f(\overrightarrow{u})(x))

\displaystyle=(\Delta^{+}f(\overrightarrow{u})(x+u)-\Delta^{-}f(\overrightarrow{u})(x+u))-(\Delta^{+}f(\overrightarrow{u})(x)-\Delta^{-}f(\overrightarrow{u})(x))

\displaystyle=\Delta f(\overrightarrow{u})(x+u)-\Delta f(\overrightarrow{u})(x)

\displaystyle=\Delta(\Delta f(\overrightarrow{u}))(u)(x)\,.

Lemma 7.24
.

If a function
f\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\to\underline{C}
is totally
monotonic, then for each
u\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
, the function

\Delta f(u):\mathcal{B}\underline{{B}_{u}}\to\underline{C}
is totally
monotonic.

Proof 7.25
.

Let
\overrightarrow{u}\in\mathcal{B}\underline{{B}_{u}}
, notice that
{({B}_{u})}_{\overrightarrow{u}}={B}_{u,\overrightarrow{u}}
.
Let
x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{{B}_{u,\overrightarrow{u}}}
.
We have

\Delta^{\varepsilon}(\Delta f(u))(\overrightarrow{u})(x)=\Delta^{\varepsilon}f(\overrightarrow{u})(x+u)-\Delta^{\varepsilon}f(\overrightarrow{u})(x)

where the subtraction makes sense because
f
is increasing.
By our assumption on
f
we have

\displaystyle\Delta^{-}f(u,\overrightarrow{u})(x)\leq\Delta^{+}f(u,\overrightarrow{u})(x)

that is

\displaystyle\Delta^{-}f(\overrightarrow{u})(x+u)+\Delta^{+}f(\overrightarrow{u})(x)\leq\Delta^{+}f(\overrightarrow{u})(x+u)+\Delta^{-}f(\overrightarrow{u})(x)

by Lemma
7.22
, and hence

\displaystyle\Delta^{-}(\Delta f(u))(\overrightarrow{u})(x)

\displaystyle\leq\Delta^{+}(\Delta f(u))(\overrightarrow{u})(x)

Theorem 7.26
.

A function
f\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\to\underline{C}
is totally monotonic
iff it is
n
-increasing for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

Proof 7.27
.

Remember that
f
is totally monotonic iff for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
all
\overrightarrow{u}\in\underline{B}^{n}
and
x\in{\underline{B}}_{\overrightarrow{u}}
we
have
\Delta^{-}f(\overrightarrow{u})(x)\leq\Delta^{+}f(\overrightarrow{u})(x)
.

We prove first by induction on
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
that for all

f\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\to\underline{C}
, if
f
is totally monotonic
then
f
is
k
-increasing.

For
k=0
, we have to prove that
f
is increasing, which results
from total monotonicity applied with
n=1
.

For
k>0
we have to prove that
f
is increasing (which results
from total monotonicity applied with
n=1
) and that for all

u\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
the function

\Delta f(u):\mathcal{B}\underline{{B}_{u}}\to\underline{C}
is

(k-1)
-increasing, for which, by inductive hypothesis, it
suffices to prove that
\Delta f(u)
is totally monotonic, and this
property results from Lemma
7.24
.

Conversely, we prove by induction on
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
that, for each

f\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\to\underline{C}
, if
f
is
k
-increasing for
all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
then for all
\overrightarrow{u}\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}^{n}
and

x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{{B}_{\overrightarrow{u}}}
, one has

\Delta^{-}f(\overrightarrow{u})(x)\leq\Delta^{+}f(\overrightarrow{u})(x)
.
For
n=0
there is nothing to prove so assume that
n>0
.
Let
(u,\overrightarrow{u})\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}^{n}
and let
x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{{B}_{u,\overrightarrow{u}}}
.
Notice that
\overrightarrow{u}\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}^{n-1}
and

x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{{B}_{\overrightarrow{u}}}
.
Since
\Delta f(u)
is
k
-increasing for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
by
Lemma
7.20
, we know by applying the
inductive hypothesis to
\Delta f(u)
that

\displaystyle\Delta^{-}(\Delta f(u))(\overrightarrow{u})(x)\leq\Delta^{+}(\Delta f(u))(\overrightarrow{u})(x)

that is

\displaystyle\Delta^{-}f(\overrightarrow{u})(x+u)-\Delta^{-}f(\overrightarrow{u})(x)\leq\Delta^{+}f(\overrightarrow{u})(x+u)-\Delta^{+}f(\overrightarrow{u})(x)

which implies

\displaystyle\Delta^{-}f(\overrightarrow{u})(x+u)+\Delta^{+}f(\overrightarrow{u})(x)\leq\Delta^{+}f(\overrightarrow{u})(x+u)+\Delta^{-}f(\overrightarrow{u})(x)

that is
\Delta^{-}f(u,\overrightarrow{u})(x)\leq\Delta^{+}f(u,\overrightarrow{u})(x)

by Lemma
7.22
, as expected.

Lemma 7.28
.

Let
f:\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\to\underline{C}
be totally monotonic and

\overrightarrow{u}\in{\underline{B}}^{n}
be such that
\sum_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
. Then the functions
\Delta^{+}f(\overrightarrow{u}),\Delta^{-}f(\overrightarrow{u}),\Delta f(\overrightarrow{u}):\mathcal{B}\underline{{B}_{\overrightarrow{u}}}\to\underline{C}

are totally monotonic.

Proof 7.29
.

The total monotonicity of
\Delta^{\varepsilon}f(\overrightarrow{u})
results
from the easy observation that if

g:\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\to\mathcal{B}\underline{C}
and

u\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
then the map
g_{u}:\mathcal{B}\underline{{B}_{u}}\to\underline{C}
defined by
g_{u}(x)=g(x+u)
is also totally monotonic, and each sum of totally
monotonic functions is totally monotonic.

The total monotonicity of
\Delta f(\overrightarrow{u})
results from
Theorem
7.26
.

Lemma 7.30
.

Let
f:\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\to\underline{C}
be totally monotonic.
Then for each
\overrightarrow{u}\in\underline{B}^{n}
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
and
x\in\underline{{B}_{\overrightarrow{u}}}

we have

\Delta f(\overrightarrow{u})(x)\leq f(x+\sum_{i=1}^{n}u_{i})
.

Proof 7.31
.

By induction on
n
.
The base case
n=0
is trivial since then

\Delta f(\overrightarrow{u})(x)=f(x)
.
For the inductive case, let
(u,\overrightarrow{u})\in\underline{B}^{n+1}

with
u+\sum_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}\in\mathcal{B}B
and

x\in\underline{{B}_{u,\overrightarrow{u}}}
, that is

x+u\in\underline{{B}_{\overrightarrow{u}}}
.
We have

\Delta f(u,\overrightarrow{u})(x)=\Delta f(\overrightarrow{u})(x+u)-\Delta f(\overrightarrow{u})(x)\leq\Delta f(\overrightarrow{u})(x+u)\leq f(x+u+\sum_{i=1}^{n}u_{i})

by inductive hypothesis.

Lemma 7.32
.

Let
f:\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\to\underline{C}
be a totally monotonic function.
Let
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
u,v\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
and

\overrightarrow{u}\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}^{n}
, and assume that

u+v+\sum_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
.
Then for each
x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{{B}_{\overrightarrow{u}}}
we have

\displaystyle\Delta f(\overrightarrow{u})(x+u)

\displaystyle=\Delta f(\overrightarrow{u})(x)+\Delta f(u,\overrightarrow{u})(x)

\displaystyle\Delta f(u+v,\overrightarrow{u})(x)

\displaystyle=\Delta f(u,\overrightarrow{u})(x)+\Delta f(v,\overrightarrow{u})(x+u)\,.

Proof 7.33
.

The first equation results from

\Delta f(u,\overrightarrow{u})=\Delta(\Delta f(\overrightarrow{u}))(u)
, see
Lemma
7.24
.
For the second equation take

x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{{B}_{u+v,\overrightarrow{u}}}
.
Let
n
be the length of
\overrightarrow{u}
.
Setting
\overrightarrow{v}=(u,\overrightarrow{u})
and
\overrightarrow{w}=(v,\overrightarrow{u})
(both of
length
n+1
), we have

\displaystyle\Delta f(\overrightarrow{v})(x)+\Delta f(\overrightarrow{w})(x+u)=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathord{+}}(n+1)}f(x+\sum_{i\in I}v_{i})-\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathord{-}}(n+1)}f(x+\sum_{i\in I}v_{i})

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt+\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathord{+}}(n+1)}f(x+u+\sum_{i\in I}w_{i})-\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathord{-}}(n+1)}f(x+u+\sum_{i\in I}w_{i})

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathord{-}}(n)}f(x+\sum_{i\in I}u_{i})+\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathord{+}}(n)}f(x+u+\sum_{i\in I}u_{i})

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt-(\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathord{+}}(n)}f(x+\sum_{i\in I}u_{i})+\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathord{-}}(n)}f(x+u+\sum_{i\in I}u_{i}))

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt+\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathord{-}}(n)}f(x+u+\sum_{i\in I}u_{i})+\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathord{+}}(n)}f(x+u+v+\sum_{i\in I}u_{i})

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt-(\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathord{+}}(n)}f(x+u+\sum_{i\in I}u_{i})+\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathord{-}}(n)}f(x+u+v+\sum_{i\in I}u_{i}))

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathord{+}}(n)}f(x+u+v+\sum_{i\in I}u_{i})+\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathord{-}}(n)}f(x+\sum_{i\in I}u_{i})

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt-(\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathord{+}}(n)}f(x+\sum_{i\in I}u_{i})+\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathord{-}}(n)}f(x+u+v+\sum_{i\in I}u_{i}))\,.

Lemma 7.34
.

Let
f:\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\to\underline{C}
be totally monotonic. Let

n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
u\in\underline{B}
and
\overrightarrow{u},\overrightarrow{v}\in\underline{B}^{n}
,
and assume that
u+\sum_{i=1}^{n}(u_{i}+v_{i})\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
.
Then for each
x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{{B}_{u,\overrightarrow{u},\overrightarrow{v}}}
we
have

\displaystyle\Delta f(\overrightarrow{u}+\overrightarrow{v})(x+u)

\displaystyle=\Delta f(\overrightarrow{u})(x)+\Delta f(u,\overrightarrow{u}+\overrightarrow{v})(x)

\displaystyle\quad+\Delta f(v_{1},u_{2}+v_{2},\dots,u_{n}+v_{n})(x+u_{1})

\displaystyle\quad+\Delta f(u_{1},v_{2},u_{3}+v_{3},\dots,u_{n}+v_{n})(x+u_{2})+\cdots

\displaystyle\quad+\Delta f(u_{1},\dots,u_{n-1},v_{n})(x+u_{n})\,.

Proof 7.35
.

Simple computations using Lemma
7.32
.

Let
\mathsf{S}^{n}B
be the cone defined by
\underline{\mathsf{S}^{n}B}=\underline{B}^{n+1}
with operations defined pointwise and
norm defined by
\left\|{(x,\overrightarrow{u})}\right\|_{\mathsf{S}^{n}B}=\left\|{x+\sum_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}}\right\|_{B}
.
It is easy to check that one actually defines a cone in that way.

Remark 7.36
.

This cone is not the
(n+1)
-fold coproduct of
\underline{B}
with itself.
Take indeed
B={1}\mathrel{\&}{1}
and
n=1
, then
\left\|{((1,0),(0,1)}\right\|_{\mathsf{S}^{1}B}=\left\|{(1,1)}\right\|_{\underline{B}}=1
whereas

\left\|{((1,0),(0,1)}\right\|_{{\underline{B}}\oplus{\underline{B}}}=2
.
Neither is it the
(n+1)
-fold product of
\underline{B}
; it is
actually (isomorphic to)

{\overbrace{1\mathrel{\&}\cdots\mathrel{\&}1}^{n+1}}\multimap{B}
.
This construct is at the origin of coherent
differentiation
[
Ehr23
]
.

Lemma 7.37
.

If
f:\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\to\underline{C}
is totally monotonic, the map
(x,\overrightarrow{u})\to\Delta f(\overrightarrow{u})(x)
is increasing
\mathcal{B}\mathsf{S}^{n}B\to\underline{C}
.

Proof 7.38
.

Follows easily from Theorem
7.26
.

Now we can state and prove the main lemma which allows to prove that
totally monotonic functions are closed under composition.
Remember that even in the setting of completely and absolutely
monotonic functions (where derivatives instead of differences are
used), the corresponding result is not completely trivial as it
requires the use of the Faà di Bruno formula.

Lemma 7.39
.

Let
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
f,h_{1},\dots,h_{n}:\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\to\underline{C}

and
g:\mathcal{B}\underline{C}\to\underline{D}
be totally monotonic functions
such that

\forall x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\ f(x)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}h_{i}(x)\in\mathcal{B}\underline{C}
.
Then the function
k:\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\to\underline{D}
defined by

k(x)=\Delta g(h_{1}(x),\dots,h_{n}(x))(f(x))

is totally monotonic.

Proof 7.40
.

With the notations and conventions of the statement, we prove by
induction on
p
that, for all
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
, for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, for
all
f,h_{1},\dots,h_{n},g
which are totally monotonic and satisfy

\forall x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\ f(x)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}h_{i}(x)\in\mathcal{B}\underline{C}
, the function
k
is

p
-increasing.

For
p=0
, the property results from
Lemma
7.37
.

We assume the property for
p
and prove it for
p+1
. Let

u\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
we have to prove that the function

\Delta k(u)
is
p
-increasing from
{B}_{u}
to
D
.
Let
x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{{B}_{u}}
, we have

\displaystyle\Delta k(u)(x)

\displaystyle=\Delta g(h_{1}(x+u),\dots,h_{n}(x+u))(f(x+u))-\Delta g(h_{1}(x),\dots,h_{n}(x))(f(x))

\displaystyle=\Delta g(h_{1}(x)+\Delta h_{1}(u)(x),\dots,h_{n}(x)+\Delta h_{n}(u)(x))(f(x)+\Delta f(u)(x))

\displaystyle\quad-\Delta g(h_{1}(x),\dots,h_{n}(x))(f(x))\text{\hskip 10.22217ptby definition of }\Delta h_{i}(u)

\displaystyle=\Delta g(\Delta f(u)(x),h_{1}(x)+\Delta h_{1}(u)(x),\dots,h_{n}(x)+\Delta h_{n}(u)(x))(f(x))

\displaystyle\quad+\Delta g(\Delta h_{1}(u)(x),h_{2}(x)+\Delta h_{2}(u)(x),\dots,h_{n}(x)+\Delta h_{n}(u)(x))(f(x)+h_{1}(x))

\displaystyle\quad+\Delta g(h_{1}(x),\Delta h_{2}(u)(x),h_{3}(x)+\Delta h_{3}(u)(x),\dots,

\displaystyle\hskip 180.00027pth_{n}(x)+\Delta h_{n}(u)(x))(f(x)+h_{2}(x))

\displaystyle\quad+\cdots+\Delta g(h_{1}(x),\dots,h_{n-1}(x),\Delta h_{n}(u)(x))(f(x)+h_{n}(x))

by Lemma
7.34
, observing that the first term of
the sum which appears in Lemma
7.34
is
annihilated by the subtraction above.

We can apply the inductive hypothesis to each of the terms of this
sum.
Let us consider for instance the first of these expressions:

\displaystyle\Delta g(\Delta f(u)(x),h_{1}(x)+\Delta h_{1}(u)(x),\dots,h_{n}(x)+\Delta h_{n}(u)(x))(f(x))\,.

We know that the functions
h^{\prime}_{1},\dots,h^{\prime}_{n+1}
defined by

h^{\prime}_{1}(x)=\Delta f(u)(x)
,

h^{\prime}_{2}(x)=h_{1}(x)+\Delta h_{1}(u)(x)=h_{1}(x+u)
,…,

h^{\prime}_{n+1}(x)=h_{n}(x)+\Delta h_{n}(u)(x)=h_{n}(x+u)
are totally
monotonic from
\underline{{B}_{u}}
to
\underline{C}
: this results from Lemma
7.28
.
Moreover we have

\forall x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\ f(x)+\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}h^{\prime}_{i}(x)=f(x+u)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}h_{i}(x+u)\in\mathcal{B}\underline{C}\,.

Therefore the inductive hypothesis applies and we know that the
function

x\mapsto\Delta g(\Delta f(u)(x),h_{1}(x)+\Delta h_{1}(u)(x),\dots,h_{p}(x)+\Delta h_{p}(u)(x))(f(x))

is
p
-increasing.
The same reasoning applies to all terms and hence the function

\Delta k(u)
is
p
-increasing from
\mathcal{B}\underline{{B}_{u}}
to

\underline{C}
, as contended.

Lemma 7.41
.

Let
f:\underline{B}\times\mathcal{B}\underline{C}\to\underline{D}
be linear in its
first argument and totally monotonic in its second argument.
Then, when restricted to
\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\times\mathcal{B}\underline{C}
, the function
f
is
totally monotonic.

Proof 7.42
.

Let
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

(x,y),(u_{1},v_{1}),\dots,(u_{n},v_{n})\in\underline{B}\times\underline{C}
be such that
(x,y)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}(u_{i},v_{i})\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\times\mathcal{B}\underline{C}
. For
\varepsilon\in\{\mathord{+},\mathord{-}\}
, we have

\displaystyle\Delta^{\varepsilon}f((u_{1},v_{1}),\dots,(u_{n},v_{n}))(x,y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}(n)}f(x+\sum_{i\in I}u_{i},y+\sum_{i\in I}v_{i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}(n)}f(x,y+\sum_{i\in I}v_{i})+\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}(n)}\sum_{j\in I}f(u_{j},y+\sum_{i\in I}v_{i})

by linearity of
f
in its first argument.
By total monotonicity of
f
in its second argument we have

\displaystyle\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{+}(n)}f(x,y+\sum_{i\in I}v_{i})\geq\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{-}(n)}f(x,y+\sum_{i\in I}v_{i})\,.

Next, assuming that
n>0
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}(n)}\sum_{j\in I}f(u_{j},y+\sum_{i\in I}v_{i})
