\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{I\in\Epset\epsilon n}{j\in I}}f(u_{j},y+\sum_{i\in I}v_{i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}(n-1)}f(u_{j},y+\sum_{i\in\mathsf{inj}_{j}(I)}v_{i})\text{ by Lemma~\ref{lemma:inset-corresp}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}(n-1)}f(u_{j},y+v_{j}+\sum_{i\in I}v(j)_{i})

where
(v(j)_{i})_{i=1}^{n-1}
is defined by

\displaystyle v(j)_{i}=\begin{cases}v_{i}&\text{if }i<j\\
v_{i+1}&\text{if }i\geq j\,.\end{cases}

By our assumption that
f
is totally monotonic in its second
argument we have, for each
j=1,\dots,n
,

\displaystyle\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{+}(n-1)}f(u_{j},y+v_{j}+\sum_{i\in I}v(j)_{i})\geq\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{-}(n-1)}f(u_{j},y+v_{j}+\sum_{i\in I}v(j)_{i})

from which it follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{+}(n)}\sum_{j\in I}f(u_{j},y+\sum_{i\in I}v_{i})\geq\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{-}(n)}\sum_{j\in I}f(u_{j},y+\sum_{i\in I}v_{i})

and hence

\displaystyle\Delta^{+}f((u_{1},v_{1}),\dots,(u_{n},v_{n}))(x,y)\geq\Delta^{-}f((u_{1},v_{1}),\dots,(u_{n},v_{n}))(x,y)

for
n>0
.
This inequation also holds trivially for
n=0
.

An immediate consequence of this lemma is the following observation
which will be useful in Section
8
.

Lemma 7.43
.

Let
f:\prod_{i=1}^{n}\underline{B_{i}}\to\underline{C}
be linear
in each of its
n
arguments.
Then the restriction of
f
to
\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{B}\underline{B_{i}}
is totally monotonic
\mathcal{B}\underline{\mathop{\&}_{i=1}^{n}B_{i}}\to\underline{C}
.

Proof 7.44
.

Simple induction on
n
using Lemma
7.41
.

Remark 7.45
.

In this statement, and other similar ones, we restrict
f
to the
unit ball not for deep reason but only because the notion of totally
monotonic function has been defined on unit balls, see
Definition
7.7
.

7.4.
The cartesian closed category of integrable cones and
stable and measurable functions

A quite remarkable property of total monotonicity is that it gives
rise to cartesian
closed
categories, as we will see in this
section.
We do not know if this phenomenon has been observed
before
[
EPT18b
]
.

Let
\mathbf{SCones}(B,C)
be the set of all stable and measurable functions
from
B
to
C
whose norm is
\leq 1
.

Theorem 7.46
.

If
f\in\mathbf{SCones}(B,C)
and
g\in\mathbf{SCones}(C,D)
then
g\mathrel{\circ}f\in\mathbf{SCones}(B,D)
.

Proof 7.47
.

The only non-obvious fact is that
g\mathrel{\circ}f
is totally monotonic,
which is obtained by Lemma
7.39
(applied with

n=0
).

So we have defined a category
\mathbf{SCones}
whose objects are the integrable
cones, and the morphisms are the stable and measurable functions.

Lemma 7.48
.

\mathbf{ICones}(B,C)\subseteq\mathbf{SCones}(B,C)
.

Proof 7.49
.

Indeed linearity clearly implies total monotonicity.

So we have a functor
\operatorname{\mathsf{Der}^{\mathsf{s}}}:\mathbf{ICones}\to\mathbf{SCones}
which acts as the
identity on objects and morphisms.
We can consider this functor as a forgetful functor since it forgets
linearity, whence its name: in
LL
the purpose of the

dereliction
rules allows to forget the linearity of morphisms.
The functor
\operatorname{\mathsf{Der}^{\mathsf{s}}}
is obviously faithful but of course not full:
see Examples
2.4

and
7.11
which provide nonlinear totally
monotonic functions.

Theorem 7.50
.

The category
\mathbf{SCones}
has all products and is cartesian closed.

Proof 7.51
.

If
(B_{i})_{i\in I}
is a family of integrable cones, we have
already defined
B=\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}B_{i}
which is the categorical
product of the
B_{i}
’s in
\mathbf{ICones}
(when equipped with the
projections
\mathsf{pr}_{i}\in\mathbf{ICones}(B,B_{i})
).
So
\operatorname{\mathsf{Der}^{\mathsf{s}}}(\mathsf{pr}_{i})\in\mathbf{SCones}(B,B_{i})
for each
i\in I
. Let

(f_{i}\in\mathbf{SCones}(C,B_{i}))_{i\in I}
, we define
f:\mathcal{B}\underline{C}\to\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
by
f(x)=(f_{i}(x))_{i\in I}
which is well defined by our assumption
that
\forall i\in I\ \left\|{f_{i}}\right\|\leq 1
.
Then
f
is easily seen to be stable because all the operations of

B
, as well as its cone order relation, are defined
componentwise.
Measurability of
f
is proven as in the proof of
Theorem
4.26
.
This shows that
B
is the categorical product of the
B_{i}
’s in

\mathbf{SCones}
.

Let
B
and
C
be integrable cones.
We have defined in Section
7.2
the integrable
cone
B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{s}}C
of stable and measurable functions
B\to C
,
we show that it is the internal hom of
B
and
C
in
\mathbf{SCones}
.
We define
\operatorname{\mathsf{Ev}}:\mathcal{B}\underline{\left({(B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{s}}C)}\mathrel{\&}{B}\right)}\to\underline{C}
by
\operatorname{\mathsf{Ev}}(f,x)=f(x)
.
The total monotonicity of
\operatorname{\mathsf{Ev}}
results from
Lemma
7.41
.
We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}\underline{\left({(B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{s}}C)}\mathrel{\&}{B}\right)}=\mathcal{B}\underline{(B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{s}}C)}\times\mathcal{B}\underline{B}

by definition of the norm in the categorical product.
It follows that
\left\|{\operatorname{\mathsf{Ev}}}\right\|\leq 1
.
We prove that
\operatorname{\mathsf{Ev}}
is measurable so let
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
and
\delta\in\mathcal{B}\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,\left({(B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{s}}C)}\mathrel{\&}{B}\right))}
which
means that
\delta=\langle{\eta,\beta}\rangle
with
\eta\in\mathcal{B}\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{s}}C)}
and
\beta\in\mathcal{B}\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,B)}
, we must prove that
\operatorname{\mathsf{Ev}}\mathrel{\circ}\delta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,C)}
so let
Y\in\mathbf{Ar}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{C}_{Y}
.
We must prove that

\displaystyle\varphi=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in Y\times X\cdot m(s,\operatorname{\mathsf{Ev}}(\delta(r)))

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in Y\times X\cdot m(s,\eta(r)(\beta(r)))

is measurable.
We have
p=(\beta\mathrel{\circ}\mathsf{pr}_{2})\mathrel{\triangleright}(m\mathrel{\circ}\mathsf{pr}_{1})\in\mathcal{M}^{B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{s}}C}_{Y\times X}
and by our assumption about
\eta
we know
that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\lambda}(r,s,r^{\prime})\in X\times Y\times X\cdot p(s,r,\eta(r^{\prime}))=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(r,s,r^{\prime})\in X\times Y\times X\cdot m(s,\eta(r^{\prime})(\beta(r)))

is measurable and hence so is
\varphi
and we have shown that
\operatorname{\mathsf{Ev}}\in\mathbf{SCones}({(B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{s}}C)}\mathrel{\&}{B},C)
.

We prove that
(B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{s}}C,\operatorname{\mathsf{Ev}})
is the internal hom of
B,C
in the cartesian
category
\mathbf{SCones}
.
So let
f\in\mathbf{SCones}({D}\mathrel{\&}{B},C)
.
For each given
z\in\mathcal{B}\underline{D}
we see easily that
g=\boldsymbol{\lambda}x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\cdot f(z,x)\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{s}}C}
, it remains to check that
g\in\mathbf{SCones}(D,B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{s}}C)
. Let us first check that
g
is totally monotonic so let
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
z,w_{1},\dots,w_{n}\in\underline{D}
be such that
z+\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\in\mathcal{B}\underline{D}
.
We must prove that

\displaystyle h^{-}=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{-}(n)}g(z+\sum_{i\in I}w_{i})\leq\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{+}(n)}g(z+\sum_{i\in I}w_{i})=h^{+}

in
\underline{B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{s}}C}
.
We use the characterization of the cone order in that cone
given by Lemma
7.14
.
So let
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
and let
x,u_{1},\dots,u_{k}\in\underline{B}
be such that
x+\sum_{j=1}^{k}u_{j}\in\mathcal{B}B
.
We must prove that

y^{-}=\sum_{J\in\mathcal{P}^{-}(k)}h^{+}(x+\sum_{i\in J}u_{j})+\sum_{J\in\mathcal{P}^{+}(k)}h^{-}(x+\sum_{i\in J}u_{j})\\
\leq\sum_{J\in\mathcal{P}^{+}(k)}h^{+}(x+\sum_{i\in J}u_{j})+\sum_{J\in\mathcal{P}^{-}(k)}h^{-}(x+\sum_{i\in J}u_{j})=y^{+}

in
\underline{C}
.
We have

\displaystyle y^{-}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{I\in\Ppset n}{J\in\Npset k}}g(z+\sum_{i\in I}w_{i},x+\sum_{i\in J}u_{j})+\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{I\in\Npset n}{J\in\Ppset k}}g(z+\sum_{i\in I}w_{i},x+\sum_{i\in J}u_{j})

\displaystyle y^{+}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{I\in\Ppset n}{J\in\Ppset k}}g(z+\sum_{i\in I}w_{i},x+\sum_{i\in J}u_{j})+\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{I\in\Npset n}{J\in\Npset k}}g(z+\sum_{i\in I}w_{i},x+\sum_{i\in J}u_{j})

Notice that
(\mathcal{P}^{+}(n)\times\mathcal{P}^{-}(k))\cap(\mathcal{P}^{-}(n)\times\mathcal{P}^{+}(k))=\emptyset

and that there is a bijection

\displaystyle(\mathcal{P}^{+}(n)\times\mathcal{P}^{-}(k))\cup(\mathcal{P}^{-}(n)\times\mathcal{P}^{+}(k))

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{P}^{-}(n+k)

\displaystyle(I,J)

\displaystyle\mapsto I\cup(J+n)

and similarly that
(\mathcal{P}^{+}(n)\times\mathcal{P}^{+}(k))\cap(\mathcal{P}^{-}(n)\times\mathcal{P}^{-}(k))=\emptyset

and that there is a bijection

\displaystyle(\mathcal{P}^{+}(n)\times\mathcal{P}^{+}(k))\cup(\mathcal{P}^{-}(n)\times\mathcal{P}^{-}(k))

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{P}^{+}(n+k)

\displaystyle(I,J)

\displaystyle\mapsto I\cup(J+n)\,.

We define a sequence
(w^{\prime}_{l},u^{\prime}_{l})_{l=1}^{n+k}
of elements of
\underline{D}\times\underline{B}
as follows:

\displaystyle(w^{\prime}_{l},u^{\prime}_{l})=\begin{cases}(w_{l},0)&\text{if }l\in\{1,\dots,n\}\\
(0,u_{l-n})&\text{if }l\in\{n+1,\dots,n+k\}\,.\end{cases}

so that
(z,x)+\sum_{l=1}^{n+k}(w^{\prime}_{l},u^{\prime}_{l})\in\mathcal{B}\underline{D}\times\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
.
With these notations, we have

\displaystyle y^{-}

\displaystyle=\sum_{K\in\mathcal{P}^{-}(n+k)}g((z,x)+\sum_{l\in K}(w^{\prime}_{l},u^{\prime}_{l}))

\displaystyle y^{+}

\displaystyle=\sum_{K\in\mathcal{P}^{+}(n+k)}g((z,x)+\sum_{l\in K}(w^{\prime}_{l},u^{\prime}_{l}))

and hence
y^{-}\leq y^{+}
since
g
is totally monotonic.

The
\omega
-continuity of
g
results from
Lemma
7.17
.
We prove that
g
is measurable
so let
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
and
\delta\in\mathcal{B}\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,D)}
, we must prove that
g\mathrel{\circ}\delta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{s}}C)}
so let
Y\in\mathbf{Ar}
and
p\in\mathcal{M}^{B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{s}}C}_{Y}
.
Let
\beta\in\mathcal{B}\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,B)}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{C}_{Y}
be such that
p=\beta\mathrel{\triangleright}m
, we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in Y\times X\cdot p(s,g(\delta(r)))

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in Y\times X\cdot m(s,g(\delta(r))(\beta(s)))

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in Y\times X\cdot m(s,f(\delta(r),\beta(s)))

and this map is measurable because
\langle{\delta\mathrel{\circ}\mathsf{pr}_{2},\beta\mathrel{\circ}\mathsf{pr}_{1}}\rangle\in\mathcal{B}\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y\times X,{D}\mathrel{\&}{B})}
and by
measurability of
f
.

Remark 7.52
.

Note that contrarily to
\mathbf{ICones}
it is very likely that the
category
\mathbf{SCones}
does not have all equalizers and therefore
is not complete.
For instance we have
f,g:\mathbf{SCones}(1,1)
given by

f(x)=x
and
g(x)=x^{2}
, and the set of all

x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{1}=[0,1]
such that
f(x)=g(x)
is

\{0,1\}
which does not look like a cone.
It would be interesting to understand if the set of solutions of
such an equation could be considered as some kind of manifold, with
a local structure of integrable cone, as in differential geometry.
The same observation applies to the category
\mathbf{ACones}
studied in
Section
8
.

So we have a functor
\_\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{s}}\_:{\mathbf{SCones}}^{\mathsf{op}}\times\mathbf{SCones}\to\mathbf{SCones}
mapping
(B,C)
to

B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{s}}C
and
f\in\mathbf{SCones}(B^{\prime},B),g\in\mathbf{SCones}(C,C^{\prime}))
to
f\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{s}}g\in\mathbf{SCones}(B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{s}}C,B^{\prime}\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{s}}C^{\prime})
which is given by
(f\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{s}}g)(h)=g\mathrel{\circ}h\mathrel{\circ}f
.
Observe that if
g\in\mathbf{ICones}(C,C^{\prime})
we have
f\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{s}}g\in\mathbf{ICones}(B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{s}}C,B^{\prime}\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{s}}C^{\prime})
so that in the
sequel we consider only
\_\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{s}}\_
as a functor

{\mathbf{ICones}}^{\mathsf{op}}\times\mathbf{ICones}\to\mathbf{ICones}
(using implicitly a
pre-composition with
\operatorname{\mathsf{Der}^{\mathsf{s}}}
).

Theorem 7.53
.

The functor
\operatorname{\mathsf{Der}^{\mathsf{s}}}:\mathbf{ICones}\to\mathbf{SCones}
preserves all limits.

Proof 7.54
.

By Theorem
4.32
it suffices to prove that

\operatorname{\mathsf{Der}^{\mathsf{s}}}
preserves all products and all equalizers.
Since products are defined in the same way in both categories, the
first property is obvious, let us check the second one.

Let
B,C
be objects of
\mathbf{ICones}
and
f,g\in\mathbf{ICones}(B,C)
, we
have already defined the equalizer
(E,e\in\mathbf{ICones}(E,B))
of

f,g
in the proof of Theorem
4.26
.
We just have to check that
(E,e)
is the equalizer of
f,g
in

\mathbf{SCones}
as well.
So let
H
be an integrable cone and
h\in\mathbf{SCones}(H,B)
be such that
f\mathrel{\circ}h=g\mathrel{\circ}h
.
This simply means that

h(\mathcal{B}\underline{H})\subseteq\mathcal{B}\underline{E}
from which it
follows that
h\in\mathbf{SCones}(H,E)
because the cone order relation
of
E
is the restriction of that of
B
to

\underline{E}\subseteq\underline{B}
(and similarly for the measurability
structure).
Let us call
h^{\prime}
this version of
h
ranging in

\mathcal{B}\underline{E}
instead of
\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
, so that

h=e\mathrel{\circ}h^{\prime}
.
It is obvious that
h^{\prime}
is the only morphism in
\mathbf{SCones}
having
this property.

The study of the exponential induced by this cartesian closed
structure on the category
\mathbf{ICones}
is developed in
Section
9
.

8.
Analytic and integrable functions on cones

Our goal now is to associate with
\mathbf{ICones}
another cartesian closed
category based on a notion of morphisms which are analytic in the sense
that they are limits of polynomial functions.
These analytic functions are actually stable and measurable, but their
definition is based on a notion of multilinear maps
21
21

21

As in
[
KT18
]
but without the support of
complex analysis.
in
\mathbf{ICones}
which preserve integrals so that analytic functions
have an implicit ‘‘integral preservation’’ property
22
22

22

A property that we don’t really know yet how to express
simply and directly in terms of the functions; of course it is not
plain integral preservation which cannot be expected from non-linear
maps.
We know that it is a property of the analytic functions themselves
because the symmetric multilinear functions of their Taylor
expansion at
0
are associated with analytic functions in a
unambiguous way by means of standard polarization formulas as we shall
see.

that general stable and measurable functions don’t have.

8.1.
The cone of multilinear and symmetric functions

The basic ingredient for defining our analytic functions is the notion
of multilinear morphisms.
More precisely, they will allow first to define homogeneous polynomial
functions (obtained by applying an
n
-linear function to
“diagonal” tuples
(x,\dots,x)
), and then analytic functions as
converging sums thereof.

Definition 8.1
.

Let
B_{1},\dots,B_{n},C
be integrable cones.
A function
f:\prod_{i=1}^{n}\underline{B_{i}}\to\underline{C}
is said to be
multilinear and continuous

if it is linear and continuous, separately, with respect to each of
its
n
arguments.

Observe that when this holds,
f
is bounded (use for instance
Lemma
2.14
and monoidal closedness in a
proof by induction on
n
).
One says that
f
is
symmetric
if
B_{1}=\cdots=B_{n}=B
and, for all
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
(the group
of permutations on
\{1,\dots,n\}
), one has

\displaystyle\forall x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in\underline{B}\quad f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=f(x_{\sigma(1)},\dots,x_{\sigma(n)})\,.

One says that
f
is
measurable
if for all
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
and
(\beta_{i}\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,B_{i})})_{i=1}^{n}
, one has
f\mathrel{\circ}\langle{\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{n}}\rangle\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,C)}
.
Lastly, one says that
f
is
integrable
(or that it
preserves integrals
)
if it is separately integrable with respect to each of its arguments.

The multilinear, continuous, symmetric, measurable and integrable
functions
\underline{B}^{n}\to\underline{C}
are easily seen to form a cone
\mathbf{Sym}_{n}(B,C)

with operations defined pointwise and norm defined by

\displaystyle\left\|{f}\right\|=\sup\{\left\|{f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})}\right\|\mid x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\}\,.

We equip this cone with a measurability structure

(\mathcal{M}^{\mathbf{Sym}_{n}(B,C)}_{X})_{X\in\mathbf{Ar}}
where
\mathcal{M}^{\mathbf{Sym}_{n}(B,C)}_{X}

is the set of all
p=\overrightarrow{\beta}\mathrel{\triangleright}m
where
\overrightarrow{\beta}=(\beta_{i}\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,B)})_{i=1}^{n}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{C}_{X}
, given by
p(f)=\boldsymbol{\lambda}r\in X\cdot m(r,f(\beta_{1}(r),\dots,\beta_{n}(r)))
.
The order relation in this cone is the pointwise order as easily
checked.

Notice last that this cone is integrable.
Let indeed
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
,
\mu\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}
and
\eta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,\mathbf{Sym}_{n}(B,C))}
and let us define a
function
f:\underline{B}^{n}\to\underline{C}
by

\displaystyle f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=\int\eta(r)(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\mu(dr)

then it is easy to check as usual that
f
is well defined,

f\in\underline{\mathbf{Sym}_{n}(B,C)}
and that
f=\int\eta(r)\mu(dr)
.

Remark 8.2
.

This integrable cone is a “subcone” of the integrable cone
{B\mathop{\otimes}\cdots\mathop{\otimes}B}\multimap{C}
, but we have not developed the
notion of subcone in the present paper.

Remark 8.3
.

We could define
\mathbf{Sym}_{n}(B,C)
more abstractly as follows: first,
generalizing Definition
5.9
, we define the integrable
cone of
n
-linear morphisms

\left({B_{1},\dots,B_{n}}\multimap{C}\right)=\left({B_{1}}\multimap{\cdots{}\multimap{{B_{n}}\multimap{C}}}\right)
,
then we observe that, when
B_{1}=\cdots=B_{n}=B
, for each permutation

\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
there is an automorphism on that cone which
acts by permutation of the arguments of
n
-linear functions, and
we define
\mathbf{Sym}_{n}(B,C)
as the equalizer of all these automorphisms
using the completeness of
\mathbf{ICones}
.
Of course we would have obtained the same object of
\mathbf{ICones}

(possibly up to an isomorphism), but the explicit description above
will be useful.

8.2.
The cone of homogeneous polynomial functions

As announced, this is the next basic concept in the definition of
analytic functions.

Definition 8.4
.

An
n
-homogeneous polynomial function from
B
to
C
is a
function
f:\underline{B}\to\underline{C}
such that there exists
h\in\underline{\mathbf{Sym}_{n}(B,C)}
satisfying

\displaystyle\forall x\in\underline{B}\quad f(x)=h(x,\dots,x)\,.

Then
h
is called a
linearization
of
f
.
We use
\underline{\mathbf{Hpol}}_{n}(B,C)
for the set of
n
-homogeneous polynomial
functions from
B
to
C
and set
\mathsf{M}_{n}(h)=f
.

Notice that we would define exactly the same class of functions
without requiring
h
to be symmetric.
We make this choice only to reduce the number of notions at hand.

Lemma 8.5
.

If
f\in\underline{\mathbf{Hpol}}_{n}(B,C)
then the restriction of
f
to
\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
is totally monotonic.

Proof 8.6
.

Let
h
be a linearization of
f
.
By Lemma
7.43
we know that the restriction
of
h
to
\mathcal{B}\underline{B}^{n}\to\underline{C}
is totally monotonic,
and since the diagonal map
d:\underline{B}\to\underline{B}^{n}
is linear of
norm
\leq 1
, the map
f=h\mathrel{\circ}d
is totally monotonic.

Lemma 8.7
.

An
n
-homogeneous polynomial function
f
has exactly one
linearization
\mathsf{L}_{n}f
.
Moreover

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathsf{L}_{n}f}\right\|\leq\frac{n^{n}}{{n}!}\left\|{f}\right\|

where
\left\|{f}\right\|=\sup_{x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}}\left\|{f(x)}\right\|
.

Notice that we also have
\left\|{f}\right\|\leq\left\|{\mathsf{L}_{n}f}\right\|
so that we
could interpret this lemma as expressing that the norms of

\underline{\mathbf{Sym}_{n}(B,C)}
and
\underline{\mathbf{Hpol}}_{n}(B,C)
are equivalent and
that these two cones are isomorphic in a weak sense (remember that in

\mathbf{ICones}
, isomorphisms must have norm

\leq 1
).

Proof 8.8
.

Let
f:\underline{B}\to\underline{C}
be an
n
-homogeneous polynomial and let
h\in\underline{\mathbf{Sym}_{n}(B,C)}
be a linearization of
f
.
By Lemma
8.5
we can define a function
h^{\prime}:\underline{B}^{n}\to\underline{C}
by

\displaystyle h^{\prime}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=\frac{1}{{n}!}\Big(\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{+}(n)}f(\sum_{i\in I}x_{i})-\sum_{I\in\mathcal{P}^{-}(n)}f(\sum_{i\in I}x_{i})\Big)=\frac{1}{{n}!}\Delta f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})(0)

and the usual proof of the polarization theorem shows that
necessarily
h^{\prime}=h
.
So
\mathsf{L}_{n}f=h
is completely determined by
f
, proving our
contention.

Next, given
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|{h(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{{n}!}\left\|{f(\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i})}\right\|\text{\hskip 10.22217ptby Lemma~\ref{lemma:fdiff-upper}}

\displaystyle=\frac{n^{n}}{{n}!}\left\|{h(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i},\dots,\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i})}\right\|

\displaystyle=\frac{n^{n}}{{n}!}\left\|{f(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i})}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{n^{n}}{{n}!}\left\|{f}\right\|

since
\left\|{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}}\right\|\leq 1
.

The set
\underline{\mathbf{Hpol}}_{n}(B,C)
is canonically a precone.
Indeed if
f,g\in\underline{\mathbf{Hpol}}_{n}(B,C)
then
f+g
(defined pointwise)
belongs to
\underline{\mathbf{Hpol}}_{n}(B,C)
because clearly

f+g=\mathsf{M}_{n}(\mathsf{L}_{n}f+\mathsf{L}_{n}g)
and we know that

\mathsf{L}_{n}f+\mathsf{L}_{n}g\in\mathbf{Sym}_{n}(B,C)
.
Multiplication by a scalar in
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is dealt with similarly.
Notice that this reasoning also shows that the maps
\mathsf{L}_{n}:\underline{\mathbf{Hpol}}_{n}(B,C)\to\underline{\mathbf{Sym}_{n}(B,C)}
and
\mathsf{M}_{n}:\underline{\mathbf{Sym}_{n}(B,C)}\to\underline{\mathbf{Hpol}}_{n}(B,C)
are linear.

We define
\left\|{\_}\right\|_{\underline{\mathbf{Hpol}}_{n}(B,C)}
as usual by
\left\|{f}\right\|_{\underline{\mathbf{Hpol}}_{n}(B,C)}=\sup_{x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}}\left\|{f(x)}\right\|_{C}
so that clearly
\left\|{f}\right\|_{\underline{\mathbf{Hpol}}_{n}(B,C)}\leq\left\|{\mathsf{L}_{n}f}\right\|_{\mathbf{Sym}_{n}(B,C)}
.
Equipped with this norm,
\underline{\mathbf{Hpol}}_{n}(B,C)
is a cone: the only non obvious
property is completeness, so let
(f_{k}\in\mathcal{B}\underline{\mathbf{Hpol}}_{n}(B,C))_{k=1}^{\infty}
be an increasing sequence
and let
f:\underline{B}\to\underline{C}
be the pointwise lub of this sequence
which is well defined since for each
x\in\underline{B}
we have

\displaystyle\left\|{f_{k}(x)}\right\|\leq\left\|{\mathsf{L}_{n}{f_{k}}}\right\|\left\|{x}\right\|^{n}\leq\frac{n^{n}}{{n}!}\left\|{f_{k}}\right\|\left\|{x}\right\|^{n}\leq\frac{n^{n}}{{n}!}\left\|{x}\right\|^{n}\,.

The sequence
(\mathsf{L}_{n}{f_{k}})_{k=1}^{\infty}
is increasing in
\frac{n^{n}}{{n}!}\mathcal{B}\underline{\mathbf{Sym}_{n}(B,C)}
and has therefore
a lub
h\in\mathcal{B}\underline{\mathbf{Sym}_{n}(B,C)}
and remember that this lub is
defined pointwise on
\underline{B}^{n}
.
It follows that
f=\mathsf{M}_{n}(h)\in\underline{\mathbf{Hpol}}_{n}(B,C)
and that we have

\displaystyle\forall x\in\underline{B}\quad f(x)=\sup_{k}f_{k}(x)\,.

Since
\forall k\ f_{k}\leq f
by monotonicity of
\mathsf{M}_{n}
it
follows that
f=\sup_{k}f_{k}
.
Last observe that
\left\|{f}\right\|\leq 1
which ends the proof of

\omega
-completeness of
\mathcal{B}\underline{\mathbf{Hpol}}_{n}(B,C)
.

So we have shown that
\underline{\mathbf{Hpol}}_{n}(B,C)
is a cone, and also that the
linear maps
\mathsf{L}_{n}
and
\mathsf{M}_{n}
are
\omega
-continuous.

Remark 8.9
.

It is important to notice that, contrarily to
\mathbf{Sym}_{n}(B,C)
, the cone order relation of
\underline{\mathbf{Hpol}}_{n}(B,C)
is
not the pointwise order.
As an example take
n=2
,
B={1}\mathrel{\&}{1}
,
C=1
, and
consider
f,g\in\underline{\mathbf{Hpol}}_{n}(B,C)
given by
f(x,y)=2xy
and

g(x,y)=x^{2}+y^{2}
.
Then

\displaystyle\mathsf{L}_{2}f((x_{1},y_{1}),(x_{2},y_{2}))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(f(x_{1}+x_{2},y_{1}+y_{2})-f(x_{1},y_{1})-f(x_{2},y_{2})))

\displaystyle=x_{1}y_{2}+x_{2}y_{1}

and similarly
\mathsf{L}_{2}g((x_{1},y_{1}),(x_{2},y_{2}))=x_{1}x_{2}+y_{1}y_{2}
and
therefore we do not have
\mathsf{L}_{2}f\leq\mathsf{L}_{2}g
(we have
\mathsf{L}_{2}f((1,0),(0,1))=1
and

\mathsf{L}_{2}g((1,0),(0,1))=0
) whereas

\forall(x,y)\in\underline{{1}\mathrel{\&}{1}}=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\ f(x,y)\leq g(x,y)
.

Given
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
,
\beta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,B)}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{C}_{X}
we define

\beta\mathrel{\triangleright}m:X\times\underline{\mathbf{Hpol}}_{n}(B,C)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
as usual by

(\beta\mathrel{\triangleright}m)(r,f)={m(r,f(\beta(r)))}
for all

f\in\underline{\mathbf{Hpol}}_{n}(B,C)
.
Notice that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\lambda}r\in X\cdot m(r,f(\beta(r)))=\boldsymbol{\lambda}r\in X\cdot m(r,\mathsf{L}_{n}f(\overline{\beta(r)}^{{n}}))

and this function is measurable because
\mathsf{L}_{n}f\in\underline{\mathbf{Sym}_{n}(B,C)}
, which implies that
\mathsf{L}_{n}f\mathrel{\circ}\langle{\beta,\dots,\beta}\rangle\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,C)}
.
Then it is easily checked that setting
\mathcal{M}_{X}=\{\beta\mathrel{\triangleright}m\mid\beta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,B)}\text{ and
}m\in\mathcal{M}^{C}_{X}\}
we define a measurability structure on
\underline{\mathbf{Hpol}}_{n}(B,C)
so that
E=(\underline{\mathbf{Hpol}}_{n}(B,C),(\mathcal{M}_{X})_{X\in\mathbf{Ar}})
is a
measurable cone that we denote as
\mathbf{Hpol}_{n}(B,C)
.

Lemma 8.10
.

\mathsf{L}_{n}\in\mathbf{MCones}(\underline{\mathbf{Hpol}}_{n}(B,C),\frac{n^{n}}{{n}!}\mathbf{Sym}_{n}(B,C))
.

Proof 8.11
.

In view of what we know about
\mathsf{L}_{n}
, it suffices to prove
that
\mathsf{L}_{n}:\underline{\mathbf{Hpol}_{n}(B,C)}\to\underline{\mathbf{Sym}_{n}(B,C)}
is
measurable so let
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
and

\eta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,\mathbf{Hpol}_{n}(B,C))}
, we must check that

\mathsf{L}_{n}\mathrel{\circ}\eta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,\mathbf{Sym}_{n}(B,C))}
.
Let
Y\in\mathbf{Ar}
and
p\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathbf{Sym}_{n}(B,C)}_{Y}
.
We have
p=\overrightarrow{\beta}\mathrel{\triangleright}m
where
\overrightarrow{\beta}\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,B)}^{n}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{B}_{Y}
so that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in Y\times X\cdot p(s,\mathsf{L}_{n}(\eta(r)))=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in Y\times X\cdot m(s,\mathsf{L}_{n}(\eta(r))(\overrightarrow{\beta}(s)))

which, coming back to the characterization of
\mathsf{L}_{n}
explicitly
provided in the proof of Lemma
8.7
,
is measurable by measurability of addition and subtraction in

\mathbb{R}
and linearity of
m(s,\_)
.

Theorem 8.12
.

\mathbf{Hpol}_{n}(B,C)
is an integrable cone and

\mathsf{L}_{n}\in\mathbf{ICones}(\underline{\mathbf{Hpol}}_{n}(B,C),\frac{n^{n}}{{n}!}\mathbf{Sym}_{n}(B,C))\,.

Proof 8.13
.

We prove integrability of
E=\mathbf{Hpol}_{n}(B,C)
so let
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
,
\eta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,E)}
and

\mu\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}
, we define
f:\underline{B}\to\underline{C}
by
f(x)=\int\eta(r)(x)\mu(dr)
using the fact that
C
is an
integrable cone.
Since
\mathsf{L}_{n}
is measurable we can define

h:\underline{B}^{n}\to\underline{C}
by

\displaystyle h(\overrightarrow{x})=\int\mathsf{L}_{n}(\eta(r))(\overrightarrow{x})\mu(dr)\,.

which is clearly symmetric.
It is
n
-linear,
\omega
-continuous, measurable by
Lemma
4.10
, and integrable by the first
statement of Theorem
6.1
.
So we have
h\in\underline{\mathbf{Sym}_{n}(B,C)}
and

h(x,\dots,x)=\int\eta(r)(x)\mu(dx)=f(x)
for all
x\in\underline{B}

which proves that
f\in\underline{\mathbf{Hpol}_{n}(B,C)}
.
Last let
p\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathbf{Hpol}_{n}(B,C)}_{0}
so that
p=x\mathrel{\triangleright}m
for some
x\in\underline{B}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{C}_{0}
.
We have
p(f)=m(f(x))=m(\int\eta(r)(x)\mu(dx))=\int m(\eta(r)(x))\mu(dr)

by definition of an integral in
C
.
So
p(f)=\int p(\eta(r))\mu(dr)
by definition of
p
, which
shows that
f
is the integral of
\eta
in
\mathbf{Hpol}_{n}(B,C)
and
hence that this measurable cone is also integrable.

The integrability of
\mathsf{L}_{n}
results from its definition and
from the fact that integrals commute with finite sums and
differences.

8.3.
The cone of analytic functions

We can finally define and study our analytic functions.

Definition 8.14
.

A function
f:\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\to\underline{C}
is analytic if it is
bounded, and there is a sequence
(f_{n}\in\underline{\mathbf{Hpol}}_{n}(B,C))_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}

such that

\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\quad f(x)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}f_{n}(x)\,.

(6)

Such a sequence
(f_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is called a
homogeneous
polynomial decomposition
of
f
.

Notice that the precise meaning of (
6
) is that,
for all
x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
, the increasing sequence

(\sum_{n=0}^{N}f_{n}(x))_{N\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is bounded in
\underline{C}
(in the
sense of the norm) and has
f(x)
as lub.

Lemma 8.15
.

If
f:\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\to\underline{C}
is analytic, then
f
has
exactly one homogeneous polynomial decomposition.

Proof 8.16
.

Let
(f_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be a homogeneous polynomial decomposition
of an analytic
f
that we can assume without loss of generality to range in

\mathcal{B}\underline{C}
since
f
is bounded. Let
x^{\prime}\in\underline{{B}^{\prime}}
and
x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
.
Let

\displaystyle\varphi:[0,1]

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\mapsto x^{\prime}(f(tx))\,.

We have
\varphi(t)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}x^{\prime}(f_{n}(x))t^{n}
by linearity and
continuity of
x^{\prime}
and hence

\displaystyle\forall n\in{\mathbb{N}}\quad x^{\prime}(f_{n}(x))=\frac{1}{{n}!}\varphi^{(n)}(0)=\frac{1}{{n}!}{\frac{d^{n}}{dt^{n}}x^{\prime}(f(tx))}\mid_{t=0}

so that if
(g_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is another homogeneous polynomial
decomposition of
f
we have
x^{\prime}(f_{n}(x))=x^{\prime}(g_{n}(x))
for all

x
,
n
and
x^{\prime}
.
Since this holds in particular for all
x^{\prime}=m\in\mathcal{M}^{B}_{0}
our
claim is proven by
Definition 3.2
.
.

If
f:\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\to C
is analytic, we use
\mathsf{P}_{n}(f)
for
the
n
th component of its unique homogeneous polynomial
decomposition and we set
\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}={n}!(\mathsf{L}_{n}\mathrel{\circ}\mathsf{P}_{n})

so that
\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}f\in\underline{\mathbf{Sym}_{n}(B,C)}
and we have

\displaystyle f(x)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{n}!}\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}f(\overline{x}^{{n}})

which can be understood as the Taylor expansion of
f
, motivating
the notation:
\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}f
can be understood as the
n
th derivative of
f
at

0
, which is an
n
-linear symmetric function.
As usual we say that
f
is measurable if, for all
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
and
\beta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,B)}
, one has
f\mathrel{\circ}\beta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,C)}
.

We define now a cone of analytic and measurable functions
\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\to\underline{C}
so let
P
be the set of these
functions.
We define the algebraic operations on
P
pointwise: if
f,g\in P
then
f+g\in P
since
(f+g)(x)=f(x)+g(x)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(\mathsf{P}_{n}f(x)+\mathsf{P}_{n}g(x))
by
continuity of addition.
Notice that if
f,g\in P
then

\displaystyle f\leq g\Leftrightarrow\forall n\in{\mathbb{N}}\quad\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}f\leq\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}g\,.

since
f\leq g
means that
\forall x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\ f(x)\leq g(x)

and
\boldsymbol{\lambda}x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\cdot(g(x)-f(x))\in P
.
Each map
\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}:P\to\underline{\mathbf{Sym}_{n}(B,C)}
is linear by Lemmas
8.7

and
8.15
.

We set as usual

\displaystyle\left\|{f}\right\|=\sup\{\left\|{f(x)}\right\|\mid x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\}

and define in that way a cone.
Let indeed
(f^{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be an increasing sequence in

\mathcal{B}P
.
For each
k,n\in{\mathbb{N}}
we have
\left\|{\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}{f^{k}}}\right\|\leq{n^{n}}
by Lemma
8.10
and
the sequence
(\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}{f^{k}})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is increasing and hence has a lub
h_{n}\in\underline{\mathbf{Sym}_{n}(B,C)}
and we have

\displaystyle\forall x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in\underline{B}\quad h_{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=\sup_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}{f^{k}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\,.

In particular we can define the homogeneous polynomial map
f_{n}=\mathsf{M}_{n}(\frac{1}{{n}!}h_{n})
, which means

\displaystyle f_{n}(x)=\frac{1}{{n}!}h_{n}(\overline{x}^{{n}})=\sup_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\frac{1}{{n}!}\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}{f^{k}}(\overline{x}^{{n}})\,.

Let
f:\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\to\underline{C}
be defined by

f(x)=\sup_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}f^{k}(x)
, we have

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle=\sup_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}f^{k}(x)

\displaystyle=\sup_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\sum_{n=0}\frac{1}{{n}!}\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}{f^{k}}(\overline{x}^{{n}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}\sup_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\frac{1}{{n}!}\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}{f^{k}}(\overline{x}^{{n}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}f_{n}(x)

which shows that
f
is analytic and is the lub
(f^{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}

in
\mathcal{B}P
since
f
is clearly measurable (as usual by the
monotone convergence theorem).

Then we define a family
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{M}_{X})_{X\in\mathbf{Ar}}
of sets of measurability tests by
stipulating that
p\in\mathcal{M}_{X}
if
p=\beta\mathrel{\triangleright}m
where
\beta\in\mathcal{B}\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,B)}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{C}_{X}
, and, if
f\in P
and
r\in X
then
p(r,f)=m(r,f(\beta(r)))
.
It is easily checked that
(P,\mathcal{M})
is a measurable cone, that we
denote as
B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{a}}C
.

We check that
B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{a}}C
is integrable so let
\eta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{a}}C)}
for some
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
and
let
\mu\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}
.
We define a function
f:\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\to\underline{C}
by

\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\quad f(x)=\int^{C}_{X}\eta(r)(x)\mu(dr)\,.

This function is well defined because for each given
x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
one has
\boldsymbol{\lambda}r\in X\cdot\eta(r)(x)\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,C)}
. For each
r\in X
we can write

\displaystyle\eta(r)(x)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{n}!}\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}(\eta(r))(\overline{x}^{{n}})

and hence

\displaystyle f(x)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{n}!}\int^{C}_{X}\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}(\eta(r))(\overline{x}^{{n}})\mu(dr)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{n}!}\Big(\int^{\mathbf{Sym}_{n}(B,C)}_{X}\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}(\eta(r))\mu(dr)\Big)(\overline{x}^{{n}})

by definition of integrals in the integrable cone
\mathbf{Sym}_{n}(B,C)
, and
hence
f\in\underline{B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{a}}C}
.
Let
p=(x\mathrel{\triangleright}m)\in\mathcal{M}^{B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{a}}C}_{0}
where

x\in\underline{B}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{C}_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle p(f)

\displaystyle=m(f(x))

\displaystyle=m\Big(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{n}!}\int\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}(\eta(r))(\overline{x}^{{n}})\mu(dr)\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{n}!}m\Big(\int\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}(\eta(r))(\overline{x}^{{n}})\mu(dr)\Big)\text{\quad by lin.~and cont.~of }m

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{n}!}\int m(\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}(\eta(r))(\overline{x}^{{n}}))\mu(dr)\text{\quad by def.~of integrals in }C

\displaystyle=\int\Big(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{n}!}m(\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}(\eta(r))(\overline{x}^{{n}}))\Big)\mu(dr)\text{\quad by the monotone convergence th.}

\displaystyle=\int m\Big(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{n}!}\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}(\eta(r))(\overline{x}^{{n}})\Big)\mu(dr)

\displaystyle=\int m(\eta(r)(x))\mu(dr)=\int p(\eta(r))\mu(dr)

which shows that
f=\int\eta(r)\mu(dr)
, and hence the measurable cone
B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{a}}C
is integrable.

Lemma 8.17
.

For each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the function
\mathsf{P}_{n}:\underline{B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{a}}C}\to\underline{\mathbf{Hpol}_{n}(B,C)}
is linear,
continuous, measurable, integrable and has norm
\leq 1
.

Proof 8.18
.

Linearity and continuity result straightforwardly from the fact that
the homogeneous polynomial decomposition
(f_{n}=\mathsf{P}_{n}(f))_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of

f
is uniquely determined by its defining property:

\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\quad f(x)=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}f_{n}(x)\,.

Let
\eta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{a}}C)}
, we must check next
that
\mathsf{P}_{n}\mathrel{\circ}\eta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,\mathbf{Hpol}_{n}(B,C))}
so let
Y\in\mathbf{Ar}
,

\beta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,B)}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{C}_{Y}
, we must prove that

\displaystyle\theta

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in Y\times X\cdot(\beta\mathrel{\triangleright}m)(s,\mathsf{P}_{n}(\eta(r)))

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in Y\times X\cdot m(s,\mathsf{P}_{n}(\eta(r))(\beta(s)))

is measurable
Y\times X\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.
This results from the fact that

\displaystyle\theta(s,r)=\frac{1}{{n}!}{\frac{d^{n}}{dt^{n}}m(s,\eta(r,t\beta(s)))}\mid_{t=0}

and from the measurability and smoothness wrt.
t
of the
map
(s,r,t)\mapsto m(s,\eta(r,t\varphi(s)))
.
Indeed the following is standard: if
Z
is a measurable space
then if a function
Z\times[0,1)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is measurable,
and smooth in its second argument, then so is its derivative
wrt. its second argument.

Last we check integrability of
\mathsf{P}_{n}
so let moreover
\mu\in\mathsf{FMeas}(X)
, and let
p\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathbf{Hpol}_{n}(B,C)}_{0}

so that
p=x\mathrel{\triangleright}m
for some
x\in\underline{B}
and

m\in\mathcal{M}^{C}_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle p\Big(\mathsf{P}_{n}\Big(\int^{\mathbf{Hpol}_{n}(B,C)}_{Y}\eta(s)\mu(ds)\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{{n}!}{\frac{d^{n}}{dt^{n}}m\Big(\int^{C}_{Y}\eta(s,tx)\mu(ds)\Big)}\mid_{t=0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{{n}!}{\Big(\frac{d^{n}}{dt^{n}}\int_{Y}m(\eta(s,tx))\mu(ds)\Big)}\mid_{t=0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{{n}!}\int_{Y}{\frac{d^{n}}{dt^{n}}m(\eta(s,tx))}\mid_{t=0}\mu(ds)

\displaystyle=\int_{Y}p(\mathsf{P}_{n}(\eta(s)))\mu(ds)

by standard properties of integration.
The fact that
\left\|{\mathsf{P}_{n}}\right\|\leq 1
results from the obvious fact
that
\mathsf{P}_{n}f(x)\leq f(x)
for all
x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
.

Theorem 8.19
.

For all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
we have
\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}\in\mathbf{ICones}(B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{a}}C,n^{n}\mathbf{Sym}_{n}(B,C))
.

Proof 8.20
.

Remember that
\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}={n}!(\mathsf{L}_{n}\mathrel{\circ}\mathsf{P}_{n})
and apply Theorem
8.12
and Lemma
8.17
.

Theorem 8.21
.

Each analytic function is stable and measurable.

Proof 8.22
.

Immediate consequence of the definition of analytic functions and of
Lemma
8.5
.

Remark 8.23
.

The converse is not true, as shown by
Remark
9.10
.
Indeed since the stable and measurable function
\mathsf{cont}
introduced in
Remark
2.7
is actually linear, if

\mathsf{cont}
were analytic we would have

\mathsf{D}^{(1)}_{0}\mathsf{cont}=\mathsf{cont}
and

\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}\mathsf{cont}=0
if
n\not=1
which is not possible
since
\mathsf{cont}
does not preserve integrals.

8.4.
The category of integrable cones and analytic functions

Our goal in this section is to show that integrable cones, together
with analytic functions as morphisms, form a category which is
cartesian closed.

8.4.1.
Composing analytic functions

We start with a special case of composition that we can think of as
the restriction of an analytic function to a
local cone
in the
sense of Section
7.1
.

Theorem 8.24
.

Let
x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
and let
f\in\underline{B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{a}}C}
. Then the function
g:\mathcal{B}\underline{{B}_{x}}\to\underline{C}
defined
by
g(u)=f(x+u)
is analytic, that is
g\in\underline{{B}_{x}\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{a}}C}
.

Proof 8.25
.

Given
u\in\mathcal{B}\underline{{B}_{x}}
we have

\displaystyle g(u)

\displaystyle=f(x+u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{n}!}\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}f(\overline{x+u}^{{n}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{n}!}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\genfrac{(}{)}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}f(\overline{u}^{{n-k}},\overline{x}^{{k}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{1}{{k}!{(n-k)}!}\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}f(\overline{u}^{{n-k}},\overline{x}^{{k}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{k}!{(n-k)}!}\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}f(\overline{u}^{{n-k}},\overline{x}^{{k}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{k}!{l}!}\mathsf{D}^{(l+k)}_{0}f(\overline{u}^{{l}},\overline{x}^{{k}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{l}!}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{k}!}\mathsf{D}^{(l+k)}_{0}f(\overline{u}^{{l}},\overline{x}^{{k}})

so it suffices to show that for each
l\in{\mathbb{N}}
the function
g_{l}:\mathcal{B}\underline{{B}_{x}}\to\underline{C}
defined by

\displaystyle g_{l}(u)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{k}!}\mathsf{D}^{(l+k)}_{0}f(\overline{u}^{{l}},\overline{x}^{{k}})

satisfies
g_{l}(u)=\varphi_{l}(\overline{u}^{{l}})
for some

\varphi_{l}\in\mathbf{Sym}_{l}({B}_{x},C)
. We show that we can set

\displaystyle\varphi_{l}(\overrightarrow{u})=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{k}!}\mathsf{D}^{(l+k)}_{0}f(\overrightarrow{u},\overline{x}^{{k}})

for all
\overrightarrow{u}=(u_{1},\dots,u_{l})\in\underline{{B}_{x}}^{l}
. So let
\overrightarrow{u}=(u_{1},\dots,u_{l})\in\underline{{B}_{x}}^{l}
and let
\lambda\geq\max_{i=1}^{l}\left\|{u_{i}}\right\|_{\underline{{B}_{x}}}
be such that

\lambda>0
so that for each
i
we have

\frac{1}{\lambda}u_{i}\in\mathcal{B}\underline{{B}_{x}}
.

For
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
let
\varphi_{l}^{N}(\overrightarrow{u})=\sum_{k=0}^{N}\frac{1}{{k}!}\mathsf{D}^{(l+k)}_{0}f(\overrightarrow{u},\overline{x}^{{k}})
so that
\varphi_{l}^{N}\in\underline{\mathbf{Sym}_{l}({B}_{x},C)}
; actually we even have
\varphi_{l}^{N}\in\underline{\mathbf{Sym}_{l}(B,C)}
. Observe that, setting
u=\frac{1}{l\lambda}\sum_{i=1}^{l}u_{i}\in\mathcal{B}\underline{{B}_{x}}
we
have
u_{i}\leq l\lambda u
for each
i=1,\dots,l
so that

\displaystyle\varphi_{l}^{N}(\overrightarrow{u})

\displaystyle\leq\varphi_{l}^{N}(\overline{l\lambda u}^{{l}})=(l\lambda)^{l}\varphi_{l}^{N}(\overline{u}^{{l}})

\displaystyle\leq(l\lambda)^{l}g(u)=(l\lambda)^{l}f(x+u)

so that
\left\|{\varphi_{l}^{N}(\overrightarrow{u})}\right\|_{C}\leq(l\lambda)^{l}\left\|{f}\right\|
and
since neither
l
nor
\lambda
depend on
N
the sequence
(\varphi_{l}^{N}(\overrightarrow{u}))_{N\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is increasing in
(l\lambda)^{l}\mathcal{B}\underline{C}
, it has a lub which is
\varphi_{l}(\overrightarrow{u})
which is therefore well-defined and belongs to
(l\lambda)^{l}\mathcal{B}\underline{C}
.
The fact that the map
\varphi_{l}:\underline{{B}_{x}}\to\underline{C}
defined in that way is
l
-linear symmetric and
\omega
-continuous results from the

\omega
-continuity of addition, scalar multiplication and from the
basic properties of lubs.
The measurability and integrability of
\varphi_{l}
result as usual
from the monotone convergence theorem.

Let
f\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{a}}C}
and
g\in\underline{C\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{a}}D}
, since
g(\mathcal{B}\underline{B})\subseteq\mathcal{B}\underline{C}
, the function
g\mathrel{\circ}f:\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\to\underline{D}
is well defined and bounded.
We assume first that
f(0)=0
so that the first term of the Taylor
expansion of
f
vanishes and we have

\displaystyle g(f(x))

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{n}!}\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}g\Big(\overline{\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{k}!}\mathsf{D}^{(k)}_{0}f(\overline{x}^{{k}})}^{{n}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{n}!}\sum_{\sigma:[n]\to{{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}}\frac{{n}!}{{\sigma}!}\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}g(\mathsf{D}^{(\sigma(1))}_{0}f(\overline{x}^{{\sigma(1)}}),\dots,\mathsf{D}^{(\sigma(n))}_{0}f(\overline{x}^{{\sigma(n)}}))

by multilinearity and continuity of the
\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}f
’s, with the
notation
{\sigma}!=\prod_{i=1}^{n}{\sigma(i)}!
.
If
n,l\in{\mathbb{N}}
we define
\mathsf{L}(n,l)
as the set of all
\sigma:[n]=\{1,\dots,n\}\to{{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}
such that

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma(i)=l
.
This set is finite and empty as soon as
n>l
(it is for obtaining
this effect that we have assumed that
f(0)=0
).
We have

\displaystyle g(f(x))

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{l}!}\sum_{n=0}^{l}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{L}(n,l)}\frac{{l}!}{{\sigma}!}\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}f(\mathsf{D}^{(\sigma(1))}_{0}g(\overline{x}^{{\sigma(1)}}),\dots,\mathsf{D}^{(\sigma(n))}_{0}g(\overline{x}^{{\sigma(n)}}))\,.

For each
l\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the function

\displaystyle h_{l}:\underline{B}^{l}

\displaystyle\to\underline{D}

\displaystyle(x_{1},\dots,x_{l})

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{n=0}^{l}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{L}(n,l)}\frac{{l}!}{{\sigma}!}\mathsf{D}^{(l)}_{0}f(\mathsf{D}^{(\sigma(1))}_{0}g(x_{1},\dots,x_{\sigma(1)}),\dots,\mathsf{D}^{(\sigma(n))}_{0}g(x_{l-\sigma(n)+1},\dots,x_{l}))

is
l
-linear, measurable and integrable as a finite sum of such
functions, however it is not necessarily symmetric (for instance, for

l=4
, this sum contains the expression

\frac{{4}!}{({2}!)^{2}}\mathsf{D}^{(2)}_{0}f(\mathsf{D}^{(2)}_{0}g(x_{1},x_{2}),\mathsf{D}^{(2)}_{0}g(x_{3},x_{4}))
), but not

\frac{{4}!}{({2}!)^{2}}\mathsf{D}^{(2)}_{0}f(\mathsf{D}^{(2)}_{0}g(x_{1},x_{3}),\mathsf{D}^{(2)}_{0}g(x_{2},x_{4}))
), so we set

\displaystyle k_{l}(\overrightarrow{x})=\frac{1}{{l}!}\sum_{\theta\in\mathfrak{S}_{l}}h_{l}(x_{\theta(1)},\dots,x_{\theta(l)})

and
k_{l}
is again a finite sum of
l
-linear, measurable and
integrable functions and hence obviously belongs to

\underline{\mathbf{Sym}_{l}(B,D)}
, and we have

\displaystyle g(f(x))=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{l}!}k_{l}(\overline{x}^{{l}})

for each
x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
which proves that
g\mathrel{\circ}f
is analytic since this function is obviously bounded.

Now we don’t assume anymore that
f(0)=0
, and we define an
obviously analytic function

f_{0}\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\to\mathcal{B}\underline{{C}_{f(0)}}
by

f_{0}(x)=f(x)-f(0)
. By Lemma
8.24
the function
g_{0}:\mathcal{B}\underline{{C}_{f(0)}}\to\underline{D}
given by
g_{0}(v)=g(f(0)+v)
is analytic and hence
g\mathrel{\circ}f=g_{0}\mathrel{\circ}f_{0}
is analytic since
f_{0}(0)=0
.
The measurability of
g\mathrel{\circ}f
is obvious so

g\mathrel{\circ}f\in\underline{B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{a}}D}
.

This shows that we have defined a category
\mathbf{ACones}
whose objects are the integrable cones and where a
morphism from
B
to
C
is a
f\in\underline{B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{a}}C}
such
that
\left\|{f}\right\|\leq 1
.
We aim now at proving that this category is cartesian closed.

Lemma 8.26
.

For all measurable cones
B,C
we have

\mathbf{ICones}(B,C)\subseteq\mathbf{ACones}(B,C)
.

This is obvious and shows that there is a forgetful faithful functor

\operatorname{\mathsf{Der}^{\mathsf{a}}}:\mathbf{ICones}\to\mathbf{ACones}
which acts as the identity on objects
and morphisms.

Proposition 8.27
.

The category
\mathbf{ACones}
has all (small) products.

Proof 8.28
.

We already know that each family
(B_{i})_{i\in I}
of integrable
cones has a product
B=\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}B_{i}
in
\mathbf{ICones}
with
projections
(\mathsf{pr}_{i}\in\mathbf{ICones}(B,B_{i}))_{i\in I}
.
We show that
B
is also the product of the family

(B_{i})_{i\in I}
with projections
(\operatorname{\mathsf{Der}^{\mathsf{a}}}(\mathsf{pr}_{i}))_{i\in I}

in
\mathbf{ACones}
. Remember that an element of
\underline{B}
is a family
(x_{i}\in\underline{B_{i}})_{i\in I}
such that the family
(\left\|{x_{i}}\right\|_{B_{i}})_{i\in I}
is bounded in
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.

So let
(f_{i}\in\mathbf{ACones}(C,B_{i}))_{i\in I}
, it suffices to prove
that the function
f:\mathcal{B}\underline{C}\to\underline{B}
given by
f(y)=(f_{i}(y))_{i\in I}
belongs to
\mathbf{ACones}(C,B)
.
The fact that
\forall y\in\mathcal{B}\underline{C}\ f(y)\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
results
from the definition of the norm of
B
and from the fact that

\forall i\in I\ \left\|{f_{i}}\right\|\leq 1
.
The measurability of
f
results trivially from its definition and
from the definition of
\mathcal{M}^{B}
.
We know that
f_{i}(y)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{n}!}\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}{f_{i}}(\overline{y}^{{n}})
. For each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
the map
\varphi_{n}:\underline{C}^{n}\to\underline{B}
defined by
\varphi_{n}(\overrightarrow{y})=(\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}{f_{i}}(\overrightarrow{y}))_{i\in I}
belongs to
\underline{\mathbf{Sym}_{n}(C,B)}
since we know that
\left\|{\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}f}\right\|\leq n^{n}
by
Theorem
8.19
.
It follows that
f
is analytic since
f(y)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{n}!}\varphi_{n}(\overline{y}^{{n}})
.

Theorem 8.29
.

The category
\mathbf{ACones}
is cartesian closed.

Proof 8.30
.

We already know that
B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{a}}C
is an integrable cone and we
have an obvious function

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{Ev}}:\underline{{(B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{a}}C)}\mathrel{\&}{B}}={(\underline{B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{a}}C})}\times{\underline{B}}

\displaystyle\to\underline{C}

\displaystyle(f,x)

\displaystyle\mapsto f(x)

which satisfies
\left\|{\operatorname{\mathsf{Ev}}}\right\|\leq 1
, we show that it is measurable.
Let

\theta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,{({B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{a}}C})}\mathrel{\&}{B})}
for some
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
, so that
\theta=\langle{\eta,\beta}\rangle
where
\eta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{a}}C)}
and
\beta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,B)}
, we must prove that
\operatorname{\mathsf{Ev}}\mathrel{\circ}\langle{\eta,\beta}\rangle\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,C)}
so let
m\in\mathcal{M}^{C}_{Y}
for some
Y\in\mathbf{Ar}
, we must prove that the function

\varphi=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in Y\times X\cdot m(s,\eta(r)(\beta(r))):Y\times X\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}

is measurable.
We build
p=(\beta\mathrel{\circ}{\mathsf{pr}_{1}})\mathrel{\triangleright}(m\mathrel{\circ}\mathsf{pr}_{2})\in\mathcal{M}^{B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{a}}C}_{X\times Y}

and since
\eta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(d,B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{a}}C)}
the map

\displaystyle\psi

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(r_{1},s,r_{2})\in X\times Y\times X\cdot p(r_{1},s,\eta(r_{2}))

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(r_{1},s,r_{2})\in X\times Y\times X\cdot m(s,\eta(r_{2})(\beta(r_{1})))

is measurable which shows that
\varphi=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in Y\times X\cdot\psi(r,s,r)

is measurable.

We prove that
\operatorname{\mathsf{Ev}}
is analytic.
We have

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{Ev}}(f,x)

\displaystyle=f(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{n}!}\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}f(\overline{x}^{{n}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{n}!}\varphi_{n}(\overline{(f,x)}^{{n+1}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{(n+1)}!}(n+1)\varphi_{n}(\overline{(f,x)}^{{n+1}})

where
\varphi_{n}:(\underline{{(B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{a}}C)}\mathrel{\&}{B}})^{n+1}\to\underline{C}
is given by

\displaystyle\varphi_{n}((f_{1},x_{1}),\dots,(f_{n+1},x_{n+1}))=\frac{1}{n+1}\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}{f_{i}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{i-1},x_{i+1},\dots,x_{n+1})

and therefore belongs to
\underline{\mathbf{Sym}_{n+1}(B,C)}
; the measurability of
\varphi_{n}
follows from
that of
\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}f
.
If follows that
\operatorname{\mathsf{Ev}}
is analytic, with

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}^{(0)}_{0}\operatorname{\mathsf{Ev}}()

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}^{(n+1)}_{0}\operatorname{\mathsf{Ev}}((f_{1},x_{1}),\dots,(f_{n+1},x_{n+1}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}{f_{i}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{i-1},x_{i+1},\dots,x_{n+1})\,.

Now we deal with the Curry transpose of analytic functions.
So let
D
be an integrable cone and let

f\in\mathbf{ACones}({D}\mathrel{\&}{B},C)
.
Given
z\in\mathcal{B}\underline{D}
let
f_{z}:\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\to\underline{C}
be given by
f_{z}(x)=f(z,x)
.
We know that
f_{z}\in\underline{B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{a}}C}
by Theorem
8.24
applied at

(z,0)\in\mathcal{B}\underline{{D}\mathrel{\&}{B}}
and by precomposing the obtained
“local” analytic function
g:{\left({D}\mathrel{\&}{B}\right)}_{(z,0)}\to C
defined
by
g(w,y)=f(z+w,y)
with the obviously analytic function

x\mapsto(0,x)
: this composition of functions coincides with

f_{z}
.

We are left with proving that the function

g:\mathcal{B}\underline{D}\to\underline{B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{a}}C}
defined by
g(z)=f_{z}
belongs to
\mathbf{ACones}(D,B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{a}}C)
.
It is obvious that
\left\|{g}\right\|\leq 1
so let us check that
g
is
measurable.
Let
\delta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,D)}
, we must prove that
g\mathrel{\circ}\delta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{a}}C)}
so let
Y\in\mathbf{Ar}
and
p\in\mathcal{M}^{B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{a}}C}_{Y}
, we must prove that
the function

\varphi=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in Y\times X\cdot p(s,g(\delta(r))):Y\times X\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}

is measurable.
We have
p=\beta\mathrel{\triangleright}m
where
\beta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,B)}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}^{D}_{Y}
so that

\varphi=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in Y\times X\cdot m(s,g(\delta(r))(\beta(s)))=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in Y\times X\cdot m(s,f(\delta(r),\beta(s)))

is measurable because
f
is measurable and
\boldsymbol{\lambda}(r,s)\in Y\times X\cdot(\delta(r),\beta(s))\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y\times X,{D}\mathrel{\&}{B})}
.
We are left with proving that
g
is analytic.
For
z\in\mathcal{B}\underline{D}
we have

\displaystyle g(z)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\cdot f(z,x)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\cdot\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{n}!}\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}f(\overline{(z,x)}^{{n}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{n}!}\boldsymbol{\lambda}x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\cdot\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}f(\overline{(z,0)+(0,x)}^{{n}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{n}!}\boldsymbol{\lambda}x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\cdot\sum_{k=0}^{n}\genfrac{(}{)}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}f(\overline{(z,0)}^{{k}},\overline{(0,x)}^{{n-k}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{1}{{k}!{(n-k)}!}{\boldsymbol{\lambda}x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\cdot\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}f(\overline{(z,0)}^{{k}},\overline{(0,x)}^{{n-k}})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{k}!}\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{l}!}{\boldsymbol{\lambda}x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\cdot\mathsf{D}^{(k+l)}_{0}f(\overline{(z,0)}^{{k}},\overline{(0,x)}^{{l}})}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{k}!}h_{k}(\overline{z}^{{k}})

where

\displaystyle h_{k}(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{l}!}{\boldsymbol{\lambda}x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\cdot\mathsf{D}^{(k+l)}_{0}f((z_{1},0),\dots,(z_{k},0),\overline{(0,x)}^{{l}})}

is well defined for all
z_{1},\dots,z_{k}\in\underline{D}
.
Indeed, as usual it suffices to take some
\lambda>0
such that

\lambda\geq\left\|{z_{i}}\right\|_{D}
for
i=1,\dots,k
and observe that for
all
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
one has, setting
z=\sum_{i=1}^{k}z_{i}
so that

\frac{1}{k\lambda}z\in\mathcal{B}\underline{D}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{l=0}^{N}\frac{1}{{l}!}{\boldsymbol{\lambda}x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\cdot\mathsf{D}^{(k+l)}_{0}f((z_{1},0),\dots,(z_{k},0),\overline{(0,x)}^{{l}})}

\displaystyle\leq h_{k}(\overline{z}^{{k}})

\displaystyle=(k\lambda)^{k}h_{k}(\frac{1}{k\lambda}\overline{z}^{{k}})

\displaystyle\leq{k}!(k\lambda)^{k}g(\frac{1}{k\lambda}z)\,.

The map
h_{k}
is multilinear by
\omega
-continuity of the algebraic
operations in each cone, it is obviously symmetric by the symmetry of
the
\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}f
.
Its
\omega
-continuity follows from that of the
\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}f
and from
commutations of lubs. Last, measurability and integrability follow as
usual from the monotone convergence theorem.
So we have
h_{k}\in\mathbf{Sym}_{n}(D,B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{a}}C)
and this shows that
g

is analytic.

To prove that
\mathbf{ACones}
is cartesian closed it suffices to prove
that
g
is the unique morphism in
\mathbf{ACones}(D,B\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{a}}C)
such that

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{Ev}}\mathrel{\circ}\left({g}\mathrel{\&}{\operatorname{\mathsf{Id}}_{B}}\right)=f

which results straightforwardly from the fact that
\operatorname{\mathsf{Ev}}
is defined
exactly as in
\mathbf{Set}
.

Theorem 8.31
.

The functor
\operatorname{\mathsf{Der}^{\mathsf{a}}}:\mathbf{ICones}\to\mathbf{ACones}
preserves all limits.

Proof 8.32
.

Preservation of categorical products resulting easily from the
construction of products in
\mathbf{ACones}

(Proposition
8.27
) and in
\mathbf{ICones}

(Theorem
4.26
), let us deal with equalizers.
So let
f,g\in\mathbf{ICones}(B,C)
and let
(E,e)
be their equalizer
in
\mathbf{ICones}
: remember that

\underline{E}=\{x\in\underline{B}\mid f(x)=g(x)\}
and that

e\in\mathbf{ICones}(E,B)
is the obvious injection.
Let
h\in\mathbf{ACones}(D,B)
be such that
f\mathrel{\circ}h=g\mathrel{\circ}h
.
This means that
h(\mathcal{B}\underline{D})\subseteq\mathcal{B}\underline{E}
.
We know that

\displaystyle h(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{n}!}h_{n}(z)

where
h_{n}\in\mathbf{Hpol}_{n}(D,B)
is fully characterized by

\displaystyle\forall x^{\prime}\in\underline{{B}^{\prime}}\quad x^{\prime}(h_{n}(z))={\frac{d^{n}}{dt^{n}}x^{\prime}(h(tz))}\mid_{t=0}\,,

(7)

see the proof of Lemma
8.15
.
We contend that
f\mathrel{\circ}h_{n}=g\mathrel{\circ}h_{n}
so let
z\in\mathcal{B}\underline{D}
and let
p\in\mathcal{M}^{C}_{0}
, we
have

\displaystyle p(f(h_{n}(z)))={\frac{d^{n}}{dt^{n}}p(f(h(tz))))}\mid_{t=0}\,,

by Equation (
7
) applied with
x^{\prime}=p\,f\in\underline{{B}^{\prime}}
and hence
p(f(h_{n}(z)))=p(g(h_{n}(z)))
which proves our contention by

Definition 3.2
.
.
This shows that
h_{n}(\mathcal{B}\underline{D})\subseteq\mathcal{B}\underline{E}
.
Therefore since the operator
\mathsf{L}_{n}
is defined in terms of addition, subtraction and
multiplication by non-negative real numbers we have
\mathsf{L}_{n}{h_{n}}\in\underline{\mathbf{Sym}_{n}(B,E)}
– measurability and
integrability follow from the fact that measurability tests and
integrals in
E
are defined as in
B
.
Finally this shows that
\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{0}h\in\underline{\mathbf{Sym}_{n}(B,E)}
and hence
h\in\mathbf{ACones}(D,E)
which shows that
(E,e)
is the equalizer of

f,g
in
\mathbf{ACones}
.

9.
The linear-non-linear adjunction, in the stable and
analytic cases

From now on we use
\mathcal{C}
to denote one of the two cartesian closed
categories
\mathbf{SCones}
and
\mathbf{ACones}
, which are both locally small.
In both cases we use
\operatorname{\mathsf{Der}}
to denote the functor

\mathbf{ICones}\to\mathcal{C}
(which was denoted by
\operatorname{\mathsf{Der}^{\mathsf{s}}}
when

\mathcal{C}=\mathbf{SCones}
and by
\operatorname{\mathsf{Der}^{\mathsf{a}}}
when
\mathcal{C}=\mathbf{ACones}
).

Remember that
\operatorname{\mathsf{Der}}
preserves all limits, see
Theorems
7.53

and
8.31
.

For that reason the two categories
\mathbf{ICones}
and
\mathcal{C}
can be
related by a linear-non-linear adjunction in the sense
of
[
Mel09
]
, and hence form a categorical model of
Intuitionistic
LL
.
We describe directly the associated Seely category.

Let
\mathsf{E}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{ICones}
be the left adjoint of
\operatorname{\mathsf{Der}}
,
which exists by Theorem
4.32
, and let us
introduce the notation
\Theta_{B,C}:\mathbf{ICones}(\mathsf{E}B,C)\to\mathcal{C}(B,\operatorname{\mathsf{Der}}C)=\mathcal{C}(B,C)
for the associated natural bijection (remember that

\operatorname{\mathsf{Der}}C=C
).

Remark 9.1
.

Just as for the tensor product (see
Remark
5.1
), we have no concrete
description of the
\mathsf{E}
functor for the time being.

We use
(\oc,\mathsf{der},\mathsf{dig})
for the induced comonad on
\mathbf{ICones}
whose
Kleisli category is (equivalent to)
\mathcal{C}
since

\displaystyle{\mathbf{ICones}}_{\oc}(B,C)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{ICones}(\oc B,C)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{ICones}(\mathsf{E}\,\operatorname{\mathsf{Der}}B,C)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathcal{C}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Der}}B,\operatorname{\mathsf{Der}}C)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}(B,C)\,.

Notice that actually
\oc B=\mathsf{E}B
and similarly for
morphisms.
The notation
\operatorname{\mathsf{der}}
for the counit of this comonad comes from the
dereliction rule of
LL
, and the notation
\operatorname{\mathsf{dig}}
for the
comultiplication comes from the
LL
digging derived rule.

Let

\mathsf{nl}_{B}=\Theta_{B,\mathsf{E}B}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Id}}_{\mathsf{E}B})\in\mathcal{C}(B,\oc B)
be the unit of the adjunction,
which is the “universal nonlinear map” on
B

in the sense that for each integrable cone
C
and each
f\in\mathcal{C}(B,C)
one has
f=\varphi\mathrel{\circ}\mathsf{nl}_{B}
for a unique
\varphi\in\mathbf{ICones}(\oc B,C)
,
namely
\varphi=({\Theta_{B,C}})^{-1}(f)
(dropping the
\operatorname{\mathsf{Der}}

symbol since this functor acts as the identity on objects and
morphisms considered as functions).
So that for
h\in\mathbf{ICones}(\oc B,C)
, one has

\Theta_{B,C}(h)=h\mathrel{\circ}\mathsf{nl}_{B}\,.

For each
x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
we set
{x}^{\oc}=\mathsf{nl}_{B}(x)\in\mathcal{B}\underline{\mathsf{E}B}
so that, for
f\in\mathcal{C}(B,C)
we have
f(x)=({\Theta_{B,C}})^{-1}(f)({x}^{\oc})
.

The next lemma is similar to Proposition
5.23
.

Lemma 9.2
.

Let
n\geq 1
, let
B_{1},\dots,B_{n},C
be objects of
\mathbf{ICones}
and
f
and
g
be elements of

\mathbf{ICones}(\oc{B_{1}}\mathop{\otimes}\cdots\mathop{\otimes}\oc{B_{n}},C)
such that

f({x_{1}}^{\oc}\mathop{\otimes}\cdots\mathop{\otimes}{x_{n}}^{\oc})=g({x_{1}}^{\oc}\mathop{\otimes}\cdots\mathop{\otimes}{x_{n}}^{\oc})
for all

(x_{i}\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B_{i}})_{i=1}^{n}
.
Then
f=g
.

Proof 9.3
.

By induction on
n
.
For
n=1
this comes from the fact that

f(x)=(\Theta_{B_{1},C})^{-1}f({x}^{\oc})
for all

x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
so that our assumption entails

f=g
.

For
n>1
, we have

\operatorname{\mathsf{cur}}(f),\operatorname{\mathsf{cur}}(g)\in\mathbf{ICones}(\oc{B_{1}},{\oc{B_{2}}\mathop{\otimes}\cdots\mathop{\otimes}\oc{B_{n}}}\multimap{C})
.
For each
x_{1}\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B_{1}}
, the two functions
\operatorname{\mathsf{cur}}(f)({x_{1}}^{\oc}),\operatorname{\mathsf{cur}}(g)({x_{1}}^{\oc})\in\mathbf{ICones}(\oc{B_{2}}\mathop{\otimes}\cdots\mathop{\otimes}\oc{B_{n}},C)
satisfy

\displaystyle\forall x_{2}\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B_{2}},\dots,x_{n}\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B_{n}}\quad\operatorname{\mathsf{cur}}(f)({x_{1}}^{\oc})({x_{2}}^{\oc}\mathop{\otimes}\cdots\mathop{\otimes}{x_{n}}^{\oc})=\operatorname{\mathsf{cur}}(g)({x_{1}}^{\oc})({x_{2}}^{\oc}\mathop{\otimes}\cdots\mathop{\otimes}{x_{n}}^{\oc})

and hence
\operatorname{\mathsf{cur}}(f)({x_{1}}^{\oc})=\operatorname{\mathsf{cur}}(g)({x_{1}}^{\oc})
by
inductive hypothesis.
As in the base case we get
f=g
.

The counit
\mathsf{der}_{B}\in\mathbf{ICones}(\oc B,B)
of the comonad

\oc\_
is also the counit of the adjunction.
It satisfies therefore

\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\quad\mathsf{der}_{B}({x}^{\oc})=x\,.

The comultiplication
\mathsf{dig}_{B}\in\mathbf{ICones}(\oc B,\oc{\oc B})=\mathbf{ICones}(\mathsf{E}\operatorname{\mathsf{Der}}B,\mathsf{E}\operatorname{\mathsf{Der}}\mathsf{E}\operatorname{\mathsf{Der}}B)
is defined by

\mathsf{dig}_{B}=\mathsf{E}(\mathsf{nl}_{B})

so that we have

\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\quad\mathsf{dig}_{B}({x}^{\oc})={x}^{\oc\oc}\,.

since by naturality of
\mathsf{nl}_{B}
we have
\operatorname{\mathsf{Der}}(\mathsf{E}(\mathsf{nl}_{B}))\mathrel{\circ}\mathsf{nl}_{B}=\mathsf{nl}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{Der}}(\mathsf{E}B)}\mathrel{\circ}\mathsf{nl}_{B}
in
\mathcal{C}
.

Lemma 9.4
.

Let
f\in\mathbf{ICones}(B,C)
and
x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
.
We have
(\oc f)({x}^{\oc})={f(x)}^{\oc}
.

Proof 9.5
.

We have
(\oc f)({x}^{\oc})=(\mathsf{E}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Der}}f))({x}^{\oc})=((\operatorname{\mathsf{Der}}(\mathsf{E}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Der}}f)))\mathrel{\circ}\mathsf{nl}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{Der}}B})(x)

where the composition is taken in
\mathcal{C}
.
By naturality we get
(\oc f)({x}^{\oc})=(\mathsf{nl}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{Der}}C}\mathrel{\circ}\operatorname{\mathsf{Der}}f)(x)={f(x)}^{\oc}
.

Consider the two functors
L,R:{\mathbf{ICones}}^{\mathsf{op}}\times{\mathbf{ICones}}^{\mathsf{op}}\times\mathbf{ICones}\to\mathbf{ICones}
defined on
objects by
L(B,C,D)=(B\Rightarrow({C}\multimap{D}))
and
R(B,C,D)=({C}\multimap{(B\Rightarrow D))}
, and similarly on morphisms.

Lemma 9.6
.

Let
B,C,D
be integrable cones. There is an isomorphism in

\mathbf{ICones}
from
L(B,C,D)=(B\Rightarrow({C}\multimap{D}))
to
R(B,C,D)=({C}\multimap{(B\Rightarrow D))}
which is natural in
B
,
C
and
D
.

Proof 9.7

(Proof sketch)
.

This needs a separate proof in each case
\mathcal{C}=\mathbf{SCones}
and

\mathcal{C}=\mathbf{ACones}
, which follows a pattern that we have seen many
times.
The natural isomorphism maps
f\in\underline{B\Rightarrow({C}\multimap{D})}
to
\boldsymbol{\lambda}y\in\underline{C}\cdot\boldsymbol{\lambda}x\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\cdot f(x,y)
.

Then we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{ICones}(\oc({B_{1}}\mathrel{\&}{B_{2}}),C)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathcal{C}({B_{1}}\mathrel{\&}{B_{2}},C)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathcal{C}(B_{1},B_{2}\Rightarrow C)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathbf{ICones}(\oc{B_{1}},B_{2}\Rightarrow C)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathbf{ICones}(1,{\oc{B_{1}}}\multimap{(B_{2}\Rightarrow C)})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathbf{ICones}(1,B_{2}\Rightarrow\left({\oc{B_{1}}}\multimap{C}\right))\text{ by Lemma~\ref{lemma:stab-lin-swap}}

\displaystyle\simeq\mathcal{C}(\top,B_{2}\Rightarrow\left({\oc{B_{1}}}\multimap{C}\right))

\displaystyle\simeq\mathcal{C}(B_{2},\left({\oc{B_{1}}}\multimap{C}\right))

\displaystyle\simeq\mathbf{ICones}(\oc{B_{2}},\left({\oc{B_{1}}}\multimap{C}\right))

\displaystyle\simeq\mathbf{ICones}({\oc{B_{2}}}\mathop{\otimes}{\oc{B_{1}}},C)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathbf{ICones}({\oc{B_{1}}}\mathop{\otimes}{\oc{B_{2}}},C)

by a sequence of natural bijections and hence by
Lemma
1.1
we have a natural isomorphism

\mathsf{m}^{2}_{B_{1},B_{2}}
in

\mathbf{ICones}({\oc{B_{1}}}\mathop{\otimes}{\oc{B_{2}}},\oc({B_{1}}\mathrel{\&}{B_{2}}))

which satisfies

\displaystyle\mathsf{m}^{2}_{B_{1},B_{2}}({{x_{1}}^{\oc}}\mathop{\otimes}{{x_{2}}^{\oc}})={\langle{x_{1},x_{2}}\rangle}^{\oc}\,.

Notice that this equation fully characterizes
\mathsf{m}^{2}_{B_{1},B_{2}}
by
Lemma
9.2
.

Similarly we define an iso
\mathsf{m}^{0}\in\mathbf{ICones}(1,\oc\top)

which is such that
\mathsf{m}^{0}(t)=t\,{0}^{\oc}
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.
Then one can prove using Lemma
1.1
again that
\oc
is a strong
monoidal comonad.

Theorem 9.8
.

Equipped with the strong monoidal comonad
\oc
, the category
\mathbf{ICones}
is a Seely category in the sense of
[
Mel09
]
.

Proof 9.9
.

Using
Lemma
9.2
it is easy to prove the remaining properties, which regard

\oc\_
and its associated morphisms.
As an example let us prove that the following diagram commutes.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\oc{B_{1}}}\mathop{\otimes}{\oc{B_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oc{({B_{1}}\mathrel{\&}{B_{2}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oc\oc{({B_{1}}\mathrel{\&}{B_{2}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\oc\oc{B_{1}}}\mathop{\otimes}{\oc\oc{B_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oc{({\oc{B_{1}}}\mathrel{\&}{\oc{B_{2}}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{m}^{2}_{B_{1},B_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{dig}_{B_{1}}}\mathop{\otimes}{\mathsf{dig}_{B_{2}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{dig}_{{B_{1}}\mathrel{\&}{B_{2}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oc{\langle{\oc{\mathsf{pr}_{1}},\oc{\mathsf{pr}_{2}}}\rangle}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{m}^{2}_{\oc{B_{1}},\oc{B_{2}}}}

Given
(x_{i}\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B_{i}})_{i=1,2}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{m}^{2}_{\oc{B_{1}},\oc{B_{2}}}(({\mathsf{dig}_{B_{1}}}\mathop{\otimes}{\mathsf{dig}_{B_{2}}})({{x_{1}}^{\oc}}\mathop{\otimes}{{x_{2}}^{\oc}}))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{m}^{2}_{\oc{B_{1}},\oc{B_{2}}}({{x_{1}}^{\oc\oc}}\mathop{\otimes}{{x_{2}}^{\oc\oc}})

\displaystyle={\langle{{x_{1}}^{\oc},{x_{2}}^{\oc}}\rangle}^{\oc}

and

\displaystyle\oc{\langle{\oc{\mathsf{pr}_{1}},\oc{\mathsf{pr}_{2}}}\rangle}(\mathsf{dig}_{{B_{1}}\mathrel{\&}{B_{2}}}(\mathsf{m}^{2}_{B_{1},B_{2}}({{x_{1}}^{\oc}}\mathop{\otimes}{{x_{2}}^{\oc}})))

\displaystyle=\oc{\langle{\oc{\mathsf{pr}_{1}},\oc{\mathsf{pr}_{2}}}\rangle}(\mathsf{dig}_{{B_{1}}\mathrel{\&}{B_{2}}}({\langle{x_{1},x_{2}}\rangle}^{\oc}))

\displaystyle=\oc{\langle{\oc{\mathsf{pr}_{1}},\oc{\mathsf{pr}_{2}}}\rangle}({\langle{x_{1},x_{2}}\rangle}^{\oc\oc})

\displaystyle={(\langle{\oc{\mathsf{pr}_{1}},\oc{\mathsf{pr}_{2}}}\rangle({\langle{x_{1},x_{2}}\rangle}^{\oc}))}^{\oc}

\displaystyle={\langle{(\oc{\mathsf{pr}_{1}})({\langle{x_{1},x_{2}}\rangle}^{\oc}),(\oc{\mathsf{pr}_{2}})({\langle{x_{1},x_{2}}\rangle}^{\oc})}\rangle}^{\oc}

\displaystyle={\langle{{x_{1}}^{\oc},{x_{2}}^{\oc}}\rangle}^{\oc}

Remark 9.10
.

Assume that
\mathcal{C}=\mathbf{SCones}
and that, as in Remark

3.1
and Section
4.1
,

\mathbf{Ar}
is the category whose only objects are
\mathbb{R}
and

0
(the one element measurable space), and all measurable
functions as morphisms.
Then the underlying set of
\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(\mathbb{R})}
is the set of all
finite measures on
\mathbb{R}
.

Consider, as in
Remark
2.7
, the map

\mathsf{cont}:\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(\mathbb{R})}\to\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(\mathbb{R})}
which
extracts the continuous part of each measure on
\mathbb{R}
.
In addition to being linear and
\omega
-continuous, this map is
also measurable (because the map

\mu\mapsto\frac{d\mu}{d\lambda}
is measurable
[
Kal17
, Theorem
1.28]
).
However, as noted in Remark
2.7
, this map does not commute
with integrals and is therefore not a morphism in
\mathbf{ICones}
.

Nevertheless,
\mathsf{cont}
is a morphism in
\mathbf{SCones}
, so
there exists

f\in\mathbf{ICones}(\oc{\mathsf{FMeas}(\mathbb{R})},\allowbreak\mathsf{FMeas}(\mathbb{R}))
such
that
\mathsf{cont}(\mu)=f({\mu}^{\oc})
.
It would be interesting to understand how this function
f
works
to get some insight on the internal structure of the stable
exponential, which is defined in a rather implicit way (by the
special adjoint functor theorem).

9.1.
The coalgebra structure of
\mathsf{FMeas}(X)

In this section we derive additional consequences of the integrability
condition on cones and linear morphisms.
First, we show that for each
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
, the integrable cone

\mathsf{FMeas}(X)
has a structure of
\oc
-coalgebra.
The definition of this coalgebra structure strongly uses the fact that

\oc{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}
is an integrable cone, that is, all measurable
paths valued in that cone have an integral wrt. each subprobability
measure on the measurable space (belonging to
\mathbf{Ar}
) where it is
defined.
This is typically a property which was not available
in
[
EPT18b
]
.

Let
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
.
In Section
6
we defined
the Dirac path
\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}\in\mathcal{B}\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,\mathsf{FMeas}(X))}

which maps
r\in X
to
\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r)
, the Dirac measure at

r
.
Since morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
are measurable we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{nl}_{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}\mathrel{\circ}\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}\in\mathcal{B}\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,\oc{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)})}

and we define

\displaystyle\mathsf{h}_{X}=\mathcal{I}^{\oc{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}}_{X}(\mathsf{nl}_{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}\mathrel{\circ}\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X})\in\mathbf{ICones}(\mathsf{FMeas}(X),\oc{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)})

using Theorem
6.1
.
In other words
\mathsf{h}_{X}
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathsf{h}_{X}(\mu)=\int_{r\in X}{\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r)}^{\oc}\mu(dr)

and satisfies
\mathsf{h}_{X}(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r))={\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r)}^{\oc}
.

Theorem 9.11
.

Equipped with
\mathsf{h}_{X}
, the object
\mathsf{FMeas}(X)
of
\mathbf{ICones}

is a coalgebra of the comonad
\oc\_
.
Moreover for each
\varphi\in\mathbf{Ar}(X,Y)
, we have

\mathsf{FMeas}(\varphi)={\varphi}_{\ast}\in{\mathbf{ICones}}^{\oc}(\mathsf{FMeas}(X),\mathsf{FMeas}(Y))

so that
\mathsf{FMeas}
is a functor
\mathbf{Ar}\to{\mathbf{ICones}}^{\oc}
.

Proof 9.12
.

We must first prove:
\mathsf{der}_{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}\,\mathsf{h}_{X}=\operatorname{\mathsf{Id}}_{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}\in\mathbf{ICones}(\mathsf{FMeas}(X),\mathsf{FMeas}(X))
.
By Theorem
6.3
this results from the fact that for
all
r\in X
one has
(\mathsf{der}_{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}\,\mathsf{h}_{X})(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r))=\mathsf{der}_{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}({\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r)}^{\oc})=\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r)
.

Next we must prove that

f_{1}=f_{2}\in\mathbf{ICones}(\mathsf{FMeas}(X),\oc{\oc(\mathsf{FMeas}(X))})
where

\displaystyle f_{1}=\mathsf{dig}_{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}\,\mathsf{h}_{X}\text{\hskip 10.22217ptand\hskip 10.22217pt}f_{2}=\oc{\mathsf{h}_{X}}\,\mathsf{h}_{X}\,.

Let
r\in X
, we have

\displaystyle f_{1}(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{dig}_{\oc{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}}({\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r)}^{\oc})={\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r)}^{\oc\oc}

\displaystyle f_{2}(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r))

\displaystyle=\oc{\mathsf{h}_{X}}({\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r)}^{\oc})={(\mathsf{h}_{X}(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r)))}^{\oc}

by Lemma
9.4
.
And hence
f_{2}(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r))={\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r)}^{\oc\oc}
so that
f_{1}=f_{2}
by Theorem
6.3
.

Let now
\varphi\in\mathbf{Ar}(X,Y)
, we must prove that
f_{1}=f_{2}
where
f_{1}=\mathsf{h}_{Y}\,{\varphi}_{\ast}
and

f_{2}=\oc({{\varphi}_{\ast}})\,\mathsf{h}_{X}
.
Let
r\in X
, we have
f_{1}(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r))=\mathsf{h}_{Y}(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{Y}(\varphi(r))={\boldsymbol{\delta}^{Y}(\varphi(r))}^{\oc}
and
f_{2}(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r))=\oc{({\varphi}_{\ast})}({\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r)}^{\oc})={({\varphi}_{\ast}(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r)))}^{\oc}={\boldsymbol{\delta}^{Y}(\varphi(r))}^{\oc}
by
Lemma
9.4
and so
f_{1}=f_{2}
by
Theorem
6.3
.
This proves the second part of the theorem.

Remark 9.13
.

One of the main goals in introducing integrable cones was precisely to get
this additional structure for each cone
\mathsf{FMeas}(X)
(notably because
this structure is required in order to interpret call-by-value languages).
It means more specifically that for each
f\in\mathcal{C}(\mathsf{FMeas}(X),B)=\mathbf{ICones}(\oc{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)},B)
we can
define
g=f\,\mathsf{h}_{X}\in\mathbf{ICones}(\mathsf{FMeas}(X),B)
such that

\displaystyle\forall\mu\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}\quad g(\mu)=\int_{r\in X}f(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r))\mu(dr)

which is a “linearization” of
f
allowing to interpret the
sampling operation of probabilistic programming languages: one
samples a
r\in X
(a real number if
X=\mathbb{R}
) according to the
distribution
\mu
and feeds the program
f
with the value

r
represented as the Dirac measure
\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r)
.
This Dirac measure represents, in our semantics, the real number

r
considered as a value.
This observation strongly supports the idea of taking the objects
or
\mathbf{Ar}
(such as the real line, or the set of natural numbers)
as our basic data-types and the measurable functions

\varphi\in\mathbf{Ar}(X,Y)
as the basic functions of our programming
language, through the functor
\mathsf{FMeas}:\mathbf{Ar}\to\mathbf{ICones}
: remember
that

\mathsf{FMeas}(\varphi)(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r))={\varphi}_{\ast}(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r))=\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(\varphi(r))
.

From the viewpoint of
LL
this means that each

X\in\mathbf{Ar}
can be seen as a positive type, that is, a type
equipped with structural rules allowing to erase and duplicate its
values, see for
instance
[
Gir91
,
LR03
,
ET19
]
.
Another way to understand
\mathsf{h}_{X}
is to see it as a

storage operator
in the sense of
[
Kri94
]
, that is,

\mathsf{FMeas}(X)
is a
data-type
.
This idea will be confirmed in Section
9.1.1

where we will see that
\mathsf{FMeas}
is a full and faithful functor
from
\mathbf{Ar}
to
{\mathbf{ICones}}^{\oc}
.

Example 9.14
.

A typical probabilistic programming language that we can interpret
in the model
\mathbf{ICones}
(we assume that
\mathbb{R}\in\mathbf{Ar}
) is the
probabilistic version of PCF presented
in
[
EPT18b
]
(to which we refer for more details
and examples), which features continuous data-types and can be
extended in various ways.
Such a language could feature a type
\rho
of real numbers,
a constant
\mathsf{unif}
such that

{\Gamma}\vdash{\mathsf{unif}}:{\rho}
corresponding to the uniform
probability distribution on the interval
[0,1]
etc.
All the types of this language, which are given by the following
grammar

\displaystyle\sigma,\tau\dots\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\rho\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,{\sigma}\Rightarrow{\tau}\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\cdots

are interpreted as objects of
\mathbf{ICones}
:

\llbracket{\rho}\rrbracket=\mathsf{FMeas}(\mathbb{R})
,

\llbracket{{\sigma}\Rightarrow{\tau}}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\sigma}\rrbracket\Rightarrow\llbracket{\tau}\rrbracket
, etc.
A term
M
such that
{\Gamma}\vdash{M}:{\tau}
where

\Gamma=(x_{1}:\sigma_{1},\dots,x_{k}:\sigma_{k})
is a typing context,
will then be interpreted as a stable and measurable morphism, or as
an analytic morphism

\llbracket{M}\rrbracket_{\Gamma}\in{\mathbf{ICones}}_{\oc}(\llbracket{\sigma_{1}}\rrbracket\mathrel{\&}\cdots\mathrel{\&}\llbracket{\sigma_{k}}\rrbracket,\llbracket{\tau}\rrbracket)
.
So if
{\Gamma,x:\rho}\vdash{M}:{\sigma}
has a free variable of type

\rho
and
{\Gamma}\vdash{N}:{\rho}
, we should consider that
N
represents a
(sub)probability distribution
\llbracket{N}\rrbracket_{\Gamma}
on
\mathbb{R}
and we
may want to sample a real number
r
along this distribution and
feed
M
with the resulting real value that
M
will use

as many times as it wants
: this value will be represented as
the Dirac measure
\boldsymbol{\delta}^{\mathbb{R}}(r)
.
In our language, the corresponding construct is a simple

\mathsf{let}
which allows to deal with the ground type
\rho

in a call-by-value way
23
23

23

This idea was already central
in
[
EPT14
,
EPT18a
,
ET19
]
,
in the discrete setting of probabilistic coherence spaces.
:

\displaystyle{\Gamma}\vdash{\mathsf{let}(x,N,M)}:{\tau}

and the semantics of this construct is

\displaystyle\llbracket{\mathsf{let}(x,N,M)}\rrbracket_{\Gamma}=\int_{r\in\mathbb{R}}^{\llbracket{\tau}\rrbracket}\llbracket{M}\rrbracket_{\Gamma}(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{\mathbb{R}}(r))\llbracket{N}\rrbracket_{\Gamma}(dr)

which is well defined since
\llbracket{M}\rrbracket_{\Gamma}
is stable and
measurable (or analytic), the function

\boldsymbol{\lambda}r\in\mathbb{R}\cdot\boldsymbol{\delta}^{\mathbb{R}}(r)
belongs to

\mathcal{B}\underline{\mathsf{Path}(\mathbb{R},\mathsf{FMeas}(\mathbb{R}))}
and the cone

\llbracket{\tau}\rrbracket
is integrable.
The constant
\mathsf{unif}
is interpreted as the probability
measure on
\mathbb{R}
which maps a measurable set
U
to the
Lebesgue measure of
U\cap[0,1]
.
For each
r\in\mathbb{R}
, the language has a constant
\underline{r}
of
type
\rho
and

\llbracket{\underline{r}}\rrbracket_{\Gamma}=\boldsymbol{\delta}^{\mathbb{R}}(r)\in\mathcal{B}\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(\mathbb{R})}
(in
each context
\Gamma
).
Our language will also have constructs
\mathsf{log}(M)
,

\mathsf{sqrt}(M)
etc. corresponding to the usual functions
which are all measurable, and typed for instance by

{\Gamma}\vdash{M}:{\rho}
{\Gamma}\vdash{\mathsf{log}(M)}:{\rho}

with semantics given by push-forward:

\llbracket{\mathsf{log}(M)}\rrbracket_{\Gamma}={\log}_{\ast}(\llbracket{M}\rrbracket_{\Gamma})
.
So for instance we can define a closed term
N
such that

{}\vdash{N}:{\rho}
by

\displaystyle N=\mathsf{let}(x,\mathsf{unif},\mathsf{let}(y,\mathsf{unif},\mathsf{mult}(\mathsf{sqrt}(\mathsf{mult}(\underline{-2.},\mathsf{log}(x))),\mathsf{cos}(\mathsf{mult}(\underline{6.28\cdots},y)))))

and then
\llbracket{N}\rrbracket
is the normal distribution
\mathcal{N}(0,1)

defined by the Box Muller method, and we can define a term
N^{\prime}

with two free variable
x
and
s
for
\mathcal{N}(x,s)
as

N^{\prime}=\mathsf{let}(y,N,\mathsf{plus}(\mathsf{mult}(s,y),x)))

such that
{x:\rho,s:\rho}\vdash{N^{\prime}}:{\rho}
.
Then
