\llbracket{{N^{\prime}}[{\underline{4.2}/x,\underline{0.7}/s}]}\rrbracket=\llbracket{N^{\prime}}\rrbracket_{x:\rho,s:\rho}(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{\mathbb{R}}(4.2),\boldsymbol{\delta}^{\mathbb{R}}(0.7))\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(\mathbb{R})}

is the measure
\mathcal{N}(4.2,0.7)
.

9.1.1.
The category of measurable functions as a full subcategory
of the Eilenberg Moore category

So we have extended the operation
\mathsf{FMeas}
on the measurable spaces
of
\mathbf{Ar}
into a functor
\mathbf{Ar}\to{\mathbf{ICones}}^{\oc}
which acts on
morphisms by push-forward.
This functor is clearly faithful, we prove that, under very reasonable
assumptions about
\mathbf{Ar}
, it is also full, which is
quite a remarkable fact: the Eilenberg-Moore category of
\oc

contains
\mathbf{Ar}
as a full subcategory.
Again, integration is an essential ingredient in the proof of this
result.

A Polish space is a complete metric space which has a countable dense
subset.

We will need two lemmas which are folklore in measure theory.

Lemma 9.15
.

Let
X
be a Polish space, equipped with its standard Borel

\sigma
-algebra
\sigma_{X}
.
Let
\mu
be a probability measure on
X
and assume that

\forall U\in\sigma_{X}\ \mu(U)\in\{0,1\}
.
Then
\mu
is a Dirac measure.

Proof 9.16
.

Given
r\in X
and
\varepsilon\geq 0
we use

B(r,\varepsilon)\subseteq X
for the closed ball of radius

\varepsilon
centered at
r
.
Let
D
be a countable dense subset of
X
.
Let
F\subseteq X
be closed and such that
\mu(F)=1
and let

\varepsilon>0
, we have
F\subseteq\bigcup_{r\in D\cap F}B(r,\varepsilon)
and hence
1=\mu(F)\leq\sum_{r\in D\cap F}\mu(B(r,\varepsilon))
and hence
\exists r\in D\cap F\ \mu(B(r,\varepsilon))=1
.
We define a sequence
(r_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of elements of
D
such
that
\forall n\in{\mathbb{N}}\ \mu(B(r_{n},2^{-n}))=1
as follows.
We obtain
r_{0}
by applying the property above with
F=X
and

\varepsilon=1
.
We get
r_{n+1}
by applying the property above with

F=B(r_{n},2^{-n})
and
\varepsilon=2^{-(n+1)}
.
Then the sequence
(r_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is Cauchy and has therefore a
limit
r
and we have
\{r\}=\bigcap_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}B(r_{n},2^{-n})
so
that
\mu(\{r\})=\inf_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\mu(B(r_{n},2^{-n}))=1
since

\mu
is a measure.
It follows that
\mu(U)=0
for each measurable
U
such that

r\notin U
since we must have
\mu(\{r\}\cup U)=1
and hence

\mu=\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r)
.

Lemma 9.17
.

If
X
and
Y
are measurable spaces such that the

\sigma
-algebra of
Y
contains all singletons (this is true in
particular if
Y
is a Polish space), and if
\kappa
is a
kernel from
X
to
Y
such that for all
r\in X
the
measure
\kappa(r)
is a Dirac measure on
Y
, then there is a
uniquely defined measurable function
\varphi:X\to Y
such that

\kappa=\boldsymbol{\delta}^{Y}\mathrel{\circ}\varphi
.

This is obvious.

Theorem 9.18
.

Let
X,Y\in\mathbf{Ar}
be such that
Y
is a Polish space
and let
f
be a morphism from
\mathsf{FMeas}(X)
to
\mathsf{FMeas}(Y)
in

\mathbf{ICones}
.
Then
f
is a coalgebra morphism from
(\mathsf{FMeas}(X),\mathsf{h}_{X})
to

(\mathsf{FMeas}(Y),\mathsf{h}_{Y})
iff there is a
\varphi\in\mathbf{Ar}(X,Y)

such that
f=\mathsf{FMeas}(\varphi)={\varphi}_{\ast}
.

As a consequence, if we assume that
X
is a Polish space
for all
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
, then
\mathbf{Ar}
is a full subcategory of
the Eilenberg Moore category of the comonad
\oc\_
through the

\mathsf{FMeas}
functor.

Most measurable spaces which appear in probability theory are Polish
spaces: discrete spaces, the real line, countable products and
measurable subspaces of Polish spaces (and hence the Cantor Space and
the Baire Space, the Hilbert Cube
etc.
) are Polish spaces.
So the restriction to Polish spaces is not a serious one.

Proof 9.19
.

Saying that
f
is a coalgebra morphism means that the following
diagram commutes in
\mathbf{ICones}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{FMeas}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oc{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oc{\mathsf{FMeas}(Y)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{h}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{h}_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oc f}

which, by Theorem
6.3
, is equivalent to

\displaystyle{(f(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r)))}^{\oc}=\oc f(\mathsf{h}_{X}(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r))=\mathsf{h}_{Y}(f(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r))=\int_{s\in Y}^{\oc{\mathsf{FMeas}(Y)}}{\boldsymbol{\delta}^{Y}(s)}^{\oc}f(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r))(ds)

(8)

for all
r\in X
, and this equation trivially holds if

f={\varphi}_{\ast}
.
Assume conversely that
f
satisfies
(
8
).
Let
V
be a measurable subset of
Y
and let

g\in\mathcal{C}(\mathsf{FMeas}(Y),1)
be defined by
g(\nu)=\nu(V)^{2}

and let

g_{0}=({\Theta_{\mathsf{FMeas}(Y),1}})^{-1}(g)\in\mathbf{ICones}(\oc{\mathsf{FMeas}(Y)},1)

which is characterized by

\forall\nu\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(Y)}\ g(\nu)=g_{0}({\nu}^{\oc})
.
We have
g_{0}({f(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r))}^{\oc})=g(f(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r)))=f(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r))(V)^{2}
and, since
g_{0}
preserves integrals,

\displaystyle f(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r))(V)^{2}

\displaystyle=g_{0}\Big(\int^{\oc{\mathsf{FMeas}(Y)}}_{s\in Y}{\boldsymbol{\delta}^{Y}(s)}^{\oc}f(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r))(ds)\Big)\text{\hskip 10.22217ptby Equation~(\ref{eq:meas-coalg-morph-charact})}

\displaystyle=\int_{s\in Y}g_{0}({\boldsymbol{\delta}^{Y}(s)}^{\oc})f(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r))(ds)

\displaystyle=\int_{s\in Y}{\boldsymbol{\delta}^{Y}(s)}(V)^{2}f(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r))(ds)

\displaystyle=\int_{s\in Y}{\boldsymbol{\delta}^{Y}(s)}(V)f(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r))(ds)

\displaystyle=f(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r))(V)

so we have
f(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r))(V)\in\{0,1\}
for all

V\in\sigma_{Y}
.
Let
g\in\mathcal{C}(\mathsf{FMeas}(Y),1)
be defined now by
g(\nu)=1

and let

g_{0}=({\Theta_{\mathsf{FMeas}(Y),1}})^{-1}(g)\in\mathbf{ICones}(\oc{\mathsf{FMeas}(Y)},1)
,
we have
g_{0}({f(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r))}^{\oc})=g(f(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r)))=1
and, since

g_{0}
preserves integrals,

\displaystyle 1=g_{0}\Big(\int^{\oc{\mathsf{FMeas}(Y)}}_{s\in Y}{\boldsymbol{\delta}^{Y}(s)}^{\oc}f(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r))(ds)\Big)

\displaystyle=\int_{s\in Y}g_{0}({\boldsymbol{\delta}^{Y}(s)}^{\oc})f(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r))(ds)

\displaystyle=\int_{s\in Y}f(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r))(ds)

\displaystyle=f(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r))(Y)

and hence the measure
f(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{X}(r))
is a Dirac measure by
Lemma
9.15
and it follows that

f=\mathsf{FMeas}(\varphi)={\varphi}_{\ast}
for a uniquely determined

\varphi\in\mathbf{Ar}(X,Y)
by Lemma
9.17
.

9.2.
Fixpoint operators in the cartesian closed category

Remember that
\mathcal{C}
is a CCC having the following property:

The objects of
\mathcal{C}
are integrable cones.
In particular, for each object
B
of
\mathcal{C}
, the set

\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
has a structure of
\omega
-cpo by the condition

2.1.
Basic definitions
, with
0
as least element.
And each
f\in\mathcal{C}(B,B)
is in particular an increasing and

\omega
-continuous function

\mathcal{B}\underline{B}\to\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
and therefore has a least
fixpoint which is
\sup_{n=0}^{\infty}f^{n}(0)\in\mathcal{B}\underline{B}
.

It is completely standard to apply this property to the map
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathcal{C}({({B}\Rightarrow{B})}\Rightarrow{B},{({B}\Rightarrow{B})}\Rightarrow{B})
given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(F)(f)=f(F(f))

which is well-defined and belongs to

\mathcal{C}({({B}\Rightarrow{B})}\Rightarrow{B},{({B}\Rightarrow{B})}\Rightarrow{B})
by cartesian
closedness of
\mathcal{C}
.
The least fixpoint
\mathcal{Y}
of
\mathcal{Z}
is an element of

\mathcal{C}({B}\Rightarrow{B},B)
which is easily seen to satisfy

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}(f)=\sup_{n=0}^{\infty}f^{n}(0)

and is therefore a least fixpoint operator that we have proven here to
be a morphism in
\mathcal{C}
, that is, a stable and measurable or an
analytic map depending on the considered category
\mathcal{C}
. This
morphism
\mathcal{Y}
is the key ingredient to interpret recursively
defined functional programs in the CCC
\mathcal{C}
.

Example 9.20
.

The function
f:[0,1]\to[0,1]
given by

f(x)=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{4}x^{2}
belongs to
\mathcal{C}(1,1)
so it
has a least fixpoint
x\in[0,1]
which must satisfy

x^{2}-4x+2=0
and is therefore
2-{\sqrt{2}}
.
Of course, when
a\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and
a>0
, the function

x\mapsto x+a
from
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
to
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
has no fixpoint, but
this is not a contradiction because it does not restrict to a
function
[0,1]\to[0,1]
.

10.
Probabilistic coherence spaces as integrable cones

So far we have seen several ways of building integrable cones: as
spaces of measures, or of paths, as products and tensor products, as
spaces of analytic maps
etc.

As announced in Example
2.4
we describe
here another source of integrable cones: the probabilistic coherence
spaces.
Intuitively, they form a model of
LL
based on

discrete
but not necessarily finite probabilities.
So their definition does not require measure theory.

We use
\overline{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}
for the completed real half-line, that is

\overline{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}=\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
, considered as a semi-ring with
multiplication satisfying
0\,\infty=0
, which is the only possible
choice since we want multiplication to be
\omega
-continuous.

Let
I
be a set. If
i\in I
we use
\mathsf{e}(i)
for the element
of
(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{I}
such that
\mathsf{e}(i)_{j}=\delta_{{i},{j}}
.

If
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\overline{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}^{I}
we define

\mathcal{P}^{\mathord{\perp}}\subseteq\overline{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}^{I}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\mathord{\perp}}=\{x^{\prime}\in(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{I}\mid\forall x\in\mathcal{P}\ \sum_{i\in I}x_{i}x^{\prime}_{i}\leq 1\}

and we use the notation
\langle x,x^{\prime}\rangle=\sum_{i\in I}x_{i}x^{\prime}_{i}
.
As usual we have
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{Q}\Rightarrow\mathcal{Q}^{\mathord{\perp}}\Rightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\mathord{\perp}}

and
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{P}^{\bot\bot}
, and as a consequence

\mathcal{P}^{\mathord{\perp}}={\mathcal{P}}^{\bot\bot\bot}
.
In other words, it is equivalent to say that
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}^{\bot\bot}
or to
say that
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{Q}^{\mathord{\perp}}
for some
\mathcal{Q}
.

Theorem 10.1
.

Let
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\overline{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}^{I}
, one has
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}^{\bot\bot}
iff the
following conditions hold

•

\mathcal{P}
is convex (that is, if
x,y\in\mathcal{P}
and

\lambda\in[0,1]
then
\lambda x+(1-\lambda)y\in\mathcal{P}
)

•

\mathcal{P}
is down-closed for the product order

•

and, for each sequence
(x(n))_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of element of

\mathcal{P}
which is increasing for the pointwise order, the pointwise
lub
\in\overline{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}^{I}
of this sequence belongs to
\mathcal{P}
.

A proof is outlined in
[
Gir04
]
and a complete proof can be
found in
[
Ehr22
]
.

Definition 10.2
.

A probabilistic coherence space (PCS) is a pair
\mathcal{X}=(|{\mathcal{X}}|,\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{X}})
where
|{\mathcal{X}}|
is a set which is at most
countable
24
24

24

This countability assumption is crucial in the present
setting, again because of our use of the monotone convergence
theorem.
and
\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{X}}\subseteq(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{|{\mathcal{X}}|}
satisfies

•

\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{X}}=\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{X}}^{\bot\bot}

•

for all
a\in|{\mathcal{X}}|
there is
x\in\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{X}}
such that

x_{a}>0

•

and for all
a\in|{\mathcal{X}}|
the set

\{x_{a}\mid x\in\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{X}}\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is bounded.

The 2nd and 3rd conditions are required to keep the coefficients
finite and are dual of each other.

Given two sets
I,J
, a vector
u\in\overline{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}^{I}
and a matrix

w\in\overline{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}^{I\times J}
, we define
{w}\cdot{u}\in\overline{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}^{J}
by

\displaystyle{w}\cdot{u}=\Big(\sum_{i\in I}w_{i,j}u_{i}\Big)_{j\in J}

and then, given
v\in\overline{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}^{J}
, observe that

\displaystyle\langle{w}\cdot{u},v\rangle=\langle w,{u}\mathrel{\overline{\mathord{\otimes}}}{v}\rangle=\sum_{i\in I,j\in J}w_{i,j}u_{i}v_{j}\in\overline{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}

where
{u}\mathrel{\overline{\mathord{\otimes}}}{v}\in\overline{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}^{I\times J}
is defined by

({u}\mathrel{\overline{\mathord{\otimes}}}{v})_{i,j}=u_{i}v_{j}
(we use this notation here to avoid
confusions with the tensor operations we have introduced for integrable
cones).

Given
w_{1}\in\overline{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}^{I_{1}\times I_{2}}
and

w_{2}\in\overline{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}^{I_{2}\times I_{3}}
, one defines

w_{2}\,w_{1}\in\overline{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}^{I_{1}\times I_{3}}
(product of matrices
written in reversed order) by

\displaystyle(w_{2}\,w_{1})_{i_{1},i_{3}}=\sum_{i_{2}\in I_{2}}(w_{1})_{i_{1},i_{2}}(w_{2})_{i_{2},i_{3}}\,.

It is easily checked that, given PCSs
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
, one defines a
PCS
{\mathcal{X}}\multimap{\mathcal{Y}}
by
|{{\mathcal{X}}\multimap{\mathcal{Y}}}|=|{\mathcal{X}}|\times|{\mathcal{Y}}|
and

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}{({\mathcal{X}}\multimap{\mathcal{Y}})}=\{t\in(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{|{{\mathcal{X}}\multimap{\mathcal{Y}}}|}\mid\forall x\in\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{X}}\ {t}\cdot{x}\in\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{Y}}\}\,.

Indeed one can check that

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}{({\mathcal{X}}\multimap{\mathcal{Y}})}=\{{x}\mathrel{\overline{\mathord{\otimes}}}{y^{\prime}}\mid x\in\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{X}}\text{ and }y^{\prime}\in\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{X}}^{\mathord{\perp}}\}^{\mathord{\perp}}\,.

Then given
s\in\mathsf{P}{({\mathcal{X}}\multimap{\mathcal{Y}})}
and
t\in\mathsf{P}{({\mathcal{Y}}\multimap{\mathcal{Z}})}
one has

\displaystyle t\,s\in\mathsf{P}{\left({\mathcal{X}}\multimap{\mathcal{Z}}\right)}

and the diagonal matrix

\operatorname{\mathsf{Id}}=(\delta_{{a},{a^{\prime}}})_{(a,a^{\prime})\in|{{\mathcal{X}}\multimap{\mathcal{X}}}|}
belongs to

\mathsf{P}{({\mathcal{X}}\multimap{\mathcal{X}})}
.
This defines the category
\mathbf{Pcoh}
of probabilistic coherence spaces.

Let
t\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
. We use

\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{fun}}}(t):\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{X}}\to\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{Y}}
for the function defined by
\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{fun}}}(t)(x)={t}\cdot{x}
.

The orthogonal (or linear negation)
\mathcal{X}^{\mathord{\perp}}
of a PCS
\mathcal{X}
is
defined by
|{\mathcal{X}^{\mathord{\perp}}}|=|{\mathcal{X}}|
and

\mathsf{P}{(\mathcal{X}^{\mathord{\perp}})}=(\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{X}})^{\mathord{\perp}}
so that
\mathcal{X}^{\bot\bot}=\mathcal{X}
. We use

{\mathord{\perp}}
for the PCS such that
|{{\mathord{\perp}}}|=\{\ast\}
and

\mathsf{P}{{\mathord{\perp}}}=[0,1]
.
Setting
1={\mathord{\perp}}^{\mathord{\perp}}
we have obviously
1={\mathord{\perp}}
and

\mathcal{X}^{\mathord{\perp}}
is trivially isomorphic to
{\mathcal{X}}\multimap{{\mathord{\perp}}}
.
Under this iso, the function
\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{fun}}}(x^{\prime}):\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{X}}\to[0,1]

associated with
x^{\prime}\in\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{X}^{\mathord{\perp}}}
is given by

\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{fun}}}(x^{\prime})(x)=\langle x,x^{\prime}\rangle
.

This linear negation is a functor
{\mathbf{Pcoh}}^{\mathsf{op}}\to\mathbf{Pcoh}
, mapping

t\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
to it transpose
t^{\mathord{\perp}}
defined by

(t^{\mathord{\perp}})_{b,a}=t_{a,b}
.

Each PCS
\mathcal{X}
induces a measurable cone
\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X})
defined by
\underline{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X})}=\{\lambda x\mid x\in\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{X}}\text{ and
}\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\}
with algebraic operations defined in the obvious
pointwise way.
Notice that if
t\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
we can extend
\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{fun}}}(t)
to a
function
\underline{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X})}\to\underline{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{Y})}
by setting

\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{fun}}}(t)(x)=\lambda^{-1}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{fun}}}(t)(\lambda x)
for each
\lambda>1

such that
\lambda x\in\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{X}}
(the function does not depend on the
choice of
\lambda
).

The norm of this cone is defined by

\displaystyle\left\|{x}\right\|_{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X})}=\sup_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}{\underline{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X})}}^{\prime}}\langle x,x^{\prime}\rangle=\inf\{\lambda>0\mid x\in\lambda\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{X}}\}

so that
\mathcal{B}\underline{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X})}=\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{X}}
.
The measurability structure of
\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X})
is given by

\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X})}_{0}=\{\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{fun}}}(x^{\prime})\mid x^{\prime}\in\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{X}^{\mathord{\perp}}}\}

and
\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X})}_{X}
is the set of all constant functions from

X\in\mathbf{Ar}
to
\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X})}_{0}
.

Lemma 10.3
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a PCS and let
\mathcal{P}\subseteq(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{|{\mathcal{X}}|}
be such
that
\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{X}}=\mathcal{P}^{\mathord{\perp}}
.
Then

\displaystyle\left\|{x}\right\|_{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X})}=\sup_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}}\langle x,x^{\prime}\rangle\,.

The proof is easy.
Notice that for each
a\in|{\mathcal{X}}|
one has

\mathsf{e}(a)\in\underline{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X})}
by the second condition in the
definition of a PCS, and that
\left\|{\mathsf{e}(a)}\right\|
is not necessarily
equal to
1
.

Lemma 10.4
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a PCS and
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
.
A function
\beta:X\to\underline{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X})}
is a measurable path of the
measurable cone
\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X})
iff
\beta(X)
is bounded and, for all
a\in|{\mathcal{X}}|
, the
function
\boldsymbol{\lambda}r\in X\cdot\beta(r)_{a}:X\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is
measurable.

Proof 10.5
.

The
\Rightarrow
direction results from the observation that, for
each
a\in|{\mathcal{X}}|
there is a
\lambda>0
such that

\lambda\mathsf{e}(a)\in\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{X}^{\mathord{\perp}}}
.
For the
\Leftarrow
direction let
\beta:X\to\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{X}}

be such that the function
\boldsymbol{\lambda}r\in X\cdot\beta(r)_{a}
is
measurable for all
a\in|{\mathcal{X}}|
.
Let
x^{\prime}\in\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{X}^{\mathord{\perp}}}
, we must prove that

\varphi=\boldsymbol{\lambda}r\in X\cdot\langle\beta(r),x^{\prime}\rangle
is measurable.
Since
|{\mathcal{X}}|
is countable, this results from the monotone
convergence theorem and from the fact that

\displaystyle\varphi(r)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in|{\mathcal{X}}|}\beta(r)_{a}x^{\prime}_{a}\,.

Theorem 10.6
.

For each PCS
\mathcal{X}
the measurable cone
\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X})
is integrable.

Proof 10.7
.

Let
\beta:X\to\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{X}}
be a measurable path (by
Lemma
10.4
this is equivalent to saying
that
\beta_{a}=\boldsymbol{\lambda}r\in X\cdot\beta(r)_{a}
is measurable

X\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
for all
a\in|{\mathcal{X}}|
since

\forall r\in X\ \left\|{\beta(r)}\right\|\leq 1
).
Let
\mu\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(X)}
.
We define
x\in(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{|{\mathcal{X}}|}
by

\displaystyle x_{a}=\int\beta_{a}(r)\mu(dr)

which is a well defined element of
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
since the function

\beta_{a}
is bounded by definition of a PCS.
Let
x^{\prime}\in\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{X}^{\mathord{\perp}}}
, we have, applying the monotone
convergence theorem,

\displaystyle\langle x,x^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in|{\mathcal{X}}|}\Big(\int\beta_{a}(r)\mu(dr)\Big)x^{\prime}_{a}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in|{\mathcal{X}}|}\int\left(\beta_{a}(r))x^{\prime}_{a}\right)\mu(dr)

\displaystyle=\int\langle\beta(r),x^{\prime}\rangle\mu(dr)\leq\left\|{\mu}\right\|

so if
\lambda>0
is such that
\lambda\left\|{\mu}\right\|\leq 1
we get
\langle\lambda x,x^{\prime}\rangle\leq 1
for all
x^{\prime}\in\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{X}^{\mathord{\perp}}}
so
that
x\in\underline{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X})}
.
The equation
\langle x,x^{\prime}\rangle=\int\langle\beta(r),x^{\prime}\rangle\mu(dr)
which
holds for all
x^{\prime}\in\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{X}^{\mathord{\perp}}}
shows that
x
is the
integral of
\beta
over
\mu
by definition of

\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X})}
.

Theorem 10.8
.

If
t\in\mathsf{P}{\left({\mathcal{X}}\multimap{\mathcal{Y}}\right)}
then

\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{fun}}}(t)\in\mathbf{ICones}(\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X}),\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{Y}))
and extended to morphisms
in that way, the operation
\mathsf{ic}
is a full and faithful
functor
\mathbf{Pcoh}\to\mathbf{ICones}
.

Proof 10.9
.

The fact that
\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{fun}}}(t)
is linear and continuous is easy (the
proof can be found in
[
DE11
]
for
instance).
Measurability and integral preservation of
\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{fun}}}(t)
boil down
again to the monotone convergence theorem. Faithfulness results from
the fact that
t
is completely determined by the action of

\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{fun}}}(t)
on the elements
\mathsf{e}(a)
of
\underline{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X})}
(for
all
a\in|{\mathcal{X}}|
; remember that indeed

\forall a\in|{\mathcal{X}}|\ \mathsf{e}(a)\in\underline{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X})}
). Last let

f\in\mathbf{ICones}(\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X}),\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{Y}))
.
We define
t\in(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{|{{\mathcal{X}}\multimap{\mathcal{Y}}}|}
by

t_{a,b}=f(\mathsf{e}(a))_{b}
.
Given
x\in\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{X}}
and
y^{\prime}\in\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{Y}^{\mathord{\perp}}}
we have

\displaystyle\langle{t}\cdot{x},y^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in|{\mathcal{X}}|,b\in|{\mathcal{Y}}|}t_{a,b}x_{a}y^{\prime}_{b}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in|{\mathcal{X}}|,b\in|{\mathcal{Y}}|}f(\mathsf{e}(a))_{b}x_{a}y^{\prime}_{b}

\displaystyle=\sum_{b\in|{\mathcal{Y}}|}f(x)_{b}y^{\prime}_{b}\text{\hskip 10.22217ptby linearity and continuity of }f

\displaystyle=\langle f(x),y^{\prime}\rangle\leq 1

since
\left\|{f}\right\|\leq 1
, which shows that
{t}\cdot{x}\in\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{Y}}

and hence
t\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
.
The equation
\langle{t}\cdot{x},y^{\prime}\rangle=\langle f(x),y^{\prime}\rangle
for all

y^{\prime}\in\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{Y}^{\mathord{\perp}}}
also shows that
{t}\cdot{x}=f(x)
and
hence the functor
\mathsf{ic}
is full.

We use
{\mathcal{X}}\mathrel{\overline{\mathord{\otimes}}}{\mathcal{Y}}
for the tensor product operation in
\mathbf{Pcoh}
,
that is
|{{\mathcal{X}}\mathrel{\overline{\mathord{\otimes}}}{\mathcal{Y}}}|=|{\mathcal{X}}|\times|{\mathcal{Y}}|
and

\mathsf{P}{({\mathcal{X}}\mathrel{\overline{\mathord{\otimes}}}{\mathcal{Y}})}=\{{x}\mathrel{\overline{\mathord{\otimes}}}{y}\mid x\in\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{X}}\text{ and
}y\in\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{Y}}\}^{\bot\bot}=\left({\mathcal{X}}\multimap{\mathcal{Y}^{\mathord{\perp}}}\right)^{\mathord{\perp}}
.

Theorem 10.10
.

If
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}
are PCSs then
\operatorname{\mathsf{fun}}
is an iso from the integrable
cone
\mathsf{ic}({\mathcal{X}}\multimap{\mathcal{Y}})
to the integrable cone

{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X})}\multimap{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{Y})}
in
\mathbf{ICones}
, and this iso is
natural in
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
.

Proof 10.11

(Proof sketch)
.

We know by Theorem
10.8
that
\operatorname{\mathsf{fun}}

is an iso of cones.
We need to prove that
\operatorname{\mathsf{fun}}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{fun}}^{-1}
are measurable
and that
\operatorname{\mathsf{fun}}
preserve integrals (then
\operatorname{\mathsf{fun}}^{-1}
also
preserves integrals by injectivity of
\operatorname{\mathsf{fun}}
).

Let
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
and

\eta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,\mathsf{ic}({\mathcal{X}}\multimap{\mathcal{Y}}))}
, we show that

\operatorname{\mathsf{fun}}\mathrel{\circ}\eta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X})}\multimap{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{Y})})}

so let
Y\in\mathbf{Ar}
,
\beta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(Y,\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X}))}
and

m\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{Y})}_{Y}
meaning that
m=\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{fun}}}(y^{\prime})
for some

y^{\prime}\in\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{Y}^{\mathord{\perp}}}
, we have, for all
(s,r)\in Y\times X
,

\displaystyle(\beta\mathrel{\triangleright}m)(s,\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{fun}}}(\eta(r)))

\displaystyle=\langle\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{fun}}}(\eta(r))(\beta(s)),y^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle{\eta(r)}\cdot{\beta(s)},y^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(a,b)\in|{\mathcal{X}}|\times|{\mathcal{Y}}|}\eta(r)_{a,b}\beta(s)_{a}y^{\prime}_{b}

and the function

\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in Y\times X\cdot(\beta\mathrel{\triangleright}m)(s,\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{fun}}}(\eta(r)))
is
measurable as a countable sum of measurable functions.
Conversely let now

\eta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X})}\multimap{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{Y})})}
, we must
prove that
\operatorname{\mathsf{fun}}^{-1}\mathrel{\circ}\eta\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,\mathsf{ic}({\mathcal{X}}\multimap{\mathcal{Y}}))}
so
let
Y\in\mathbf{Ar}
and
p\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{ic}({\mathcal{X}}\multimap{\mathcal{Y}})}_{Y}
, that is
p=\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{fun}}}(z)
for some

z\in\mathsf{P}{\left({\mathcal{X}}\multimap{\mathcal{Y}}\right)^{\mathord{\perp}}}=\mathsf{P}{({\mathcal{X}}\mathrel{\overline{\mathord{\otimes}}}{\mathcal{Y}^{\mathord{\perp}}})}
, we
have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in Y\times X\cdot p(s,\operatorname{\mathsf{fun}}^{-1}(\eta(r)))

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in Y\times X\cdot\langle z,\operatorname{\mathsf{fun}}^{-1}(\eta(r))\rangle

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}(s,r)\in Y\times X\cdot\sum_{(a,b)\in|{\mathcal{X}}|\times|{\mathcal{Y}}|}z_{a,b}\eta(r)(\mathsf{e}(a))_{b}

which is measurable as a countable sum of measurable functions since
we know that for all
a,b
the function
\boldsymbol{\lambda}r\in X\cdot\eta(\mathsf{e}(a))_{b}
is measurable by our
assumption that
\eta
is a measurable path.

The fact that
\operatorname{\mathsf{fun}}
preserves integrals results from the
pointwise definition of integration in

{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X})}\multimap{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{Y})}
.

Theorem 10.12
.

There is a natural isomorphism
\varphi_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}}\in\mathbf{ICones}({\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X})}\mathop{\otimes}{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{Y})},\mathsf{ic}({\mathcal{X}}\mathrel{\overline{\mathord{\otimes}}}{\mathcal{Y}}))
.

Proof 10.13

(Proof sketch)
.

The map
\boldsymbol{\lambda}(x,y)\in\underline{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X})}\times\underline{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{Y})}\cdot{x}\mathrel{\overline{\mathord{\otimes}}}{y}

is easily seen to be bilinear,
\omega
-continuous, measurable and
separately integrable so that we have an associated
\varphi_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}}\in\mathbf{ICones}({\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X})}\mathop{\otimes}{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{Y})},\mathsf{ic}({\mathcal{X}}\mathrel{\overline{\mathord{\otimes}}}{\mathcal{Y}}))

characterized by
\varphi_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}}({x}\mathop{\otimes}{y})={x}\mathrel{\overline{\mathord{\otimes}}}{y}
.
We define now

\psi_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}}:\mathsf{ic}({\mathcal{X}}\mathrel{\overline{\mathord{\otimes}}}{\mathcal{Y}})\to{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X})}\mathop{\otimes}{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{Y})}
.
First, given
(a,b)\in|{{\mathcal{X}}\mathrel{\overline{\mathord{\otimes}}}{\mathcal{Y}}}|
we set

\psi_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}}(\mathsf{e}(a,b))={\mathsf{e}(a)}\mathop{\otimes}{\mathsf{e}(b)}
.
Next given
z\in\underline{\mathsf{ic}({\mathcal{X}}\mathrel{\overline{\mathord{\otimes}}}{\mathcal{Y}})}
such that

\operatorname{\mathsf{supp}}({z})=\{(a,b)\in|{{\mathcal{X}}\mathrel{\overline{\mathord{\otimes}}}{\mathcal{Y}}}|\mid z_{(a,b)}\not=0\}
is
finite we set

\psi_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}}(z)=\sum_{(a,b)\in|{{\mathcal{X}}\mathrel{\overline{\mathord{\otimes}}}{\mathcal{Y}}}|}z_{(a,b)}{\mathsf{e}(a)}\mathop{\otimes}{\mathsf{e}(b)}
which is a well defined
finite sum in the cone
\underline{{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X})}\mathop{\otimes}{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{Y})}}
.
We contend that

\displaystyle\left\|{\psi_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}}(z)}\right\|_{{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X})}\mathop{\otimes}{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{Y})}}\leq\left\|{z}\right\|_{\mathsf{ic}({\mathcal{X}}\mathrel{\overline{\mathord{\otimes}}}{\mathcal{Y}})}

so let
\varepsilon>0
and assume without loss of generality that

\left\|{z}\right\|\leq 1
.
By Proposition
3.12
there is

g\in\mathcal{B}\underline{{{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X})}\mathop{\otimes}{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{Y})}}\multimap{{\mathord{\perp}}}}

such that

\left\|{\psi_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}}(z)}\right\|_{{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X})}\mathop{\otimes}{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{Y})}}\leq g(\psi_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}}(z))+\varepsilon
.
Let
h\in\mathcal{B}\underline{\left({\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X})}\multimap{\left({\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{Y})}\multimap{{\mathord{\perp}}}\right)}\right)}
be the the bilinear morphism associated to
g
by the iso of
Theorem
5.19
.
We have

\displaystyle g(\psi_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}}(z))

\displaystyle=\sum_{(a,b)\in|{{\mathcal{X}}\mathop{\otimes}{\mathcal{Y}}}|}z_{(a,b)}g({\mathsf{e}(a)}\mathop{\otimes}{\mathsf{e}(b)})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(a,b)\in|{{\mathcal{X}}\mathop{\otimes}{\mathcal{Y}}}|}z_{(a,b)}h(\mathsf{e}(a),\mathsf{e}(b))\leq 1

because

(h(\mathsf{e}(a),\mathsf{e}(b)))_{(a,b)\in|{{\mathcal{X}}\mathop{\otimes}{\mathcal{Y}}}|}\in\mathsf{P}{({\mathcal{X}}\mathrel{\overline{\mathord{\otimes}}}{\mathcal{Y}})^{\mathord{\perp}}}
by Theorem
10.10
and by our
assumption that
\left\|{z}\right\|\leq 1
.
So we have

\left\|{\psi_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}}(z)}\right\|_{{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X})}\mathop{\otimes}{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{Y})}}\leq 1+\varepsilon

and since this holds for all
\varepsilon>0
our contention is
proven. Now let
z
be any element of

\underline{\mathsf{ic}({\mathcal{X}}\mathrel{\overline{\mathord{\otimes}}}{\mathcal{Y}})}
and assume again that

\left\|{z}\right\|\leq 1
. Let
(I_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be an increasing sequence
of finite sets such that
\bigcup I_{n}=|{\mathcal{X}}|\times|{\mathcal{Y}}|
and let

z(n)\in\underline{\mathsf{ic}({\mathcal{X}}\mathrel{\overline{\mathord{\otimes}}}{\mathcal{Y}})}
be defined by

\displaystyle z(n)_{(a,b)}=\begin{cases}z_{(a,b)}&\text{if }(a,b)\in I_{n}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

so that the sequence
(z(n))_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is increasing and has
z

as lub in
\underline{\mathsf{ic}({\mathcal{X}}\mathrel{\overline{\mathord{\otimes}}}{\mathcal{Y}})}
.
The sequence
(\psi_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}}(z(n)))_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is increasing and all
its elements have norm
\leq 1
since each
z(n)
has finite
support and norm
\leq 1
and hence it has a lub in

\underline{{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X})}\mathop{\otimes}{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{Y})}}
.
It is easy to check that this lub does not depend on the choice of
the
I_{n}
’s, so we can set

\psi_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}}(z)=\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\psi_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}}(z(n))
so that actually

\displaystyle\psi_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}}(z)=\sum_{(a,b)\in|{\mathcal{X}}|\times|{\mathcal{Y}}|}z_{a,b}{\mathsf{e}(a)}\mathop{\otimes}{\mathsf{e}(b)}\,.

The proof that

\psi_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}}\in\mathbf{ICones}(\mathsf{ic}(({\mathcal{X}}\mathrel{\overline{\mathord{\otimes}}}{\mathcal{Y}})),{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X})}\mathop{\otimes}{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{Y})})
follows the standard pattern and it is obvious that it is
the inverse of
\varphi_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}}
.

10.1.
More constructions

We outline very briefly the additive and exponential constructions on
PCSs.
The categorical product
\mathcal{X}=\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}\mathcal{X}_{i}
of a family

(\mathcal{X}_{i})_{i\in I}
of PCSs can be described by
|{\mathcal{X}}|=\bigcup_{i\in I}\{i\}\times|{\mathcal{X}_{i}}|
and

x\in(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{|{\mathcal{X}}|}
belongs to
\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{X}}
if

\forall i\in I\ {\mathsf{pr}_{i}}\cdot{x}\in\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{X}_{i}}
where
\mathsf{pr}_{i}\in(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{|{\mathcal{X}}|\times|{\mathcal{X}_{i}}|}
is given by
(\mathsf{pr}_{i})_{(j,a),b}=\delta_{{i},{j}}\delta_{{a},{b}}
, so that

\mathsf{pr}_{i}\in\mathsf{P}{(}\mathcal{X},\mathcal{X}_{i})
for each
i\in I
.
Then it is easy to check that
(\mathcal{X},(\mathsf{pr}_{i})_{i\in I})
is the
categorical product of the family
(\mathcal{X}_{i})_{i\in I}
in
\mathbf{Pcoh}
and
that there is a natural isomorphism from

\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X}_{i})}
to
\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X})
.

For the coproduct
\mathcal{Y}=\mathop{\oplus}_{i\in I}\mathcal{X}_{i}
we can take
\mathcal{Y}=(\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}\mathcal{X}_{i}^{\mathord{\perp}})^{\mathord{\perp}}
so that
|{\mathcal{Y}}|=|{\mathcal{X}}|
, and
\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{Y}}=\{x\in\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{X}}\mid\sum_{i\in I}\left\|{{\mathsf{pr}_{i}}\cdot{x}}\right\|\leq 1\}
, equipped with injections

\mathsf{in}_{i}=\mathsf{pr}_{i}^{\mathord{\perp}}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(\mathcal{X}_{i},\mathcal{Y})
.
So for instance the coproduct
{1}\oplus{1}
has
\{0,1\}
as
web, and
\mathsf{P}{({1}\oplus{1})}=\{u\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}\mid u_{0}+u_{1}\leq 1\}
.

For the exponential, we introduce the following notations.
Given
u\in(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{I}
we define
u^{!}\in(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({I})}
by

u^{!}_{m}=u^{m}=\prod_{i\in I}u_{i}^{m(i)}
.
Given a PCS
\mathcal{X}
we define a PCS
\oc{\mathcal{X}}
by
|{\oc{\mathcal{X}}}|=\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({|{\mathcal{X}}|})
and
\mathsf{P}{(\oc{\mathcal{X}})}=\{x^{!}\mid x\in\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{X}}\}^{\bot\bot}
so that
t\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(\oc{\mathcal{X}},\mathcal{Y})
means exactly that
t\in(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({|{\mathcal{X}}|})\times|{\mathcal{Y}}|}
and

\displaystyle\widehat{t}(x)=\Big(\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({|{\mathcal{X}}|})}t_{m,b}x^{m}\Big)_{b\in|{\mathcal{Y}}|}\in\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{Y}}

from which it is not hard to derive that the integrable cones
\mathsf{ic}({\oc{\mathcal{X}}}\multimap{\mathcal{Y}})
and
\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X})\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{a}}\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{Y})
are isomorphic.

Remark 10.14
.

There is a morphism
\varphi_{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathbf{ICones}(\oc^{\mathsf{a}}{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{X})},\mathsf{ic}(\oc{\mathcal{X}}))
for
all PCSs
\mathcal{X}
such that
\varphi_{\mathcal{X}}(x^{\mathord{\oc}_{\mathsf{a}}})=x^{!}
for all

x\in\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{X}}
. This morphism is similar to the one of
Theorem
10.12
, we conjecture that it is an iso.

10.2.
Example: the Cantor Space as an equalizer of
\mathbf{Pcoh}
morphisms

Since
\mathbf{ICones}
is a complete category, the equalizer of two
parallel
\mathbf{Pcoh}
morphisms is an integrable cone.
As we shall see now, this cone needs not be a PCS which means that,
contrarily to the larger category
\mathbf{ICones}
, the category
\mathbf{Pcoh}

is not complete.
This example also shows that interesting “non discrete” cones arise
as limits of diagrams in
\mathbf{Pcoh}
.

Consider the PCS
\mathcal{S}
such that
|{\mathcal{S}}|=\{0,1\}^{<\omega}

is the set of finite sequences of
0
’s and
1
’s and where

x\in(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{|{\mathcal{S}}|}
belongs to
\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{S}}
if, for each

u\subseteq|{\mathcal{S}}|
which is an antichain (meaning that if

s,s^{\prime}\in u
then
s\leq s^{\prime}\Rightarrow s=s^{\prime}
where
\leq
is the
prefix order), one has
\sum_{s\in u}x_{s}\leq 1
.
Since
\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{S}}=A^{\mathord{\perp}}
where
A
is the set of all characteristic
functions of antichains,
\mathcal{S}
is a PCS (the second and third
conditions of Definition
10.2
result from the observation
that each singleton is an antichain).
Notice that

\displaystyle\left\|{x}\right\|_{\mathcal{S}}=\sup_{x^{\prime}\in A}\langle x,x^{\prime}\rangle

(9)

by Lemma
10.3
.

The PCS
\mathcal{S}
is the “least solution” (in the sense explained
in
[
DE11
,
ET19
]
) of the equation

\mathcal{S}={1}\mathrel{\&}{({\mathcal{S}}\oplus{\mathcal{S}})}
.

There is a morphism
\theta\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{S})
which is given by

\displaystyle\theta_{s,t}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }s=ta\text{ for some }a\in\{0,1\}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

where we use simple juxtaposition for concatenation.
Indeed given an antichain
u
and
x\in\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{S}}
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{t\in u}({\theta}\cdot{x})_{t}=\sum_{s\in v}x_{s}\leq 1

where
v=\{sa\mid s\in u\text{ and }a\in\{0,1\}\}
is an
antichain since
u
is an antichain. Let
C
be the integrable
cone which is the equalizer of
\theta
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{Id}}_{S}
, considered as
morphisms of
\mathbf{ICones}
through the full and faithful functor

\mathsf{ic}
.

Theorem 10.15
.

The integrable cone
C
is isomorphic to
\mathsf{FMeas}(\mathcal{C})
where

\mathcal{C}
is the Cantor Space equipped with the Borel sets of its
usual topology (the product topology of
\{0,1\}^{\omega}
where

\{0,1\}
has the discrete topology).

Proof 10.16
.

We have

\displaystyle\underline{C}=\{x\in\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{S})\mid{\theta}\cdot{x}=x\}\,,

that is, an element of
\underline{C}
is an
x\in(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{|{\mathcal{S}}|}
such that
x\in\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{S}}
and

\displaystyle\forall s\in|{\mathcal{S}}|\quad x_{s}=x_{s0}+x_{s1}\,.

Given
s\in|{\mathcal{S}}|
we set

\mathord{\uparrow}s=\{\alpha\in\mathcal{C}\mid s<\alpha\}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
,
which is a clopen of
\mathcal{C}
.
Let
U
be an open subset of
\mathcal{C}
, the set

\mathord{\downarrow}U
of all
s\in|{\mathcal{S}}|
which are minimal (for
the prefix order) such that
\mathord{\uparrow}s\subseteq U
is an
antichain, and we have

\displaystyle U=\bigcup\{\mathord{\uparrow}s\mid s\in\mathord{\downarrow}U\}

(10)

by definition of the topology of
\mathcal{C}
.
Given
x\in\underline{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{S})}
we define a function

\mathsf{meas}(x):\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
on the open sets of

\mathcal{C}
by

\displaystyle\mathsf{meas}(x)(U)=\sum_{s\in\mathord{\downarrow}U}x_{s}

and we have
\mathsf{meas}(x)(U)\leq\left\|{x}\right\|
by our assumption that

x\in\underline{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{S})}
.
This function
\mathsf{meas}(x)
is additive (that is

\mathsf{meas}(x)(\bigcup_{i\in I}U_{i})=\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{meas}(x)(U_{i})
for each countable family

(U_{i})_{i\in I}
of pairwise disjoint open subsets of

\mathcal{C}
).
And so
\mathsf{meas}(x)
extends to a uniquely defined finite
measure on the Borel sets of the Cantor Space, that is to an element
of
\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(\mathcal{C})}
.
Notice that
\mathsf{meas}:\underline{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{S})}\to\mathsf{FMeas}(\mathcal{C})
is
linear and satisfies

\left\|{\mathsf{meas}(x)}\right\|=x_{\langle{}\rangle}\leq\left\|{x}\right\|
where

\langle{}\rangle\in|{\mathcal{S}}|
is the empty sequence.

Let
\mu\in\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(\mathcal{C})}
, we define

\mathsf{rep}(\mu)\in(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{|{\mathcal{S}}|}
by

\mathsf{rep}(\mu)_{s}=\mu(\mathord{\uparrow}s)
. Given an antichain

u\subseteq|{\mathcal{S}}|
notice that the clopens

(\mathord{\uparrow}s)_{s\in u}
are pairwise disjoint and that

U=\bigcup_{s\in u}\mathord{\uparrow}s
is open and hence measurable, so,
since
\mu
is a measure, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{s\in u}\mathsf{rep}(\mu)_{s}=\sum_{s\in u}\mu(\mathord{\uparrow}s)=\mu\big(\bigcup_{s\in u}\mathord{\uparrow}s\big)=\mu(U)\leq\mu(\mathcal{C})\,.

Since this holds for each antichain
u
we have shown that

\mathsf{rep}(\mu)\in\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{S}}
.
Notice that for each
s\in|{S}|
we have

\mathord{\uparrow}s=\mathord{\uparrow}s0\cup\mathord{\uparrow}s1
and that this union
is disjoint, so that

\mu(\mathord{\uparrow}s)=\mu(\mathord{\uparrow}s0)+\mu(\mathord{\uparrow}s1)
since

\mu
is a measure, that is

\mathsf{rep}(\mu)\in\underline{C}
.
Notice also that the function
\mathsf{rep}
is linear and
satisfies
\mathsf{rep}(\mu)\leq\left\|{\mu}\right\|
since for each antichain

u\subseteq|{\mathcal{S}}|
one has

\sum_{s\in u}\mathsf{rep}(\mu)_{s}=\sum_{s\in u}\mu(\mathord{\uparrow}s)=\mu(\bigcup_{s\in u}\mathord{\uparrow}s)\leq\mu(\mathcal{C})=\left\|{\mu}\right\|
.

We prove that the functions
\mathsf{meas}
and
\mathsf{rep}
are
inverse of each other. Let first
x\in\underline{C}
, we have, for all

s\in|{\mathcal{S}}|
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{rep}(\mathsf{meas}(x))_{s}=\mathsf{meas}(x)(\mathord{\uparrow}s)=x_{s}

since
\mathord{\downarrow}\mathord{\uparrow}s=\{s\}
. Let now

\mu\in\mathsf{FMeas}(\mathcal{C})
and let
U\in\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{C})
we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{meas}(\mathsf{rep}(\mu))(U)=\sum_{s\in\mathord{\downarrow}U}\mathsf{rep}(\mu)_{s}=\sum_{s\in\mathord{\downarrow}U}\mu(\mathord{\uparrow}s)=\mu(U)

by Formula (
10
). It follows that

\mathsf{meas}(\mathsf{rep}(\mu))=\mu
.

It follows that
\mathsf{meas}
and
\mathsf{rep}
define an order
isomorphism between
\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(\mathcal{C})}
and
\underline{C}
and
therefore are
\omega
-continuous (this uses also the fact that

\left\|{\mathsf{meas}(x)}\right\|=\left\|{x}\right\|
since

\left\|{x}\right\|=\left\|{\mathsf{rep}(\mathsf{meas}(x))}\right\|\leq\left\|{\mathsf{meas}(x)}\right\|

and similarly
\left\|{\mathsf{rep}(\mu)}\right\|=\left\|{\mu}\right\|
).

The fact that the map

\mathsf{meas}:\underline{\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{S})}\to\underline{\mathsf{FMeas}(\mathcal{C})}
is linear
is measurable and integrable results as usual from the monotone
convergence theorem.
So we have
\mathsf{meas}\in\mathbf{ICones}(\mathsf{ic}(\mathcal{S}),\mathsf{FMeas}(\mathcal{C}))
and
hence by restriction

\mathsf{meas}\in\mathbf{ICones}(\underline{C},\mathsf{FMeas}(\mathcal{C}))
since

\left\|{\mathsf{meas}(x)}\right\|=\mathsf{meas}(x)(\mathcal{C})\leq\left\|{x}\right\|
for all

x\in\underline{C}
.
Again, checking that
\mathsf{rep}
is
measurable and integrable is routine; as an example let us prove the
last property so let
X\in\mathbf{Ar}
and let

\kappa\in\underline{\mathsf{Path}(X,\mathsf{FMeas}(\mathcal{C}))}
.
Let
m\in\mathcal{M}^{C}_{0}
, that is
m=\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{fun}}}(x^{\prime})
for some

x^{\prime}\in\mathsf{P}{\mathcal{S}^{\mathord{\perp}}}
.
We have

\displaystyle m\Big(\int^{C}\mathsf{rep}(\kappa(r))\mu(dr)\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\in|{\mathcal{S}}|}x^{\prime}_{s}\Big(\int^{\mathsf{ic}(S)}\mathsf{rep}(\kappa(r))\mu(dr)\Big)_{s}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\in|{\mathcal{S}}|}x^{\prime}_{s}\int\mathsf{rep}(\kappa(r))_{s}\mu(dr)

\displaystyle=\int\big(\sum_{s\in|{\mathcal{S}}|}x^{\prime}_{s}\kappa(r)(\mathord{\uparrow}s)\big)\mu(dr)

\displaystyle=\int m(\mathsf{rep}(\kappa(r)))\mu(dr)\,.

By Formula (
9
) we have

\left\|{\mathsf{rep}}\right\|\leq 1
and hence

\mathsf{rep}\in\mathbf{ICones}(\mathsf{FMeas}(\mathcal{C}),C)
.

Conclusion

Elaborating on earlier work by the first author (together with
Michele Pagani and Christine Tasson) on a denotational semantics based
on measurable cones and by the second author on a notion of
convex QBS where integration is the fundamental algebraic
operation
[
Geo21
]
,
we have developed a theory of integration for measurable cones,
introducing the category of
integrable cones
and of linear
morphisms preserving integrals.
We have shown that this category is a model of Intuitionistic
LL

featuring two exponential comonads; for defining the tensor
product and the exponentials we have used the special adjoint functor
theorem which avoids providing explicit combinatorial constructions of
these objects.

The construction is parameterized by a small full subcategory

\mathbf{Ar}
of the category of measurable spaces and measurable
functions.
The model obtained in that way has many pleasant properties.

•

It contains the category of probabilistic coherence
spaces as a full subcategory.

•

It contains the category whose objects are those of
\mathbf{Ar}

and whose morphisms are the substochastic kernels as a full
subcategory.

•

For both exponentials, the associated Eilenberg Moore category
contains
\mathbf{Ar}
as a full subcategory, if we assume that all the
objects of
\mathbf{Ar}
are standard Borel spaces which btw. is a very
natural and harmless requirement.

The two latter properties strongly rely on the fact that the morphisms
of the underlying linear category preserve integrals.
The last one means that
\mathbf{Ar}
can be considered as a category of
basic data-types (the objects of
\mathbf{Ar}
) and basic operations on
them (the morphisms of
\mathbf{Ar}
).

In future work we will explain how this model can be used for
interpreting call-by-value or even call-by-push-value probabilistic
functional programming languages with continuous data-types
(interpreted as the aforementioned coalgebras) as well as recursive
types.
